. By
choosing
\delta>0
small enough, we can make sure that
1-6\delta^{1/2}<\theta
and the density of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is at least
1-\epsilon
. Then,
by

Proposition 10.6.1
we get

{\mathbf{w}}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(x_{0}^{\prime})
. Then,
{\mathbf{w}}\in\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(x_{0})
.

Thus, for all
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x_{1}]
sufficiently close to

U^{+}[x_{1}]
so that (
11.1.17
) holds,
we have

\mathbf{j}(\mathcal{U}_{x_{0}}^{-1}(\mathcal{Q}))\in\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(x_{0})
, which
implies
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x_{0}]
. Note that for fixed
x_{0}
,

x_{1}
,
\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x_{0}]
and
\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x_{1}]
are both analytic subsets
of
\mathcal{C}[x_{0}]=\mathcal{C}[x_{1}]
. Thus, we get,

\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x_{1}]\subset\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x_{0}]
. Since
x_{0},x_{1}\in\Psi
we
have
\dim\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x_{1}]=\dim\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x_{0}]
.
This implies that

\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x_{0}]=\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x_{1}]
. This is equivalent to
I(x_{0},x_{1})\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x_{0})=\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x_{1})
.
∎

11.1.25.
Proof of the equivalence relation property in
Proposition 11.1.6

We assume
Lemma 11.1.7
.
Note that the map
\mathbf{j}:\mathcal{C}(x)\to\mathbf{H}(x)
takes the identity coset of
\mathcal{C}(x)
to
0
. We have
0\in\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(x)
,
therefore,

(11.1.26)

U^{+}(x)\in\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x).

Thus
x\sim_{ij}x
.

Suppose
x^{\prime}\sim_{ij}x
. Then,
x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]
, and so
x\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x^{\prime}]
.
By (
Eqn. (11.1.26)
),
U^{+}(x)\in\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x)
, and by
Lemma
Lemma 11.1.7
,
\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x^{\prime})=I(x,x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x)
. Therefore,
I(x,x^{\prime})(U^{+}(x))\in\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x^{\prime})
, and thus
x\sim_{ij}x^{\prime}
.

Now suppose
x^{\prime}\sim_{ij}x
and
x^{\prime\prime}\sim_{ij}x^{\prime}
. Then,
x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]
. Also,
I(x^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime})(U^{+}(x^{\prime\prime}))\in\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x^{\prime})=I(x,x^{\prime})\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x)
.
Since
I(x,x^{\prime})^{-1}I(x^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime})=I(x^{\prime\prime},x)
, we have
I(x^{\prime\prime},x)(U^{+}(x^{\prime\prime}))\in\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x)
. Therefore
x^{\prime\prime}\sim_{ij}x
.
∎

11.1.27.
The measurability property

For a.e.
x\in X
, the set
\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x)\subset\mathcal{C}(x)
is an algebraic subset, and furthermore its
\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(x)
-orbits form an algebraic space
\mathcal{Y}(x)
.
We can therefore form a measurable bundle
\mathcal{Y}\to X
, defined
\nu
-a.e., parametrizing these sets, with the property that
I(y,x)\mathcal{E}_{ij}(y)\in\mathcal{Y}(x)
.

Let
X_{\mathfrak{B}_{0}}
denote the space of atoms of the measurable partition
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
, which is by construction a standard measurable space and there is a measurable quotient map
\psi\colon X\to X_{\mathfrak{B}_{0}}
.
By a standard measurable selection theorem
there exists a measurable section
\sigma_{0}\colon X_{\mathfrak{B}_{0}}\to X
.
Let
\mathcal{Y}_{0}:=\sigma_{0}^{*}\mathcal{Y}
be the pulled back bundle, which is a measurable bundle over
X_{\mathfrak{B}_{0}}
.
The collection of maps
I(x,y)
, measurable in
(x,y)\in X\times X
restricted to the subset with
x\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[y]
, defines a measurable map

\psi_{ij}\colon X\to\mathcal{Y}_{0}\text{ via }\psi_{ij}(y):=I(y,\sigma_{0}(\psi(y)))\mathcal{E}_{ij}(y)\in\mathcal{Y}(\sigma_{0}(\psi(y)))\cong\mathcal{Y}_{0}(\psi(y)).

The atoms of the equivalence relation
\sim_{ij}
are, by construction, the fibers of the measurable map
\psi_{ij}
.
Indeed by
Lemma 11.1.7
, we have

x\sim_{ij}y\qquad\text{if and only if $y\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]$ and }\mathcal{E}_{ij}(y)=I(x,y)\mathcal{E}_{ij}(x).

Together with the cocycle property of
I(x,y)
, this establishes the equality of fibers of
\psi_{ij}
and equivalence classes of
\sim_{ij}
, and thus the measurability of
\sim_{ij}
.

11.1.28.
The equivalence classes
\Psi_{ij}[x]

For a full measure subset of
X
, denote the atoms of the equivalence class by

\Psi_{ij}[x]=\{x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]\colon x^{\prime}\sim_{ij}x\}.

For correspondence of notation, note that the above sets in

[
EM18
, Lemma 11.3]

are denoted by
\mathcal{C}_{ij}[x]
.

Let
\Psi_{ij}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra
of
\nu
-measurable sets which are unions of the equivalence classes

\Psi_{ij}[x]
.
By
§11.1.27
, the atoms of
\Psi_{ij}
are the fibers of the measurable maps
\psi_{ij}
, hence
\Psi_{ij}
is a countably generated
\sigma
-algebra.

11.1.29 Lemma
.

There exists an invariant set of full measure
X_{0}\subset X
such that, if
x\in X_{0}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and
u\in U^{+}(x)
then we have:

(a)

\left(g_{t}\Psi_{ij}[x]\right)\cap\mathfrak{B}_{0}[g_{t}x]\cap g_{t}\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]=\Psi_{ij}[g_{t}x]\cap\mathfrak{B}_{0}[g_{t}x]\cap g_{t}\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]
.

(b)

\left(u\Psi_{ij}[x]\right)\cap\mathfrak{B}_{0}[ux]\cap u\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]=\Psi_{ij}[ux]\cap\mathfrak{B}_{0}[ux]\cap u\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]
.

Proof.

Note that the sets
U^{+}[x]
and
\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(x)
are

g_{t}
-equivariant. Therefore, so are the
\mathcal{E}_{ij}[x]
, which implies
(a). Part (b) is also clear since, in view of

Proposition 10.1.5
and the Ledrappier invariance principle
Corollary B.2.7
,

u_{*}(x,ux)\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(x)=\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(ux)
.
∎

11.1.30 Definition

(The measures
f_{ij}[x]
)
.

We now define
f_{ij}[x]

to be the conditional measure
of
\nu
along the
\Psi_{ij}[x]
. In other words,
f_{ij}[x]

is defined so that for any measurable
\phi:X\to\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E}(\phi\mid\Psi_{ij})(x)=\int_{X}\phi\,df_{ij}[x].

We view
f_{ij}[x]
as a measure on
\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]

which is supported on
\Psi_{ij}[x]
.

11.1.31 Definition

(The measures
\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]
)
.

By
Lemma 11.1.29
, for almost every
x
and all
t\geq 0
we have

\Psi_{ij}[x]\subset g_{t}\Psi_{ij}[g_{-t}x].

We define a locally finite Radon measure

\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]

on
\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
as follows: we declare
\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x](\Psi_{ij}[x])=1
and for any compact
K\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
, we take
t>0
so that
g_{-t}K\subset\mathfrak{B}_{0}[g_{-t}x]
and declare

\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x](K):=\frac{(g_{t})_{*}f_{ij}[g_{-t}x](K)}{(g_{t})_{*}f_{ij}[g_{-t}x](\Psi_{ij}[x])}.

The
g_{t}
-invariance of the measure
\nu
ensures that
\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x](K)
is well-defined independent of the choice of

t>0
making
g_{-t}K\subset\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x].

11.1.32.
The measures
\overline{f}_{ij}[x]

Let
\pi_{U^{+}}\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]\to\mathcal{C}[x]
denote the map
x\mapsto U^{+}[x]
.
We would like to define a measure
\overline{f}_{ij}[x]
on

\mathcal{C}[x]
by
\overline{f}_{ij}[x]=(\pi_{U^{+}})_{\ast}\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]
but
this does not make sense since for
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{C}[x]

the
\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]
-measure of
\pi_{U^{+}}^{-1}(\mathcal{V})
is typically
infinite. Instead we proceed as follows. Fix a compact
K_{U}\subset U^{+}[x]
of positive Haar measure.
Suppose
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{C}[x]

is a compact set. For a.e.
c\in\mathcal{V}
choose
g_{c}\in\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(x)
such
that
g_{c}U^{+}[x]=c
and the map
c\mapsto g_{c}
is measurable.
(If
g_{c}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(x)
is another such
choice, then
g_{c}^{\prime}=g_{c}u_{c}
for some
u_{c}\in U^{+}(x)
).
Then, let
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
be
\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal{V}}g_{c}K_{U}
. Since the conditionals of
\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]
along the
sets
U^{+}[g_{c}x]
are Haar under the action of
g_{c}U^{+}(x)g_{c}^{-1}
,

\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x](\mathcal{V}^{\prime})
does not depend on the choice of
g_{c}
. Thus, we may
define the measure
\overline{f}_{ij}[x]
on
\mathcal{C}[x]

by
\overline{f}_{ij}[x](\mathcal{V})=\widetilde{f}_{ij}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})
.
It is easy to see that
up to normalization,
\overline{f}_{ij}[x]
does not depend on the
choice of
K_{U}
.

We normalize
\overline{f}_{ij}[x]
so that
\overline{f}_{ij}[x](\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{C}}[x])=1
, where

(11.1.33)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{C}}[x]:=\{U^{+}[y]\;\>:\;\>y\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]\}

11.1.34.
Recovering
\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]
from
\bar{f}_{ij}[x]

Let us now explain how to compute
\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x](E)
for a (bounded) measurable set
E\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
, given
\bar{f}_{ij}[x]
.
Recall that we have a measurable function
g(c)\in\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(x)
over
c\in\mathcal{C}[x]
such that
c=g(c)U^{+}[x]
and let
\nu_{U^{+}[x]}
denote a fixed Haar measure on
U^{+}[x]
, so that
g(c)_{*}\nu_{U^{+}[x]}
is a Haar measure on
c
.
Then

\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x](E):=\fint_{\mathcal{C}[x]}g(c)_{*}\nu_{U^{+}[x]}\left(E\right)d\bar{f}_{ij}(c)

where
\fint
denotes the integral normalized by the scalar obtained by using
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]
instead of
E
in the domain of integration.

11.1.35.
The measures
\mathbf{f}_{ij}(x)

Recall the maps
\mathcal{U}_{x}\colon\mathcal{C}(x)\to\mathcal{C}[x]

and
\mathbf{j}\colon\mathcal{C}(x)\to\mathbf{H}(x)
.
We let
\mathbf{f}_{ij}(x)=(\mathbf{j}\circ\mathcal{U}_{x}^{-1})_{*}\overline{f}_{ij}[x].

Again,
\mathbf{f}_{ij}(x)
is a locally finite Radon measure on
\mathbf{H}(x).

We note that the support of each
\mathbf{f}_{ij}(x)
is contained in
\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(x).

We record the following elementary properties that follow from the constructions:

11.1.36 Lemma
.

For
x,y
in a set of full measure:

(i)

If
y\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]
then we have

I(x,y)_{*}\overline{f}_{ij}[x]\propto\overline{f}_{ij}[y].

(ii)

If
y\in\mathcal{F}_{ij}[x]
then we have

\mathbf{f}_{ij}(y)\propto\mathbf{R}(x,y)_{*}\mathbf{f}_{ij}(x).

Moreover, if
y\in G[x]
then we have

\mathbf{f}_{ij}(y)\propto\mathbf{R}(x,y)_{*}\mathbf{f}_{ij}(x).

Here
\propto
means that the two measures agree up to a scalar factor.

11.1.37 Definition

(The “distance”
d_{*}(\cdot,\cdot)
)
.

We consider the set of Radon measures on a manifold, with the vague
topology (so
\mu_{n}\to\mu
if and only if for all continuously
compactly supported
f
,
\mu_{n}(f)\to\mu(f)
). This topology is
metrizable, and let
\hat{d}^{x}_{*}(\cdot,\cdot)
be a metric inducing this
topology on
\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
.
Then, for measures
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
on
\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
, let

d_{*}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=\hat{d}^{x}_{*}\left(\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{1}(\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x])},\frac{\nu_{2}}{\nu_{2}(\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x])}\right).

For measures
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
on
\mathbf{H}(x)
, define

d_{*}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=\hat{d}^{x}_{*}\left(\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{1}(D(x))},\frac{\nu_{2}}{\nu_{2}(D(x))}\right),

where
D(x)=\mathbf{j}(\mathcal{U}_{x}^{-1}(\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{C}}[x]))
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{C}}[x]
is defined by
Eqn. (11.1.33)
.

11.2.
Proposition 11.4 from EM

11.2.1.
Top-balanced
Y
-configurations

11.2.2 Definition

(top-balanced
Y
-configuration)
.

Fix
C>0
. Using the notation §
6.1.5
, we
say a
Y
-configuration
Y
is
(C,ij)
-top-balanced
if
q_{3}=g^{ij}_{t_{3}}(q_{1})
and
q_{2}=g^{ij}_{t_{2}}(uq_{1})
where

|t_{2}-t_{3}|\leq C.

We denote by
Y_{ij,tb}(q_{1},u,\ell,\tau)
the unique

(0,ij)
-top-balanced
Y
conguration with
q_{1}(Y)=q_{1}
,
u(Y)=u
,

\ell(Y)=\ell
and
\tau(Y)=\tau
.

The main goal of this section is to prove the next result, analogous to
[
EM18
, Prop. 11.4]
:

11.2.3 Proposition
.

For any
\delta>0
, there exists a compact set
K_{0}\subset X
with
\nu(K_{0})>1-\delta
, with the following properties.

Suppose
Y
,
Y^{\prime}
are bottom-linked, right-linked,
left-shadowing and
(C(\delta),ij)
-top-balanced
Y
-configurations with
Y\in K_{0}
and
Y^{\prime}\in K_{0}
.

Suppose (using the notation §
6.1.5
)
there exist
\tilde{q}_{2}\in K_{0}
and
\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}\in K_{0}
such that
\sigma(\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}^{u}[\sigma(\tilde{q}_{2})]
,
and also
d^{Q}(\tilde{q}_{2},q_{2})<\xi
and
d^{Q}(\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})<\xi
, for some
\xi>0
.

Then, provided
\xi
is small enough and
\tau=\tau(Y)
is large enough (depending on
K_{0}
), we have that

(11.2.4)

\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{u}[\tilde{q}_{2}].

Furthermore, there exists
\xi^{\prime}>0
(depending on
\xi
,
K_{0}
and
C

and
t_{2}
)
with
\xi^{\prime}\to 0
as
\xi\to 0
and
\tau\to\infty

such that

(11.2.5)

d_{*}(\wp^{+}(\tilde{q}_{2},\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})_{*}\widetilde{f}_{ij}[\tilde{q}_{2}],\widetilde{f}_{ij}[\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}])\leq\xi^{\prime},

where
d_{*}(\cdot,\cdot)
is as in
Definition 11.1.37
.

In the course of the proof, we will also prove
the following analogue of
[
EM18
, Lemma 11.6]
.

11.2.6 Lemma
.

For every
\delta>0
there exists a subset
K_{0}\subset X
with

\nu(K_{0})>1-\delta
and
C(\delta)<\infty

such that the following holds: Suppose
Y\in K_{0}
and
Y^{\prime}\in K_{0}

are bottom-linked, left-shadowing and
(C,ij)
-top-balanced
Y
-configurations. Then,

|t_{0}(Y)-t_{0}(Y^{\prime})|\leq C_{1}(\delta),

where
C_{1}(\delta)
depends on
C(\delta)
and
\delta
.

The proof of
Proposition 11.2.3
and

Lemma 11.2.6
will rely on the interpolation
map from
§6.2
,
instead of the more ad hoc construction from

[
EM18
]
.

11.2.7.
Preliminary constructions at the halfway points

Fix a compact set
K_{1}
and suppose
Y\in K_{1}
,
Y^{\prime}\in K_{1}
are
bottom-linked, right-linked,
left-shadowing and
(C,ij)
-top-balanced
Y
-configurations.
We use the notation §
6.1.5
. Suppose also

\tilde{q}_{2}\in K_{1}
and
\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}\in K_{1}
are as in

Proposition 11.2.3
.

Recall we write
y_{1/2}=g_{-\ell/2}uq_{1}
and
z_{1/2}=\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{fake,loc}[y_{1/2}]\cap\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[q^{\prime}_{1/2}]
as in
Figure 6.2.8
.
Recall also
y_{1/2}^{\prime}:=g_{-(\tau+l/2)}q_{2}^{\prime}=g_{-\ell/2}u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}
.
By assumption
y_{1/2}
,
y_{1/2}^{\prime}
, and
\widetilde{q}^{\prime}
are in
K_{1}
.

Recall that in
§6.2
we constructed (see
Figure 6.2.8
):

•

a subresonant “interpolation” map

\widetilde{\phi}(y_{1/2},z_{1/2})\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[y_{1/2}]\to\mathcal{W}^{u}[z_{1/2}]

•

an for any biregular point
x_{1/2}
on
\mathcal{W}^{u}[z_{1/2}]=\mathcal{W}^{u}[q_{1/2}^{\prime}]
a strictly subresonant map

\wp_{fake}^{+}(x_{1/2},z_{1/2})\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[x_{1/2}]\to\mathcal{W}^{u}[z_{1/2}]

We will denote the inverse of
\wp_{fake}^{+}(x_{1/2},z_{1/2})
by
\wp_{fake}^{+}(z_{1/2},x_{1/2})
.

For the constructions, see
Theorem 6.2.7
and
Proposition 6.2.13
.

We now define

(11.2.8)

\widetilde{\psi}(y_{1/2},y_{1/2}^{\prime})=\wp_{fake}^{+}(z_{1/2},y_{1/2}^{\prime})\circ\widetilde{\phi}(y_{1/2},z_{1/2}).

Note from the construction of
\widetilde{\phi}(y_{1/2},z_{1/2})
we have alternatively

(11.2.9)

\widetilde{\psi}(y_{1/2},y_{1/2}^{\prime})={\wp^{+}\left(q_{1/2}^{\prime},y_{1/2}^{\prime}\right)}\circ{\wp^{-}\left(q_{1/2},q_{1/2}^{\prime}\right)}\circ{\wp^{+}\left(y_{1/2},q_{1/2}\right)}.

11.2.10.
Pushing to the upper-left corner

Using the dynamics, we can now push these maps to the upper-left corner of the
Y
-diagram.

Define

\displaystyle z_{2}

\displaystyle:=g_{(\tau+\ell/2)}z_{1/2}

\displaystyle\text{the pushforward of a halfway point}.

We construct analogous objects in the upper-left corner of
Figure 4.5.9
:

•

An interpolation map

\phi_{\tau}(q_{2},z_{2})\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[q_{2}]\to\mathcal{W}^{u}[z_{2}]
,

\phi_{\tau}(q_{2},z_{2})=g_{\tau+\ell/2}\circ\widetilde{\phi}(y_{1/2},z_{1/2})\circ g_{-\tau-\ell/2}

•

Strictly subresonant polynomial maps
\wp_{fake}^{+}(x_{2},z_{2})\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[x_{2}]\to\mathcal{W}^{u}[z_{2}]
,

\wp_{fake}^{+}(x_{2},z_{2})=g_{\tau+\ell/2}\circ\wp_{fake}^{+}(x_{1/2},z_{1/2})\circ g_{-\tau-\ell/2}

for any biregular point
x_{2}\in\mathcal{W}^{u}[z_{2}]
.

•

The pushforward of
\tilde{\psi}(y_{1/2},y_{1/2}^{\prime})
:

\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[q_{2}]\to\mathcal{W}^{u}[q_{2}^{\prime}].
,

(11.2.11)

\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})=g_{\tau+\ell/2}\circ\widetilde{\psi}(y_{1/2},y_{1/2}^{\prime})\circ g_{-\tau-\ell/2}.

Note that in view of their definitions in
Eqn. (11.2.11)
and
Eqn. (11.2.9)
, we have that

(11.2.12)

\displaystyle\text{both }\psi\text{ and }\tilde{\psi}\text{ preserve the Zimmer flags}.

since all maps involved do, by the Ledrappier invariance principle.

11.2.13.
Convergence at tilde-points

Let
\tau(q)
be the measurable family of maps from
Proposition D.2.4
, and set

\eta(q,q^{\prime}):=\tau(q^{\prime})^{-1}\circ\tau(q)\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[q]\to\mathcal{W}^{u}[q^{\prime}].

Note that the map is subresonant, and in fact resonant if
\mathcal{W}^{u}[q],\mathcal{W}^{u}[q^{\prime}]
are equipped with resonant structures induced by the Oseledets decomposition at
q,q^{\prime}
.

11.2.14 Proposition

(Boundedness and Convergence)
.

For any
\delta>0
there exists a compact set
K\subset X

with
\nu(K)>1-\delta
with the following property. Suppose
Y
,
Y^{\prime}
are bottom-linked, right-linked,
left-shadowing and
(C(\delta),ij)
-top-balanced
Y
-configurations with
Y\in K
and
Y^{\prime}\in K
.

Then we have that:

(i)

The norm of
\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})
is bounded.

(ii)

Suppose (using the notation §
6.1.5
)
there exist
\tilde{q}_{2}\in K
and
\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}\in K
such that
\sigma_{0}(\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}^{u}[\sigma_{0}(\tilde{q}_{2})]
,
and also
d^{X}(\tilde{q}_{2},q_{2})<\xi
and
d^{X}(\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})<\xi
, for some
\xi>0
.
The transformation

\eta({q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}})\circ\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\circ\eta({\widetilde{q}_{2},q_{2}})\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[\widetilde{q}_{2}]\to\mathcal{W}^{u}[\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}]

converges to
\wp^{+}(\tilde{q}_{2},\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})
as

\tau,\ell\to+\infty
and
\xi\to 0
.

Proof of
Proposition 11.2.14
.

We recall the following notation. Suppose
\phi
is a subresonant map
between two unstable manifolds. Then, its linearization when acting on
the linearized unstables
L\mathcal{W}^{u}
is denoted
L\phi
.

We first prove (ii).
Note that the map

\Upsilon({\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}^{\prime}})=\eta(q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\circ\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\circ\eta(\widetilde{q}_{2},q_{2})\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[\widetilde{q}_{2}]\to\mathcal{W}^{u}[\widetilde{q}^{\prime}_{2}]

is a subresonant diffeomorphism taking
\widetilde{q}_{2}
to
\widetilde{q}^{\prime}_{2}
.
We have
\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})=\wp_{fake}^{+}(z_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\circ\phi(q_{2},z_{2})
and in view of
(
11.2.12
),
L\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})
intertwines the splittings of
L\mathcal{W}^{u}[q_{2}]
and
L\mathcal{W}^{u}[q_{2}^{\prime}]
.

Assuming
\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2},q_{2}^{\prime}
, and
q_{2}
all belong to a fixed
Lusin set
K_{2}
, given
\epsilon>0
we may assume

(i)

\left\|\gr_{\bullet}L(\eta(\widetilde{q}_{2},q_{2}))-\gr_{\bullet}I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{\widetilde{q}_{2},q_{2}}\right\|<\epsilon

(ii)

\left\|\gr_{\bullet}L(\eta(q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}))-\gr_{\bullet}I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\right\|<\epsilon

(iii)

\left\|\gr_{\bullet}L\phi(q_{2},z_{2})-\gr_{\bullet}I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{q_{2},z_{2}}\right\|<\epsilon

(iv)

\left\|\gr_{\bullet}(I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\circ P_{fake}^{+}(z_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\circ I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{\widetilde{q}_{2},z_{2}})-\gr_{\bullet}P^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\right\|<\epsilon.

The first two estimates follow by applying
Proposition E.2.11
to the trivialization of
L\mathcal{W}^{u}
provided by
Proposition D.2.4
, which gives the analogous estimates but without
\gr_{\bullet}
.
But the estimate on
L\mathcal{W}^{u}
implies the one on
\gr_{\bullet}L\mathcal{W}^{u}
.

For the last two estimates, note that
z_{2}
is not assumed biregular, it is however backwards-regular and we can obtain the needed bounds.
Specifically, the third estimate follows from
Proposition 6.3.4
.

For the fourth estimate, recall that
\gr_{\bullet}P_{fake}^{+}(z_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})=H_{z_{2},q_{2}^{\prime}}^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}
where
H_{z_{2},q_{2}^{\prime}}^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}
is the holonomy on
\gr_{\bullet}L\mathcal{W}^{u}
as in
Corollary E.2.20
.
The estimate follows from the Hölder continuity of holonomies on Pesin sets, as explicated in
Corollary E.2.20
,
Eqn. (E.2.22)
.

It now follows that

\displaystyle\|\gr_{\bullet}L\Upsilon_{\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q_{2}}^{\prime}}-

\displaystyle\gr_{\bullet}P^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\gr_{\bullet}I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\gr_{\bullet}P_{fake}^{+}(z_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\gr_{\bullet}I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{q_{2},z_{2}}\gr_{\bullet}I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{\widetilde{q}_{2},q_{2}}-\gr_{\bullet}P^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\|+C\epsilon

\displaystyle=\|\gr_{\bullet}I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\gr_{\bullet}P_{fake}^{+}(z_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\gr_{\bullet}I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{\widetilde{q}_{2},z_{2}}-\gr_{\bullet}P^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\|+C\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq(C+1)\epsilon.

It follows that

\gr_{\bullet}L\Upsilon_{\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q_{2}}^{\prime}}
converges to
\gr_{\bullet}P^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})
. Since
L\Upsilon_{\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q_{2}}^{\prime}}
intertwines the splittings, it follows that

L\Upsilon_{\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q_{2}}^{\prime}}
converges to
P^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})
.
This implies
\Upsilon_{\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q_{2}}^{\prime}}
converges to
\wp^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})
and finishes the proof of (ii).

To prove (i), recall that

\gr_{\bullet}L\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})=H^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{z_{2},q_{2}^{\prime}}\circ\gr_{\bullet}L\phi(q_{2},z_{2})

where
H^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}
is the holonomy on
\gr_{\bullet}L\mathcal{W}^{u}
.
By
Corollary E.2.20
the holonomy
H^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{z_{2},q_{2}^{\prime}}
and
I^{L\mathcal{W}^{u}}_{z_{2},q_{2}^{\prime}}
are bounded distance apart (in fact arbitrarily close), which together with the third estimate above implies that
\gr_{\bullet}L\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})
is bounded on
K_{2}
.
Note that
L\psi
preserves the Oseledets decomposition by construction, and the isomorphism induced by the Oseledets splitting between
\gr_{\bullet}L\mathcal{W}^{u}
and
L\mathcal{W}^{u}
is bounded on compact sets of arbitrarily large measure.
It follows that
L\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})
itself is bounded on such sets.
∎

11.2.15 Remark
.

Consider two points
a,b\in Q
and a subresonant map
\psi\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[a]\to\mathcal{W}^{u}[b].
Suppose moreover that
\psi
intertwines a compatible family of subgroups as in
Definition 3.1.5
at
a
and
b
; that is, suppose that

\psi(U^{+}[a])=U^{+}[b]
so that
\psi
induces a map
\mathcal{C}[a]\to\mathcal{C}[b]
. If moreover
\psi(a)=b
then this map descends to a well-defined map

\mathbf{L}\psi\colon\mathbf{H}(a)\to\mathbf{H}(b).

The
\psi
from
Eqn. (11.2.11)
has this property, and this is the map we use going forward.
Recall that
\mathbf{R}(\cdot,\cdot)
was defined in (
10.3.2
). Then

\mathbf{R}(\cdot,\cdot)
acts on
\mathbf{H}
, and thus on

\mathbf{E}\subset\mathbf{H}
.

To single out the measurable connections on
\mathbf{H}
, we will write
\mathbf{P}^{\pm}
for
P^{\pm}_{\mathbf{H}}
.

11.2.16 Proposition

(Agreement of transports)
.

Suppose
Y
,
Y^{\prime}
are bottom-linked, left-shadowing and right-linked

Y
-configurations. We use the notation from

§6.1.5
.
Consider the two maps

\displaystyle\mathbb{b}\mathbf{R}(q_{3}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{P}^{-}(q_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})\colon

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(q_{3})\to\mathbf{E}(q_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\circ\mathbb{b}\mathbf{R}(q_{3},q_{2})\colon

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(q_{3})\to\mathbf{E}(q_{2}^{\prime})

(i)

The two maps agree, when all points belong to a set of full measure on which the maps are defined.

(ii)

For any
\delta>0
there exists a compact set

K_{1}\subset X
with
\nu(K)>1-\delta
, such that
if
Y\in K
,
Y^{\prime}\in K
and both
Y
and
Y^{\prime}

are
(C,ij)
-top-balanced,
then each of the maps above is of bounded norm on

\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}
, with bound on the norm depending
only on
\delta
and
C
.

Proof.

We first prove (i).
Note that by

Proposition 10.1.5
, we have (acting on
\mathbf{E}
)

(11.2.17)

\mathbf{R}(q_{3},q_{2})=g_{(\tau+\ell/2)}\circ\mathbf{P}^{+}(q_{1/2},y_{1/2})\circ g_{-(\tau+\ell/2)}

Similarly,

(11.2.18)

\mathbf{R}(q_{3}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})=g_{(\tau+\ell/2)}\circ\mathbf{P}^{+}(q_{1/2}^{\prime},y_{1/2}^{\prime})\circ g_{-(\tau+\ell/2)}

Also from (
11.2.9
), we have

(11.2.19)

\mathbf{P}^{+}(q_{1/2}^{\prime},y_{1/2}^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{P}^{-}(q_{1/2},q_{1/2}^{\prime})=\mathbf{L}\tilde{\psi}(y_{1/2},y_{1/2}^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{P}^{+}(q_{1/2},y_{1/2}).

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}(q_{3}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\circ\mathbf{P}^{-}(q_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})=g_{(\tau+\ell/2)}\circ\mathbf{P}^{+}(q_{1/2}^{\prime},y_{1/2}^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{P}^{-}(q_{1/2},q_{1/2}^{\prime})\circ g_{-(\tau+\ell/2)}

by (
11.2.18
)

\displaystyle=g_{(\tau+\ell/2)}\circ\mathbf{L}\tilde{\psi}(y,y_{1/2}^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{P}^{+}(q_{1/2},y_{1/2})\circ g_{-(\tau+\ell/2)}\qquad

by (
11.2.19
)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\circ g_{(\tau+\ell/2)}\circ\mathbf{P}^{+}(q_{1/2},y_{1/2})\circ g_{-(\tau+\ell/2)}\qquad

by (
11.2.11
)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{R}(q_{3},q_{2})\qquad

by (
11.2.17
)

To prove (ii), note that by the Ledrappier invariance principle (
Corollary B.2.7
),

\mathbf{P}^{-}(q_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(q_{3})=\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(q_{3}^{\prime})
. Also,
by the choice of the Lusin set
K
, we can make sure that

\|\mathbf{P}^{-}(q_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})\|\leq C(\delta)
. We may also choose
K
so
that the the function
C(\cdot)
of

Proposition 10.5.3
is bounded by

C(\delta)
on
K
. Then, by

Proposition 10.5.3
, for
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(q_{3}^{\prime})
,
\|\mathbf{R}(q_{3}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime}){\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\|\leq C(\delta)\|{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\|
. This completes the proof of (ii).
∎

Figure 11.2.20.

11.2.21.
Proof of
Proposition 11.2.3

We first have

d_{*}(\mathbf{P}^{-}(q_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{3}),\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{3}^{\prime}))<\xi^{(2)}\text{ with $\xi^{(2)}\to 0$ as $\xi\to 0$.}

We also have (by
Proposition 11.2.16
(ii)
) that
\mathbf{R}(q_{3}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})
is a bounded map.
By
Lemma 11.1.36
, we have
d_{*}\left(\mathbf{R}(q_{3}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{3}^{\prime}),\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{2}^{\prime})\right)=0
.
Assembling, we have

d_{*}\left(\mathbf{R}(q_{3}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{P}^{-}(q_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{3}),\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{2}^{\prime})\right)<\xi^{(3)}\text{with $\xi^{(3)}\to 0$ as $\xi\to 0$.}

We again have
d_{*}\left(\mathbf{R}(q_{3},q_{2})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{3}),\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{2})\right)=0

so

\displaystyle d_{*}\big(\mathbf{L}\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{2}),\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{2}^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=d_{*}\left(\mathbf{R}(q_{3}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{P}^{-}(q_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{3}),\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{2}^{\prime})\right)

is bounded above by
\xi^{(4)}\to 0
as
\xi\to 0
.

By the above and
Proposition 11.2.14
, we have

\displaystyle d_{*}(\mathbf{P}^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(\widetilde{q}_{2}),\mathbf{f}_{ij}(\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq d_{*}(\mathbf{P}^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(\widetilde{q}_{2}),I^{\mathbf{H}}_{q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\mathbf{L}\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})I^{\mathbf{H}}_{\widetilde{q}_{2},q_{2}}\mathbf{f}_{ij}(\widetilde{q}_{2}))

\displaystyle\quad+d_{*}(I^{\mathbf{H}}_{q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\mathbf{L}\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})I^{\mathbf{H}}_{\widetilde{q}_{2},q_{2}}\mathbf{f}_{ij}(\widetilde{q}_{2})),I^{\mathbf{H}}_{q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\mathbf{L}\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{2})))

\displaystyle\quad+d_{*}(I^{\mathbf{H}}_{q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\mathbf{L}\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{2}),I^{\mathbf{H}}_{q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\quad+d_{*}(I^{\mathbf{H}}_{q_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\mathbf{f}_{ij}(q_{2}^{\prime}),\mathbf{f}_{ij}(\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}))

and all four terms on the right go to zero as
\xi\to 0
.

We have now shown

d_{*}(\mathbf{P}^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\mathbf{f}_{ij}(\widetilde{q}_{2}),\mathbf{f}_{ij}(\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}))<\xi^{(5)}

for some
\xi^{(5)}
.
Pushing forward both measures by
(\mathbf{j}_{\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}}^{-1})^{-1}
we have that

d_{*}(\wp^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\overline{f}_{ij}[\widetilde{q}_{2}],\overline{f}_{ij}[\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}])<\xi^{(6)}.

By the construction in
§11.1.34
, it also follows that:

d_{*}(\wp^{+}(\widetilde{q}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})\widetilde{f}_{ij}[\widetilde{q}_{2}],\widetilde{f}_{ij}[\widetilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}])<\xi^{(7)}

and the inequality asserted in
Eqn. (11.2.5)
holds.

The convergence in (
11.2.4
) now follows since the

\psi
maps converge to the
P^{+}
maps and a point in the lifted
unstable is a point downstairs plus a flag and
P^{+}
intertwines the
flags. (See discussion in
§
2.5.4
).
∎

11.2.22.
Proof of
Lemma 11.2.6

We will need the following:

11.2.23 Lemma
.

For every
\delta>0
there exists a compact
K\subset X
with

\nu(K)>1-\delta
such that the following holds: Suppose
x\in K
,
x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}^{-}[x]\subset\mathcal{W}^{s}[x]

and suppose
t>0
is such that
g_{t}x\in K
,
g_{t}x^{\prime}\in K
. Then,

(11.2.24)

|\lambda_{ij}(x,t)-\lambda_{ij}(x^{\prime},t)|\leq C(\delta).

Proof.

For any
\delta>0
we can choose a subset
K_{0}=K_{0}(\delta)
such
that
\nu(K_{0})>1-\delta
and also the Lyapunov splitting of
\mathbf{H}
and the
dynamical norm
\|\cdot\|
vary continuously with respect to a trivialization and
ambient norm provided by
Corollary B.2.7
.
Then, provided
x,x^{\prime},g_{t}x,g_{t}x^{\prime}\in K_{0}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}^{-}[x]
,
we have

(11.2.25)

\|\mathbf{P}^{-}(x,x^{\prime})\|\leq C(\delta),\qquad\|\mathbf{P}^{-}(g_{t}x,g_{t}x^{\prime})\|\leq C(\delta).

From
Eqn. (10.2.2)
which defined

\lambda_{ij}
, let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{E}^{\lambda_{i}}_{\leq j}(x)/\mathbf{E}^{\lambda_{i}}_{\leq j-1}(x)
and let

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{P}^{-}(x,x^{\prime})\mathbf{v}
. In view of (
11.2.25
),

\left\|\mathbf{v}\right\|
and
\left\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\right\|
are within a bounded multiplicative constant.

Next we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{2}:=g_{t}\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\prime}:=g_{t}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}\right\|:=e^{\lambda_{ij}(x,t)}\left\|\mathbf{v}\right\|

\displaystyle\text{ and }\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\prime}\right\|:=e^{\lambda_{ij}(x^{\prime},t)}\left\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\right\|

We also have
\mathbf{P}^{-}(g_{t}x,g_{t}x^{\prime}){\mathbf{v}}_{2}={\mathbf{v}}_{2}^{\prime}
. Thus, in view of
(
11.2.25
),
\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}\right\|
and
\left\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}\right\|
are also
within a bounded multiplicative constant depending on
\delta
, and so
(
11.2.24
) follows.
∎

Proof of
Lemma 11.2.6
.

We use the same notation as in the proof of

Proposition 11.2.3
.
Let
\tilde{\psi}
and

\psi
be as in the proof of

Proposition 11.2.3
.

First, we check that

|\lambda_{ij}(y_{1/2};\tau+\ell/2)-\lambda_{ij}(y_{1/2}^{\prime};\tau+\ell/2)|<c_{1}

for a constant that depends only on
\delta
. This argument is similar
to the proof of
Lemma 11.2.23
.
From
Eqn. (10.2.2)
which defined
\lambda_{ij}
, let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{E}^{\lambda_{i}}_{\leq j}/\mathbf{E}^{\lambda_{i}}_{\leq j-1}(y_{1/2})
and let
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{L}\widetilde{\psi}(y_{1/2},y_{1/2}^{\prime})\mathbf{v}
.
Note that
\left\|\widetilde{\psi}\right\|
is uniformly bounded in terms of

\delta
by
Proposition 11.2.14
(i), so
\left\|\mathbf{v}\right\|
and
\left\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\right\|
are within a bounded multiplicative constant.

Next we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{2}:=g_{\tau+l/2}\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\prime}:=g_{\tau+l/2}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}\right\|:=e^{\lambda_{ij}\left(y_{1/2},\tau+l/2\right)}\left\|\mathbf{v}\right\|

\displaystyle\text{ and }\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\prime}\right\|:=e^{\lambda_{ij}\left(y_{1/2},\tau+l/2\right)}\left\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\right\|

Recall that by (
11.2.12
), the map
\mathbf{L}\psi
preserves the Zimmer flags and intertwines the dynamics.
So
\mathbf{L}\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\mathbf{v}_{2}=\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, by
Proposition 11.2.14
(i)
,
\mathbf{L}\psi(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})
is bounded in norm, with bound depending on
\delta
.
So
\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}\right\|
and
\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\prime}\right\|
are within a bounded multiplicative constant depending on
\delta
, and so the bound

|\lambda_{ij}(y_{1/2};\tau+\ell/2)-\lambda_{ij}(y_{1/2}^{\prime};\tau+\ell/2)|<C_{1}(\delta)\text{ follows.}

Since
Y
is
(C,ij)
-top-balanced,

|\lambda_{ij}(y_{1/2};\tau+\ell/2)-\lambda_{ij}(q_{1/2};t_{0}(Y)+\ell/2)|\leq C.

Similarly, since
Y^{\prime}
is
(C,ij)
-top-balanced,

|\lambda_{ij}(y_{1/2}^{\prime};\tau+\ell/2)-\lambda_{ij}(q_{1/2}^{\prime};t_{0}(Y^{\prime})+\ell/2)|\leq C.

Combining the above three displayed equations, we get

\lambda_{ij}(q_{1/2}^{\prime};t_{0}(Y^{\prime})+\ell/2)-\lambda_{ij}(q_{1/2};t_{0}(Y)+\ell/2)|\leq C(\delta).

By
Lemma 11.2.23
,

\lambda_{ij}(q_{1/2}^{\prime};t_{0}(Y^{\prime})+\ell/2)-\lambda_{ij}(q_{1/2};t_{0}(Y^{\prime})+\ell/2)|\leq C(\delta).

Therefore,

|\lambda_{ij}(q_{1/2};t_{0}(Y^{\prime})+\ell/2)-\lambda_{ij}(q_{1/2};t_{0}(Y)+\ell/2)|\leq C(\delta),

and the bound
|t_{0}(Y)-t_{0}(Y^{\prime})|<C(\delta)
follows because

\lambda_{ij}
is monotonic and with Lipschitz constant bounded above
and below by
Proposition B.4.1
.
∎

12.
Extra Invariance

Outline of section

In this section we prove
Theorem 4.1.2
.
The proof of
Theorem 4.1.4
is analogous and follows the same steps, with only straightforward notational changes.

Recall from
Definition 11.1.30
and
Definition 11.1.31
that
f_{ij}[x]
denotes conditional measures supported on the atoms
\Psi_{ij}[x]
, while
\tilde{f}_{ij}[x]
denotes (conditional) leafwise measures, which locally on
\Psi_{ij}[x]
agree with
f_{ij}[x]
, and are extended equivariantly (up to scaling) for the dynamics.

12.0.1.
Measurable trivializations of measures

In order to describe the continuity properties of the measures
\tilde{f}_{ij}[x]
on
\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
, we make the following construction.
Let
T(x)\colon L\mathcal{W}^{u}(x)\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{u}}
be a measurable trivialization of the bundle
L\mathcal{W}^{u}(x)
, compatible with the appropriate subresonant structure on
\mathbb{R}^{n_{u}}
.
Recall that
L_{x}\colon\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]\to L\mathcal{W}^{u}(x)
denotes the linearization map, and set
\mathfrak{f}_{ij}(x):=T(x)_{*}(L_{x})_{*}\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]
to be a measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n_{u}}
.

Let
\wp^{+}(x,y)
be the map introduced in
Proposition D.2.2
.

Theorem 4.1.2
will be derived from the
following:

12.0.2 Proposition
.

Suppose
U^{+}

are as in
Theorem 4.1.2
and that the QNI
condition of
Definition 3.2.2
holds.
Then there exists
0<\delta_{0}<0.1
, a subset
K_{*}\subset X
with

\nu(K_{*})>1-\delta_{0}
such that
all the functions
\mathfrak{f}_{ij}
,
\forall ij\in\tilde{\Lambda}

are uniformly continuous on
K_{*}
, and
C>1
(depending on
K_{*}
)
such that for every
0<\epsilon<C^{-1}/100
there exists a
subset

with

, such that for every
x\in E
there exists
ij\in\tilde{\Lambda}
and

with

(12.0.3)

C^{-1}\epsilon\leq d_{\mathcal{H},loc}^{x}(U^{+}[x],U^{+}[y])\leq C\epsilon

and furthermore:

(12.0.4)

\tilde{f}_{ij}[y]\propto\wp^{+}(x,y)_{*}\tilde{f}_{ij}[x].

To establish that
\nu(E)>\delta_{0}
we will show that any compact set
K_{00}
with
\nu(K_{00})>1-2\delta_{0}
contains a point

satisfying

and

.

A broad outline of the proof of

is contained in

.

12.1.5 Definition

(

12.1.6 Claim
.

There exists
\ell_{3}=\ell_{3}(K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)>0
, a
set
K_{3}=K_{3}(K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)
of measure at least

1-c_{3}(\delta)
and for each
q_{1}\in K_{3}
a subset
Q_{3}=Q_{3}(q_{1},K_{00},\ell,\delta,\epsilon,\eta)\subset\mathcal{B}_{0}(q_{1})
of
measure at least
(1-c_{3}^{\prime}(\delta))|\mathcal{B}_{0}(q_{1})|
such that for each

q_{1}\in K_{3}
and
u\in Q_{3}
there exists a subset
E_{good}(q_{1},u)\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that the density of
E_{good}(q_{1},u)
(for

\ell>\ell_{3}
) is at least
1-c_{3}^{\prime\prime}(\delta)
such that if
q_{1}\in K_{3}
,
u\in Q_{3}
and
\ell\in E_{good}(q_{1},u)
, we have for each

ij
,

Y_{ij,balanced}(q_{1},u,\ell)\in K.

Also, we have

c_{3}(\delta)
,
c_{3}^{\prime}(\delta)
and

c_{3}^{\prime\prime}(\delta)\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
.

The proof is a routine application of the biLipschitz estimates from
Proposition 8.4.15
:

Proof of claim.

Let

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{00}(q_{1})=\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{00}(q_{1},K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)=\{t>0\;\>:\;\>g_{t}q_{1}\in K\text{ and }g_{-t}q_{1}\in K\}.

For
ij\in\tilde{\Lambda}
, let

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(q_{1})=\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(q_{1},K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)=\{\lambda_{ij}(q_{1},t)\;\>:\;\>g_{t}q_{1}\in K,\quad t>0\}.

Then by the ergodic theorem and (
10.2.3
),
there exists a set
K_{\mathcal{D}}=K_{\mathcal{D}}(K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)

with
\nu(K_{\mathcal{D}})\geq 1-\delta
and

\ell_{\mathcal{D}}=\ell_{\mathcal{D}}(K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)>0

such that for
q_{1}\in K_{\mathcal{D}}
and all
ij\in\{00\}\cup\tilde{\Lambda}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(q_{1})
has
density at least
1-2\kappa\delta
for
\ell>\ell_{\mathcal{D}}
. Let

E_{2}(q_{1},u)=E_{2}(q_{1},u,K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)=\{\ell\;\>:\;\>g_{\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1},u,\ell)}uq_{1}\in K\},

From the definition of
E_{2}(q_{1},u)
, it follows that if
\ell\in E_{2}(q_{1},u)
then for any
ij
,

(12.1.7)

q_{2}(Y_{ij,balanced}(q_{1},u,\ell))\in K.

Let

E_{3}(q_{1},u)=E_{3}(q_{1},u,K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)=\\
=\{\ell\in E_{2}(q_{1},u)\;\>:\;\>\forall ij\in\tilde{\Lambda},\quad\lambda_{ij}(uq_{1},\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1},u,\ell))\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(q_{1})\}.

Note that for any
ij
we have
\lambda_{ij}(uq_{1},\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1},u,\ell))\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(q_{1})

if and only if

\lambda_{ij}(uq_{1},\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1},u,\ell))=\lambda_{ij}(q_{1},s)\text{ and }g_{s}q_{1}\in K.

Thus, if
\ell\in E_{3}(q_{1},u)
, then for any
ij
,
(
12.1.7
) holds and also

(12.1.8)

q_{3}(Y_{ij,balanced}(q_{1},u,\ell))\in K.

We choose
K_{2}=K\cap K_{\mathcal{D}}
, and

K_{3}=K_{2}\cap\{x\in X\;\>:\;\>|\{u\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(x)\;\>:\;\>ux\in K_{2}\}|>(1-\delta)|\mathcal{B}_{0}(x)|\}.

Suppose
q_{1}\in K_{3}
,
and
uq_{1}\in K_{2}
.
Let

E_{bad}=\{t\;\>:\;\>g_{t}uq_{1}\notin K\}.

Then, since
uq_{1}\in K_{\mathcal{D}}
, for
\ell>\ell_{\mathcal{D}}
,
the density of
E_{bad}
is at most
2\kappa\delta
. We have

E_{2}(q_{1},u)^{c}=\{\ell\;\>:\;\>\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1},u,\ell)\in E_{bad}\}.

Then, by
Proposition 8.4.15
, for
\ell>\kappa\ell_{\mathcal{D}}
,
the density of
E_{2}(q_{1},u)
is at least
1-4\kappa^{2}\delta
.

Let

\hat{\mathcal{D}}(q_{1},u)=\hat{\mathcal{D}}(q_{1},u,K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)=\{t\;\>:\;\>\forall ij\in\tilde{\Lambda},\quad\lambda_{ij}(uq_{1},t)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(q_{1})\}.

Since
q_{1}\in K_{\mathcal{D}}
, for each
j
, for
\ell>\ell_{\mathcal{D}}
, the
density of
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{ij}(q_{1})
is at least

1-2\kappa\delta
.
Then, by (
10.2.3
), for
\ell>\kappa\ell_{\mathcal{D}}
,
the density of

\hat{\mathcal{D}}(q_{1},u)
is at least

(1-4|\tilde{\Lambda}|\kappa^{2}\delta)
.
Now from the definitions we have

E_{3}(q_{1},u)=E_{2}(q_{1},u)\cap\{\ell\;\>:\;\>\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1},u,\ell)\in\hat{\mathcal{D}}(q_{1},u)\}.

Therefore, in view of
Proposition 8.4.15
, the
density of
E_{3}(q_{1},u)
is at least
1-c(\delta)
where

as
\delta\to 0
.

Let
E_{4}(q_{1},u)=\{\ell>0\;\>:\;\>g_{-\ell/2}uq_{1}\in K\}
. Let

is at least

where

as
\delta\to 0
. Let

. Then, if

then

) and (

)
hold, and also

,

,
g_{-\ell}q_{1}\in K
, and

. Thus,
Y_{ij,balanced}(q_{1},u,\ell)\in K
.
∎

The numbers

(12.1.18)

\lambda_{ij}\left(u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime},\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\ell)\right)=\lambda_{ij}(q_{1}^{\prime},t_{ij}^{\prime}).

Alternatively,

(12.1.19)

t_{ij}^{\prime}(u^{\prime})=t_{0}\left(Y_{ij,balanced}(q_{1}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\ell)\right).

Then, by the same argument,

(12.1.20)

g_{t_{ij}^{\prime}(u^{\prime})}q_{1}^{\prime}\in K.

12.1.21.
The map
{\mathbf{v}}(u)
and the generalized subspace
\mathcal{U}(u)
.

For

, let

(12.1.22)

{\mathbf{v}}(u)={\mathbf{v}}(q,q^{\prime},u,\ell,\tau)=\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1},u,\ell,\tau)F^{s}_{q}(q^{\prime}),

.

. Then, by

,

\mathbf{j}(\mathcal{U}(u))={\mathbf{v}}(u)+O(e^{-\alpha\ell}).

12.1.23.
Standing Assumption.

Let
\epsilon_{0}(\delta)=\epsilon_{0}(\delta,\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}(\delta))<\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}(\delta)

\epsilon<0.01\epsilon_{0}(\delta)

12.1.24 Claim
.

There exists a subset
Q_{5}=Q_{5}(q_{1},\ell,K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)\subset Q_{4}(q_{1},\ell)

(12.1.27)

C_{1}^{\prime}(\delta)\epsilon\leq d^{u}_{\mathcal{H},loc}(U^{+}[g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1}),\phi_{\tau(u)}^{-1}(U^{+}[q_{1}^{\prime}]))\leq C_{1}(\delta)\epsilon.

.

Proof of claim.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{u}
be the subspace of

Lemma 9.2.1
applied to the linear map

be as in
(

Let

be such that for all

,

d_{V^{s}(q)}(F^{s}_{q}(q^{\prime}),\mathcal{M}_{u})\geq\beta(\delta)

where

. Then,
(

),

. Also, by (

Then, let

Q_{5}=\{u\in Q_{5}^{\prime}\;\>:\;\>d(u,\partial\mathcal{B}_{0}(q_{1}))>\delta\}
, hence

|Q_{5}|\geq(1-c_{5}^{\prime}(\delta)-c_{4}^{\prime}(\delta)-c_{n}\delta)|\mathcal{B}_{0}(q_{1})|,

where

depends only on the dimension.

We have
C(\delta)^{-1}\epsilon\leq\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1},u,\ell,t)\|\leq C(\delta)\epsilon
by the definition of

. We now apply

to the linear map

\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1},u,\ell,t)
. Then, for all

,

c(\delta)\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1},u,\ell,t)\|\leq\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1},u,\ell,t)F_{q}(q^{\prime})\|\leq\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1},u,\ell,t)\|.

Therefore,

C^{\prime}(\delta)^{-1}\epsilon\leq\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1},u,\ell,t)F_{q}(q^{\prime})\|\leq C^{\prime}(\delta)\epsilon

This immediately implies (
12.1.26
), in view
of the definition of
{\mathbf{v}}(u)
. Then,
(
12.1.27
) follows from

Proposition 5.1.16
.

Finally, suppose
u\in Q_{5}
, and
u^{\prime}\in U^{+}(q_{1}^{\prime})
is such that

Eqn. (12.1.28)
holds.
Let
z_{t}:=g_{\ell/2+t}z_{1/2}
.
Recall that the interpolation map satisfies
\phi\left(y_{1/2}\right)=z_{1/2}
where

; therefore

for

.

The assumption in

states that

d^{u}\left(z_{\tau(u)},g_{\tau(u)}u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}\right)\leq 1\text{ therefore }d^{u}\left(z_{0},u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}\right)\leq e^{-\kappa\cdot\tau(u)}.

Since

(and
z_{0}=\phi_{0}(uq_{1})
) this implies that there exists a

such that

Now
Theorem 6.3.3
implies that

d^{Q}\left(uq_{1},\phi_{0}^{-1}(u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime})\right)\leq C_{2}(\delta)e^{-\kappa\cdot\tau(u)}

for some

.

we have

d^{u}\left(uq_{1},\phi_{0}^{-1}(u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime})\right)\leq C_{3}(\delta)e^{-\kappa\cdot\tau(u)}.

Again since

, we have that

. Therefore, for

However, by

and since

was chosen so that the map

is
uniformly continuous with respect to the Hausdorff topology,
q_{1}\in K
, and
q_{1}^{\prime}\in K
,
we have, for

sufficiently large,

d_{\mathcal{H}}^{Q}(\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q_{1}],\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q_{1}^{\prime}])\leq 0.1\epsilon^{\prime}.

Therefore,
u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q_{1}^{\prime}]
, which implies
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(q_{1}^{\prime})
.
∎

12.1.29.
Standing Assumption.

We assume
\ell>\ell_{5}
.

12.1.30.
Recap

Under our standing assumptions, for
u\in Q_{5}(q_{1},\ell)
,
the
Y
-configuration
Y=Y_{ij,balanced}(q_{1},u,\ell)
satisfies
Y\in K
. If
u^{\prime}\in U^{+}(q_{1}^{\prime})
is such that
(
12.1.28
) holds, then the
Y
-configuration

Y^{\prime}=Y(q_{1}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\ell,\tau(u))
is bottom-linked to
Y
and left-shadows

Y
. Also, (
12.1.26
) and (
12.1.27
) hold.

However, we do not know that
Y^{\prime}\in K
(we only know that

q^{\prime}\in K
,
q_{1}^{\prime}\in K
and
q_{1/2}^{\prime}\in K
).
The next two claims aim to deal with this issue.

12.1.31 Claim
.

Suppose
u\in Q_{5}(q_{1},\ell)
,
u^{\prime}\in Q_{4}(q_{1}^{\prime},\ell)
and (
12.1.28
) holds.
Then, there exists
C_{0}=C_{0}(\delta)
such that

(12.1.32)

|\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1},u,\ell)-\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\ell)|\leq C_{0}(\delta).

Proof of claim.

Let
\tau=\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1},u,\ell)
,
\tau^{\prime}=\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\ell)
. In view of
(
12.1.10
),
we have
g_{\tau}uq_{1}\in K
,
g_{\tau^{\prime}}u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}\in K
.
Suppose
\tau\leq\tau^{\prime}
.
By the definition of
K
, there exists
s<\tau
with
(\tau-s)<(1/2)\kappa^{-2}(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)+T_{0}(\delta)
such that
g_{s}uq_{1}\in K_{0}
,
g_{s}u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}\in K_{0}
. By
Lemma 8.4.1
and
Lemma 9.3.1
, we have (as long as
\ell
is
sufficiently large compared with
\epsilon
),

\displaystyle\epsilon=\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1}^{\prime},\ell,u^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\geq e^{\kappa^{-1}(\tau^{\prime}-s)}\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1}^{\prime},\ell,u^{\prime},s)\|

\displaystyle\geq e^{\kappa^{-1}(\tau^{\prime}-s)}C(\delta)^{-1}\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1},\ell,u,s)\|

\displaystyle\geq e^{\kappa^{-1}(\tau^{\prime}-s)}C(\delta)^{-1}e^{-\kappa(\tau-s)}\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(q_{1},\ell,u,\tau)\|=\epsilon

Thus,

1\geq e^{\kappa^{-1}(\tau^{\prime}-s)}C(\delta)^{-1}e^{-\kappa(\tau-s)}

hence

\kappa^{-1}(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)\leq\kappa^{-1}(\tau^{\prime}-s)\leq\kappa(\tau-s)+C(\delta)\leq(1/2)\kappa^{-1}(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)+C(\delta).

Thus,
(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)<2\kappa C(\delta)
as required.

The proof in the case
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
is identical.
∎

12.1.33 Claim
.

There exists a subset
Q_{6}(q_{1},\ell)=Q_{6}(q_{1},\ell,K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)\subset Q_{5}(q_{1},\ell)

12.1.35.
Remark.

The claim implies that for all
u\in Q_{6}(q_{1},\ell)
there exists
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(q_{1}^{\prime})
such that for
any
ij
, if we write

and

,

and

and

are bottom-linked and also

up to the

\tau(Y)
.

Proof of Claim.

Note that the sets

\{\mathcal{B}_{\tau(u)}[uq_{1}]\;\>:\;\>u\in Q_{5}(q_{1},\ell)\}

are a cover of
Q_{5}(q_{1},\ell)q_{1}
.
Then, since these sets satisfy the condition of

Lemma A.5.7
(b), we can

u_{j}\in Q_{5}(q_{1},\ell)
,

,
with

Q_{5}(q_{1},\ell)q_{1}\subset\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{N}\mathcal{B}_{\tau(u_{j})}[u_{j}q_{1}]
; in particular,

\mathcal{B}_{\tau(u_{j})}[u_{j}q_{1}]
and
\mathcal{B}_{\tau(u_{k})}[u_{k}q_{1}]
are disjoint for

j\neq k
. Let

P_{j}=g_{-\tau(u_{j})}\mathcal{B}^{(\epsilon_{0}^{\prime})}_{0}[g_{\tau(u_{j})}u_{j}q_{1}]\subset\mathcal{B}_{\tau(u_{j})}[u_{j}q_{1}]\subset U^{+}[q_{1}].

Then
P_{j}
and
P_{k}
are disjoint for
j\neq k
.

Using notation (
6.3.2
), let
\phi^{j}_{s}=\phi_{s}(Y(q_{1},u_{j},\ell),q_{1}^{\prime})
, that is
\phi^{j}_{s}
denote the interpolation operator
\phi_{s}
where

is replaced by

. Let

P_{j}^{\prime}=\phi_{0}^{j}\left(g_{-\tau(u_{j})}\mathfrak{B}^{(\epsilon_{0}^{\prime})}_{0}[g_{\tau(u_{j})}u_{j}q_{1}]\right)\cap U^{+}\left[q_{1}^{\prime}\right]=\\
g_{-\tau(u_{j})}\left(\phi^{j}_{\tau(u_{j})}\left(\mathfrak{B}^{(\epsilon_{0}^{\prime})}_{0}\left[g_{\tau(u_{j})}u_{j}q_{1}\right]\right)\cap U^{+}\left[g_{\tau(u_{j})}q_{1}^{\prime}\right]\right)\subset U^{+}\left[q_{1}^{\prime}\right].

In view of
Theorem 7.4.3
,

d_{\mathcal{H}}^{Q}\left(P_{j},P_{j}^{\prime}\right)=O\left(e^{-\alpha\ell}\right).

Also, since
K
was chosen so that the maps

is
uniformly continuous with respect to the Hausdorff topology,
we have, for
\ell
sufficiently large,

(12.1.36)

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}^{Q}(\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q_{1}],\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q_{1}^{\prime}])

\displaystyle\leq 0.1\epsilon^{\prime}(\delta)

(12.1.37)

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{H}}^{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{0}[q_{1}],\mathcal{B}_{0}[q_{1}^{\prime}])

\displaystyle\leq 0.1\epsilon^{\prime}(\delta)

Thus, since
P_{j}\subset\mathfrak{B}_{0}^{(\epsilon^{\prime})}[q_{1}]
, we have
P_{j}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q_{1}^{\prime}]
. Since by construction,
P_{j}^{\prime}\subset U^{+}[q_{1}^{\prime}]
, we have

P_{j}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{B}_{0}[q_{1}^{\prime}]
.

Note that by (
12.1.27
) and

Theorem 6.3.3

for any
u\in P_{j}
,
u^{\prime}\in P_{j}^{\prime}
,

(12.1.38)

d^{Q}(g_{\tau(u_{j})}uq_{1},g_{\tau(u_{j})}u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime})<C(\delta)\epsilon+O(e^{-\alpha\ell}).

We now claim that
P_{j}^{\prime}
and
P_{k}^{\prime}
are disjoint for
j\neq k
.

Suppose
u^{\prime}\in P_{j}^{\prime}\cap P_{k}^{\prime}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
\tau(u_{j})\leq\tau(u_{k})
.
Then
w\equiv g_{\tau(u_{j})}u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}=\phi^{j}_{\tau(u_{j})}(x_{j})=\phi^{k}_{\tau(u_{j})}(x_{k})
, where

x_{j}\in{\mathfrak{B}}^{(\epsilon_{0}^{\prime})}_{0}[g_{\tau(u_{j})}u_{j}q_{1}]
,
x_{k}\in{\mathfrak{B}}^{(\epsilon_{0}^{\prime})}_{0}[g_{\tau(u_{j})}u_{k}q_{1}]
.
By
Theorem 6.3.3
,
for all
0\leq t\leq\tau(u_{j})
,

d^{Q}(g_{-t}x_{j},g_{-t}x_{k})=O(e^{-\alpha\ell}).

Let
y_{j}=g_{\tau(u_{j})}u_{j}q_{1}
,
y_{k}=g_{\tau(u_{j})}u_{k}q_{1}
.

By
Lemma 2.5.10
applied with

, we have:

For

sufficiently large this implies

. However, since

. Now, since

,

\mathfrak{B}_{\tau(u_{k})}[u_{k}q_{1}]\subseteq\mathfrak{B}_{\tau(u_{j})}[u_{k}q_{1}]=\\
=g_{-\tau(u_{j})}\mathfrak{B}_{0}[y_{k}]=g_{-\tau(u_{j})}\mathfrak{B}_{0}[y_{j}]=\mathfrak{B}_{\tau(u_{j})}[u_{j}q_{1}],

which is a contradiction for

.
This proves the required
disjointness.

By
Lemma 9.4.2

provided
P_{j}\cap Q_{5}(q_{1},\ell)\neq\emptyset
, we have

\kappa^{-1}|P_{j,m}|\leq|P_{j,m}^{\prime}|\leq\kappa|P_{j}|
, where

depends only on the Lyapunov
spectrum, and we have normalized the measures

so that

. (Note that

applies since in view of

, and because

holds).
We now apply

to obtain the set

.

It remains to replace

by

in

(12.1.39)

|\tau(u)-\tau(u_{j})|\leq C_{1}(\delta).

Then, provided

,

),
(

∎

12.1.40 Claim
.

,

(12.1.42)

\|(g_{s}u_{2}g_{t}^{-1})_{*}{\mathbf{v}}(u)-{\mathbf{v}}(u_{1})\|=O(e^{\kappa^{\prime}L^{\prime}}e^{-\alpha\ell}).

, for
1-\delta
fraction
of

Note that

\mathcal{B}(g_{t}^{-1}g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1})=g_{\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1},u,\ell)-t}B_{u}.

Therefore, for
1-\delta
fraction of

,

(12.1.43)

d\left(\frac{{\mathbf{v}}(u_{1})}{\|{\mathbf{v}}(u_{1})\|},\mathbf{E}(g_{\tau(u_{1})}u_{1}q_{1})\right)\leq C(\epsilon,\delta)[e^{\kappa^{\prime}L^{\prime}-\alpha\ell}+e^{-\alpha L^{\prime}}]

We can now choose
L^{\prime}>0
to be
\alpha^{\prime}\ell
where
\alpha^{\prime}>0
is
a small constant depending only on the Lyapunov spectrum,
and
\ell_{8}>0
so that for
\ell>\ell_{8}

the right-hand-side of the above equation is at most
e^{-\alpha^{\prime}\ell}
.

The collection of balls
\{B_{u}\}_{u\in Q_{7}^{*}(q_{1},\ell)}

are a cover of
Q_{7}^{*}(q_{1},\ell)
. These balls satisfy
the condition of
Lemma A.5.7
(b); hence we may
choose a pairwise disjoint subcollection which still covers

Q_{7}^{*}(q_{1},\ell)
. Then, by summing (
12.1.43
), we see
that
(
12.1.41
) holds for
u
in a subset

Q_{8}\subset\mathcal{B}_{0}[q_{1}]
of measure at least
(1-c_{8}(\delta))|\mathcal{B}_{0}[q_{1}]|=(1-\delta)(1-c_{7}^{*}(\delta))|\mathcal{B}_{0}[q_{1}]|
.
∎

12.1.44 Claim
.

There exists a subset
Q_{8}^{*}(q_{1},\ell)=Q_{8}^{*}(q_{1},\ell,K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)\subset Q_{8}(q_{1},\ell)
with

such that for

where

as

.

Proof of Claim.

This follows immediately from

Lemma A.6.6
.
∎

Choice of parameters #3: Choice of
\delta
.

(12.1.47)

12.1.48 Claim
.

There exist sets
Q_{9}(q_{1},\ell)=Q_{9}(q_{1},\ell,K_{00},\delta,\epsilon,\eta)\subset Q_{8}^{*}(q_{1},\ell)

with

,
such that for

(12.1.49)

d\left(\frac{{\mathbf{v}}(u)}{\|{\mathbf{v}}(u)\|},\bigcup_{ij\in\tilde{\Lambda}}\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1})\right)<C(\delta)\eta.

Proof of claim.

Suppose
u\in Q_{8}^{*}(q_{1},\ell)
. Then, by
(
12.1.41
) and (
12.1.26
),
we may write

{\mathbf{v}}(u)={\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)+{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime\prime}(u),

where

and
\|{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime\prime}(u)\|\leq C(\delta)e^{-\alpha^{\prime}\ell}
.

Let
L=L_{0}(\delta,\eta)
where
L_{0}(\delta,\eta)
is as in

Proposition 10.5.2
.
Suppose
u_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{\tau(u)-L}(uq_{1})u\cap Q_{8}(q_{1},\ell)
.
Let
u_{2}
be such that
u_{1}=u_{2}u
. Then,
by
Lemma 9.5.2
,

{\mathbf{v}}(u_{1})=(g_{\tau(u_{1})}\circ(u_{2})_{*}\circ g_{-\tau(u)}){\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)+O(e^{-\alpha^{\prime}\ell+\kappa^{3}L})

Since
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{E}(g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1})
and in view of

Proposition 10.1.5
, we can rewrite the

(12.1.50)

{\mathbf{v}}(u_{1})=\mathbf{R}(g_{\tau(u_{1})}u_{1}q_{1},g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1}){\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)+O(e^{-\alpha^{\prime}\ell+\kappa^{3}L}).

In view of (
12.1.26
), we have, for
sufficiently large

,

e^{-\kappa T_{1}(\delta)/2}\epsilon\leq\|{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)\|\leq e^{\kappa T_{1}(\delta)/2}\epsilon,

and

e^{-\kappa T_{1}(\delta)/4}\epsilon\leq\|{\mathbf{v}}(u_{1})\|\leq e^{\kappa T_{1}(\delta)/4}\epsilon.

Let
s>0
be such that

y\equiv g_{s}u_{1}q_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)}[g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1},L]
. From

we have

\|\mathbf{R}(g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1},y){\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)\|=\|{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)\|
. Thus,

(12.1.51)

|s-\tau(u_{1})|\leq T_{1}(\delta),

and hence

.
Then,
by

applied with

,

and
{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)
, we get that for a
at least
\theta^{\prime}
-fraction of

d\left(\frac{\mathbf{R}(g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1},y){\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)}{\|\mathbf{R}(g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1},y){\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)\|},\bigcup_{ij\in\tilde{\Lambda}}\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(y)\right)<2\eta.

From (

) and the definition of

we have

{\mathbf{v}}(u_{1})=g_{\tau(u_{1})-s}\mathbf{R}(g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1},y){\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(u)+O(e^{-\alpha^{\prime}\ell+\kappa^{3}L}).

Therefore, in view of (

),
there a subset
Q_{tmp}
of

such that for

In view of (

) and

), the above equation holds for at

-fraction of

.
We may choose

so that

the right-hand side of the above equation is at
most

. Thus, (

) holds for

(\theta^{\prime}/2)
-fraction of

.

The collection of balls

are a cover of

. These balls satisfy

(b); hence we may

. Then, by summing over the disjoint subcollection, we see
that the claim holds on a set

of measure at least

(\theta^{\prime}/2)|Q_{8}^{*}|\geq(\theta^{\prime}/2)(1-c_{8}^{*}(\delta))\geq(\theta^{\prime}/2)(1-\theta^{\prime}/2)
.
∎

\ell

12.1.53.

u
,

,

q_{2}^{\prime}
,

ij
,

Let

.
We have

,
Y^{\prime}_{*}\in K
.
By (

),

(12.1.54)

\displaystyle|\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1},u,\ell)-\tau_{(\epsilon)}(q_{1}^{\prime},u^{\prime},\ell)|\leq C_{0}(\delta),

g_{\tau(u)}u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}\in g_{[-C,C]}K,

By the definition of

C_{4}(\delta)
and

q_{2}\equiv g_{s}g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1}\in K_{0},\qquad q_{2}^{\prime}\equiv g_{s}g_{\tau(u)}u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}\in K_{0}.

Let

be the
Y
-configuration with the same points as

,

q_{2}(Y_{sh})=q_{2}
. Similarly, let

-configuration with the same points as

. Then,

,

.

In view of (

,

(iv)
.

, we

\frac{1}{C(\delta)}\epsilon\leq d_{\mathcal{H},loc}^{q_{2}}(U^{+}[q_{2}],\phi_{\tau(u)+s}^{-1}(U^{+}[q_{2}^{\prime}]))\leq C(\delta)\epsilon.

Additionally, we have the inequality in

, and since

by

we conclude that:

(12.1.56)

d^{Q}(q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})\leq C_{5}(\delta)d^{Q}(g_{\tau(u)uq_{1},g_{\tau(u^{\prime})}u^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}})\\
\leq C_{5}(\delta)C(\delta)\epsilon\leq 0.01\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}(\delta).

where we have used the standing assumption
§
12.1.23
.
Therefore, the
Y
-configuration
Y_{sh}^{\prime}
left-shadows the

Y
-configuration
Y_{sh}
.

In view of (
12.1.49
), we can choose

ij\in\tilde{\Lambda}
so that

(12.1.57)

d\left(\frac{{\mathbf{v}}(u)}{\|{\mathbf{v}}(u)\|},\mathbf{E}_{[ij],bdd}(g_{\tau(u)}uq_{1})\right)\leq C(\delta)\eta.

Since
Y_{bal}
and

are
(0,ij)
-top-balanced, and since

,
Y_{sh}
and
Y_{sh}^{\prime}
are
(C,ij)
-top-balanced,
for some

. Then, by

,
we have
|t_{0}(Y_{sh})-t_{0}(Y_{sh}^{\prime})|\leq C(\delta)
. However the

(resp.

) agree with the

(resp.
Y_{bal}^{\prime}
). Therefore,

(12.1.58)

|t_{ij}(u)-t_{ij}^{\prime}(u^{\prime})|\leq C_{5}(\delta).

Therefore, by (

) and (

), we
have

g_{t_{ij}(u)}q_{1}\in K,\quad\text{ and }\quad g_{t_{ij}(u)}q_{1}^{\prime}\in g_{[-C_{5}(\delta),C_{5}(\delta)]}K.

By the definition of
K
, we can find
s^{\prime\prime}\in[0,C_{5}^{\prime\prime}(\delta)]

such that

q_{3,ij}\equiv g_{s^{\prime\prime}+t_{ij}(u)}q_{1}\in K_{0},\quad\text{ and }\quad q_{3,ij}^{\prime}\equiv g_{s^{\prime\prime}+t_{ij}(u)}q_{1}^{\prime}\in K_{0}.

Let
Y
be the
Y
-configuration with the same points as

,
except that

. Similarly, let
Y^{\prime}
be the

Y
-configuration with the same points as
Y_{sh}^{\prime}
,

. Then,
Y\in K_{0}
,
Y^{\prime}\in K_{0}
.

By construction,

and

are right-linked. Also, since

since

and

were

\delta)
of

and

. In addition,

and

are

Y_{sh}

and
Y_{sh}^{\prime}
. We will apply

Proposition 11.2.3
to

and
Y^{\prime}
in the next
subsection.

12.1.59.
Taking the limit as
\eta\to 0
.

For fixed

\delta
and
\epsilon
,
we now take a sequence of
\eta_{k}\to 0
(this forces
\ell_{k}\to\infty
) and pass to

\tilde{q}_{2}\in K_{0}
be the limit of the

q_{2}
, and
\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}\in K_{0}
be the limit of the
q_{2}^{\prime}
.
We may also

is fixed,
where

is as in (

). By passing to

), we get

(12.1.60)

\frac{1}{C(\delta)}\epsilon\leq d_{\mathcal{H},loc}^{\tilde{q}_{2}}(U^{+}[\tilde{q}_{2}],U^{+}[\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}])\leq C(\delta)\epsilon.

We now apply
Proposition 11.2.3
(with
\xi\to 0
as

). By (

),
\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{+}[\tilde{q}_{2}]
. By applying
g_{s}
to

) and then passing to the limit, we get

U^{+}[\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{E}_{ij}(\tilde{q}_{2})
. Finally,

) that

, and thus,

. Hence,

Now, by (

), we have

f_{ij}(\tilde{q}_{2})\propto P^{+}(\tilde{q}_{2},\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime})_{*}f_{ij}(\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}).

We have
\tilde{q}_{2}\in K_{0}\subset K_{00}\cap K_{*}
, and

.
This concludes the proof of

.
∎

12.2.
The implication have friend implies have invariance

12.2.1.
Setup

We now show that
Proposition 12.0.2
implies
Theorem 4.1.2
.
Take a sequence
\epsilon_{n}\to 0
and apply

Proposition 12.0.2
with
\epsilon=\epsilon_{n}
; after passing to a subsequence, assume
ij
is
constant.
We get, for each
n
a set
E_{n}\subset K_{*}

with
\nu(E_{n})>\delta_{0}
and with the property that for every
x\in E_{n}
there exists
y\in\Psi_{ij}[x]\cap K_{*}
such that
Eqn. (12.0.3)
and

Eqn. (12.0.4)
hold for
\epsilon=\epsilon_{n}
.
Let

F=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{n=k}^{\infty}E_{n}\subset K_{*},

(so
F
consists of the points which are in infinitely many

E_{n}
).
Suppose
x\in F
.
Then
there exists a sequence
y_{n}\to x
such that
y_{n}\in\Psi_{ij}[x]
,

y_{n}\not\in U^{+}[x]
, and such that
\widetilde{f}_{ij}[y_{n}]\propto\wp^{+}(x,y_{n})_{*}\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]
.
Because
y_{n}\in\Psi_{ij}[x]
we have that
\tilde{f}_{ij}[y_{n}]\propto\tilde{f}_{ij}[x]
(and this holds for any
x,y_{n}
that are in the same atom
\Psi_{ij}[x]
).
We can therefore conclude that

\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]\propto\wp^{+}(x,y_{n})_{*}\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x].

Since
y_{n}\notin U^{+}(x)\cdot x
, we have that
\wp^{+}(x,y_{n})\not\in U^{+}(x)
.

12.2.2.
Extra invariance up to scale

For
x\in X
, let
U_{new}^{+}(x)
denote the maximal connected
subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(x)

such that for
u\in U_{new}^{+}(x)
we have that

(12.2.3)

u_{*}\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]\propto\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x].

Since
\wp^{+}(x,y_{n})\not\in U^{+}(x)
for every
n
and since
\wp^{+}(x,y_{n})\to{\bm{1}}
as
y_{n}\to x
we have for

x\in F
, that
U_{new}^{+}(x)
strictly contains
U^{+}(x)
.
We will now check that
U_{new}^{+}(x)
yields a family of compatible subgroups as in
Definition 3.1.5
.

Suppose
x\in F
,
t<0
and
g_{t}x\in F
.
Then, since the leafwise measures
\tilde{f}_{ij}[x]
are
g_{t}
-equivariant (up to proportionality) we have that
Eqn. (12.2.3)
holds for
u\in g_{-t}U^{+}(g_{t}x)
.
Therefore, by the maximality of
U_{new}^{+}(x)
, for
x\in F
and
t<0
with
g_{t}x\in F
we have that

(12.2.4)

g_{-t}U_{new}^{+}(g_{t}x)=U_{new}^{+}(x),

and
Eqn. (12.2.3)

still holds.

We now verify that
U^{+}_{new}(x)
can be defined for
x
in a full measure set.
Since
\nu(F)>\delta_{0}>0
and
g_{t}
is
ergodic, for almost all
x\in X
there exist arbitrarily large
t>0
such that
g_{-t}x\in F
.
Then, we define
U_{new}^{+}(x)
to be
g_{t}U_{new}^{+}(g_{-t}x)
.
This is consistent in view of
Eqn. (12.2.4)
.

12.2.5.
Extra invariance on the nose

We next claim for
u\in U_{new}^{+}(x)
that

(12.2.6)

u_{*}\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]=\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]

and not just that the measures are proportional.
Indeed, from
Eqn. (12.2.3)
we get that for a.e.
x
and
u\in U_{new}^{+}(x)
,

(12.2.7)

u_{*}\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x]=e^{\beta_{x}(u)}\widetilde{f}_{ij}[x],

where
\beta_{x}\colon U_{new}^{+}(x)\to\mathbb{R}
is a homomorphism.

It follows from (
12.2.7
) that for a.e.
x\in X
,

u\in U_{new}^{+}(x)
and
t>0
,

(12.2.8)

\beta_{g_{-t}x}(g_{-t}ug_{t})=\beta_{x}(u).

We can write

\beta_{x}(u)=L_{x}(\log u),

where
L_{x}\colon\mathfrak{u}^{+}(x)\to\mathbb{R}
is a Lie algebra homomorphism
(which is in particular a linear map). Let
K\subset X
be a positive
measure set for which there exists a constant
C
with
\|L_{x}\|\leq C

for all
x\in K
. Now for almost all
x\in X
and
u\in U^{+}_{new}(x)
there exists a
sequence
t_{j}\to\infty
so that
g_{-t_{j}}x\in K
and
g_{-t_{j}}ug_{t_{j}}\to{\bm{1}}
, where
{\bm{1}}
is the identity element of

U^{+}_{new}
.
Then,
Eqn. (12.2.8)
applied to the
sequence
t_{j}
implies that
\beta_{x}(u)=0
almost everywhere.
Therefore, for almost all
x\in X
,
the conditional measure of
\nu
along the orbit
U_{new}^{+}[x]
is the
push-forward of the Haar measure on
U_{new}^{+}(x)
, and hence
Eqn. (12.2.6)
holds.

12.2.9.
Checking compatibility with admissible partitions

For checking part (ii) of
Definition 3.1.5
, we proceed with any measurable partition
\mathfrak{B}
, which is subordinate to the unstable foliation
\mathcal{W}^{u}
.
Let
f_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon X\to X_{\mathfrak{B}}
be the measurable map to the space of atoms of
\mathfrak{B}
.
Let also
\sigma_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon X_{\mathfrak{B}}\to X
be a section of the projection, i.e. a measurable choice of “center” in each atom.

Now, since the family of leafwise measures
\tilde{f}_{ij}[x]
is measurable in
x
, the Lie algebra of its stabilizer
\mathfrak{u}^{+}_{new}(x)
is a measurable family of Lie algebras in
\mathfrak{g}^{ssr}(x)
.
Recall that
U^{+}_{new}(x)
was the corresponding (nilpotent) Lie group and
U^{+}_{new}[x]\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
are the corresponding orbits.
We need to check that the partition whose atoms are
U^{+}_{new}[x]\cap\mathfrak{B}[x]
is itself measurable.
We will follow the technique of
§11.1.27
.

Form the measurable bundle
\mathcal{O}\to X
whose fiber is
\mathcal{O}(x):=\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]/U^{\prime}(x)
, where
U^{\prime}(x)
ranges over all connected nilpotent Lie subgroups of
\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(x)
, of the same dimension as
U^{+}_{new}(x)
.
The Lie algebras
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}(x)
determine
U^{\prime}(x)
uniquely and the space of Lie algebras is an algebraic subset of a Grassmannian of
\mathfrak{g}^{ssr}(x)
.

Let next
s_{new}\colon X\to\mathcal{O}
be the (tautological) measurable section which maps
x
to the orbit
U^{+}_{new}[x]
.
The space
\mathcal{O}
carries on the unstables a “Gauss–Manin” connection
I(x,y)\colon\mathcal{O}(x)\to\mathcal{O}(y)
obtained by the identification
\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]=\mathcal{W}^{u}[y]
for
y\in\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
.

On the subset of
X\times X
consisting of pairs
(x,y)
with
x\in\mathfrak{B}[y]
the map
I(x,y)
is measurable (recall that
\mathfrak{B}
is subordinate to
\mathcal{W}^{u}
).
Recall also that
\sigma\colon X_{\mathfrak{B}}\to X
is a measurable choice
of center, and let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{B}}:=\sigma^{*}\mathcal{O}
be the pullback of the
space of orbits to the “center” in each atom; to ease notation we
will write
x_{c}:=\sigma_{\mathfrak{B}}(f_{\mathfrak{B}}(x))\in\mathfrak{B}[x]

as the “projection to the center”.
We can now construct a measurable map
c_{\mathfrak{B}}\colon X\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{B}}
that assigns to
x
the point
I(x,x_{c})s_{new}(x)\in\mathcal{O}(x_{c})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f_{\mathfrak{B}}(x))
.
By construction, we have that
c_{\mathfrak{B}}(x)=c_{\mathfrak{B}}(y)
if and only if
x,y
are in the same atom of
\mathfrak{B}
and
y\in U^{+}_{new}[x]
, or equivalently
x\in U^{+}_{new}[y]
.
∎

Appendix A
Nonuniform partially hyperbolic background

We consider a continuous-time dynamical system
g_{t}\colon Q\to Q
preserving an ergodic probability measure
\nu
.
The space
Q
is assumed to be a manifold, though this is not always needed.
All statements are understood to hold
\nu
-a.e. unless further specified.

A.0.1.
Summary of constructions with charts and distances

In addition to standard constructions of stable and unstable manifolds, we will make use of the following distances adapted to the dynamics:

Ambient distance:

d^{Q}
coming from the Riemannian metric on
Q
.

Lyapunov distance:

d^{\mathcal{L}}_{q}(q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime})
will only be defined for
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{k,\varepsilon}[q]
(see
§A.2.9
); the parameters
k,\varepsilon
will be suppressed from the notation.

Unstable distance:

d^{u}(q,q^{\prime})
will only be defined for
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{u}[q]
and will have a uniform contraction property under
g_{-t}
for
t\geq 0
(see
§A.2.10
); the parameter
\varepsilon>0
is suppressed from the notation.

Induced ambient distance:

d^{Q,u}
on
\mathcal{W}^{u}[q]
induced by the path metric from the ambient distance.

Distance on flags:

For two flags
F_{1},F_{2}
of a fixed vector space
V
with a Riemannian positive-definite bilinear form, we denote by
\dist(F_{1},F_{2})
the corresponding distance in the flag manifold induced by the unique metric which is invariant under
\Orthog(V)
.

We will also construct the following types of charts adapted to the dynamics:

Lyapunov charts:

\mathcal{L}_{k,\varepsilon}[q]\subset Q
will denote the
(k,\varepsilon)
-Lyapunov chart at
q
(see
§A.2.9
).

Local unstable manifold:

(see
§A.2.10
)

\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[q]:=\{q^{\prime}\colon d^{u}(q,q^{\prime})<1\}\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}[q].

A.1.
General dynamics estimates

We begin with some basic notions which are relevant to nonuniformly hyperbolic systems.

A.1.1 Proposition

(Growth dichotomy)
.

Suppose that
a\colon Q\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable.
Then for a set of full measure
Q_{0}
:

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\frac{1}{|t|}a(g_{t}q)=\limsup_{t\to-\infty}\frac{1}{|t|}a(g_{t}q)\in\{0,\infty\}

The analogous result holds for
\liminf
and
\{0,-\infty\}
.

The proof is contained in
[
Arn98
, 4.1.3]
.

A.1.2 Definition

(Temperedness and slow variation)
.

Consider a measurable function
A\colon Q\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
Then
A
is called
tempered
if in
Proposition A.1.1
, the function
a:=\log A
has both
\limsup
and
\liminf
equal to
0
.

The function
A
is called
\varepsilon
-slowly varying
if
e^{-\varepsilon|t|}\leq A(q)/A(g_{t}q)\leq e^{\varepsilon|t|}
for
\nu
-a.e.
q\in Q
and
\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
.

The following properties are a consequence of Birkhoff ergodic theorem as well as
[
Arn98
, 4.3.2-4.3.3 and Prop. 4.1.3]
.

A.1.3 Proposition

(On temperedness and slow variation)
.

(i)

Suppose
A
is
\varepsilon
-slowly varying, or more generally satisfies

\sup_{t\in[-1,1]}|\log\left(A(q)/A(g_{t}q)\right)|\leq\phi(q)\quad\text{ for some }\phi\in L^{1}(Q,\nu).

Then
A
is tempered.

(ii)

Suppose
A
is tempered.
Then for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists an
\varepsilon
-slowly varying
B
such that
A\leq B
.

Suppose
E\to Q
is a cocycle over
g_{t}
, with a metric on fibers satisfying the integrability assumption

(A.1.4)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[-1,1]}\log\left\|g_{t}^{E}(q)\right\|\in L^{1}(Q,\nu).

We state the Oseledets multiplicative ergodic theorem in a form that will be convenient for us (see
[
Arn98
, Thm. 4.3.4]
).

A.1.5 Theorem

(Tempered Oseledets)
.

There exists a measurable decomposition into subcocycles
E=\oplus E^{\lambda_{i}}
with distinct
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, and a tempered function
C(q)
such that

\displaystyle C(q)^{-1}e^{-\varepsilon|t|}\left\|v\right\|\leq\left\|g_{t}v\right\|e^{\lambda_{i}t}\leq C(q)e^{\varepsilon|t|}\left\|v\right\|

\displaystyle\forall v\in E^{\lambda_{i}}(q)

\displaystyle\measuredangle\left(E^{\lambda_{i}}(q),E^{\lambda_{j}}(q)\right)>C(q)^{-1}

\displaystyle\forall i\neq j

Note that by
Proposition A.1.3
, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists an
\varepsilon
-slowly varying
C_{\varepsilon}(q)
such that the above bounds hold with
C_{\varepsilon}
instead of
C
.

A.1.6.
Lyapunov norms

We recall next a construction from
[
BP07
, §3.5.1-3]
, see also
[
Arn98
, Thm. 4.3.6]
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a measurable norm
\left\|-\right\|_{\varepsilon}
on the cocycle such that

e^{-|t|\varepsilon}\left\|v_{i}\right\|_{\varepsilon}\leq\left\|g_{t}v_{i}\right\|_{\varepsilon}e^{-t\lambda_{i}}\leq e^{t|\varepsilon|}\left\|v_{i}\right\|_{\varepsilon}\quad\forall v_{i}\in E(q)^{\lambda_{i}}.

Furthermore, the original norm
\left\|-\right\|
on
E
and the
\varepsilon
-Lyapunov norm
\left\|-\right\|_{\varepsilon}
are related by

A_{\varepsilon}(q)^{-1}\left\|v\right\|\leq\left\|v\right\|_{\varepsilon}\leq A_{\varepsilon}(q)\left\|v\right\|\quad\forall v\in E(q)

for an
\varepsilon
-slowly varying measurable function
A_{\varepsilon}(q)
.

A.1.7.
One-sided Lyapunov norms

We next describe a variation of the constructions in
[
BP07
, §3.5.1-3]
, but adapted to unstable manifolds and backwards dynamics.

A.1.8 Proposition

(Unstable Lyapunov norms)
.

Suppose
E\to Q
is an admissible cocycle which varies smoothly along unstables, equipped with its norm which varies smoothly along unstables.
Write
E^{u}\oplus E^{cs}
for the decomposition into strictly positive and nonpositive Lyapunov exponents, and let
\lambda>0
be its smallest strictly positive Lyapunov exponent.

There exists a set of full measure
Q_{0}\subset Q
, as well as its saturation by the unstable foliation
Q_{0}^{u}
, such that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a norm
\left\|-\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}
on
E|_{Q_{0}^{u}}
which varies smoothly along unstables, and a measurable
\varepsilon
-slowly varying
C_{\varepsilon}
, such that
\forall t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\left\|g_{-t}v\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}\leq e^{-(\lambda-\varepsilon)t}\left\|v\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\forall v\in E^{u}

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(q)e^{-\varepsilon t}\left\|v\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}\leq

\displaystyle\left\|g_{-t}v\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\forall v\in E^{cs}(q)

Proof.

Write
E=E^{u}\oplus E^{u,\perp}
where
E^{u}
is the subcocycle with strictly positive Lyapunov exponents and
E^{u,\perp}
is its orthogonal complement (both bundles vary smoothly along unstables).
Let
\Pi\colon E\to E^{u,\perp}
be the projection map.
Note that
\Pi g_{t}\Pi v=\Pi g_{t}v
for any vector
v
.

Let
\lambda
be the smallest strictly positive Lyapunov exponent of
E
.
For
v\in E^{u}
define now the norm (the integral converges by the Oseledets theorem):

\displaystyle\left\|v\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\|g_{-s}v\right\|^{2}e^{2(\lambda-\varepsilon)s}ds

and keep the original norm on
E^{u,\perp}
and keep the decomposition orthogonal.
It follows by construction that
\left\|g_{-t}v\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}\leq e^{-(\lambda-\varepsilon)t}\left\|v\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}
for
t\geq 0
and any
v\in E^{u}
.
Note also that

\left\|v\right\|\leq\left\|v\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}\leq A(q)\left\|v\right\|\quad\forall v\in E(q)

for a tempered function
A
.

Now the second bound for the original norm follows from the Oseledets theorem again, and so it follows for the modified norm by adjusting the slowly varying functions.
∎

The next result allows us to relate the hyperbolic behavior of points along the same stable manifold.

A.1.9 Theorem

(Shadowing Flags)
.

Let
A_{n}
and
B_{n}
be sequence of matrices. Fix
D
and
\eta>0
with
\|A_{n}\|,\|B_{n}\|,\|A_{n}^{-1}\|,\|B_{n}^{-1}\|\leq De^{\eta n}
.
Fix
\delta>0
and
\kappa>\eta>0
.
Suppose
\{A_{n}\}
is forwards regular with Lyapunov exponents
\Lambda
. Suppose also that

\|A_{n}-B_{n}\|\leq\delta e^{-\kappa n},\quad\quad\|A_{n}^{-1}-B_{n}^{-1}\|\leq\delta e^{-\kappa n}.

Then

(i)

\{B_{n}\}
is forwards regular with Lyapunov exponents
\Lambda
.

(ii)

If
\{A_{n}\}\in\Omega^{+,\Lambda}_{N,\epsilon}
and
\delta>0
is sufficiently small, then
\{B_{n}\}\in\Omega^{+,\Lambda}_{2N,\epsilon}

(iii)

The Lyapunov flags for
\{A_{n}\}
and
\{B_{n}\}
are
\delta^{\theta}
-close for some
\theta=\theta(\Lambda)
.

Proof.

This is in different notation
[
Rue79
, Thm. 4.1]
, with the modification that we extend our finite sequence to an infinite one with
B_{n+1}=A_{n+1}
.
∎

A.2.
Lyapunov charts and distances

A.2.1.
Setup

We assume that
(Q,g_{t})
has an ambient systole function satisfying the assumptions from
§2.1.6
, and that the ergodic invariant measure
\nu
satisfies the assumptions preceding
Proposition 2.2.4
.

A.2.2.
Start of proof of
Proposition 2.2.4

Recall that
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bullet\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
denotes the standard norm on the tangent space
TQ(q)
and
\exp_{q}\colon TQ(r_{a}(q))\to Q
are our exponential charts.
Recall that in case of Finsler norms, by
Remark 2.1.4
that, up to a uniformly bounded constant, we may assume each norm
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bullet\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{q}
is induced by an inner product
{\left\langle\kern-1.72218pt\left\langle\kern-1.72218pt\left\langle\bullet,\bullet\right\rangle\kern-1.72218pt\right\rangle\kern-1.72218pt\right\rangle}_{q}
at every
q
.

A.2.3 Lemma

(Standard hyperbolicity estimates)
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a measurable function
A_{\varepsilon}\colon Q\to[1,\infty)
such that

(i)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\in TQ^{\lambda_{i}}(q)
,

\frac{1}{A_{\varepsilon}(q)}e^{-\varepsilon/8|t|}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|v\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\leq e^{-\lambda_{i}t}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|Dg_{t}v\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\leq A_{\varepsilon}(q)e^{\varepsilon/8|t|}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|v\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}.

(ii)

for all
\lambda_{i}\neq\lambda_{j}
,
\sin\angle(TQ^{\lambda_{i}}(q),TQ^{\lambda_{j}}(q))\geq\frac{1}{A_{\varepsilon}(q)}
.

(iii)

A_{\varepsilon}(g_{t}q)\leq e^{\varepsilon/4|t|}A_{\varepsilon}(q)
.

Proof of
Proposition 2.2.4
.

Let
\left\|\bullet\right\|_{\varepsilon}
be the Lyapunov norm and let
A_{\varepsilon}\colon Q\to[1,\infty)
the
\varepsilon/2
-slowly growing measurable function in
§A.1.6
comparing
\left\|\bullet\right\|_{\varepsilon}
and
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bullet\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
.
By
Proposition A.1.3
, given
\varepsilon>0
and
k\in\{1,2,3,\dots\}
we may take
\psi_{k,\varepsilon}\colon Q\to[1,\infty)
an
\varepsilon/2
-slowly growing measurable function such that

\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)\geq[A_{\varepsilon}(q)]^{2}\max\left\{1,\phi_{a}(q)^{e_{0}+e},2\varepsilon^{-1}\phi_{a}(q)^{a_{k}}\right\}.

Set

\phi_{k,\varepsilon}(q):=A_{\varepsilon}(q)\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q).

Then
Proposition 2.2.4
(i)
holds.
Define the norm
\left\|\bullet\right\|_{k,\epsilon}
as

(A.2.4)

\left\|v\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}:=\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)\left\|v\right\|_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{ for }v\in TQ(q).

For
v\in TQ(q)
, we have

(A.2.5)

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|v\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\leq A_{\varepsilon}(q)\left\|v\right\|_{\varepsilon}\leq\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)\left\|v\right\|_{\varepsilon}=\left\|v\right\|_{\varepsilon,k}

and

(A.2.6)

\left\|v\right\|_{\varepsilon}=\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)\left\|v\right\|_{\varepsilon}\leq\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)A_{\varepsilon}(q){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|v\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}.

and so
Proposition 2.2.4
(ii)
holds.
Also, for
v\in TQ(q)
,

\frac{\left\|D_{q}g_{t}v\right\|_{k,\epsilon}}{\left\|v\right\|_{k,\epsilon}}\frac{\left\|v\right\|_{\epsilon}}{\left\|D_{q}g_{t}v\right\|_{\epsilon}}=\psi_{k,\epsilon}(g_{t}(q))\psi_{k,\epsilon}(q)^{-1}.

Since

e^{-|t|\epsilon/2}\leq\psi_{k,\epsilon}(g_{t}(q))\psi_{k,\epsilon}(q)^{-1}\leq e^{|t|\epsilon/2},

Proposition 2.2.4
(iii)
holds by the properties of the function
A_{\varepsilon}
in
§A.1.6
.

Finally, recall that relative to the norms
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bullet\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
, for all
t\in[-1,1]
the map

G_{t,q}\colon B_{q}\big(q;r_{d}(q)\big)\to B\big({g_{t}(q)};r_{a}(g_{t}(q))\big)
,

G_{t,q}(v)=\exp_{g_{t}q}^{-1}\circ g_{t}\circ\exp_{q}(v)

is well-defined.
If
\left\|v\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}\leq 1
then by the choice of
\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)
,

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|v\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\leq A_{\varepsilon}(q)\left\|v\right\|_{\varepsilon}=A_{\varepsilon}(q)\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)^{-1}\left\|v\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}\leq\phi_{a}(q)^{-e-e_{0}}\leq\phi_{a}(q)^{-e}r_{a}(q)=r_{d}(q)

and thus, for
t\in[-1,1]
,
G_{t,q}(\bullet)
is defined on the
\left\|\bullet\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}
-ball
B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;1)
.
Furthermore, relative to the
\left\|\bullet\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}
-norms, for
v\in B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;1)
and
2\leq j\leq k
we have

\displaystyle\left\|D^{j}_{v}G_{t,q}\right\|_{\left\|\bullet\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq\phi_{k,\varepsilon}(g_{t}q)\left\|D^{j}_{v}G_{t,q}\right\|_{{\left|\kern-0.75346pt\left|\kern-0.75346pt\left|\bullet\right|\kern-0.75346pt\right|\kern-0.75346pt\right|}}\left(\frac{A_{\varepsilon}(q)}{\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)}\right)^{j}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\varepsilon t}A_{\varepsilon}(q)\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)\left\|D^{j}_{v}G_{t,q}\right\|_{{\left|\kern-0.75346pt\left|\kern-0.75346pt\left|\bullet\right|\kern-0.75346pt\right|\kern-0.75346pt\right|}}\left(\frac{A_{\varepsilon}(q)}{\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)}\right)^{j}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\varepsilon t}A_{\varepsilon}(q)^{j+1}\left\|D^{j}_{v}G_{t,q}\right\|_{{\left|\kern-0.75346pt\left|\kern-0.75346pt\left|\bullet\right|\kern-0.75346pt\right|\kern-0.75346pt\right|}}{\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)}^{-(j-1)}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\varepsilon t}A_{\varepsilon}(q)^{-j+3}\left\|D^{j}_{v}G_{t,q}\right\|_{{\left|\kern-0.75346pt\left|\kern-0.75346pt\left|\bullet\right|\kern-0.75346pt\right|\kern-0.75346pt\right|}}(2\varepsilon^{-1}\phi_{a}(q)^{a_{k}})^{-(j-1)}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon

and thus on the
\left\|\bullet\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}
-ball
B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;1)
,

(A.2.7)

\left\|D_{v}G_{t,q}-Dg_{t}(q)\right\|_{\left\|\bullet\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}}\leq\varepsilon\|v\|_{k,\varepsilon}

and

(A.2.8)

\left\|G_{t,q}-Dg_{t}(q)\right\|_{C^{k},\left\|\bullet\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}}\leq\varepsilon.

Set
\lambda_{0}=\max\{|\lambda_{i}|,1\}.

Take
r_{0}=e^{-2\lambda_{0}}
.
For all
0<\varepsilon<1/2
sufficiently small,
e^{\lambda_{0}+\varepsilon}+\varepsilon\leq e^{\lambda_{0}+2\varepsilon}
.
Thus for all
t\in[-1,1]
and
v\in TQ(q)
with
\left\|v\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}\leq r_{0}
, by (
A.2.7
),

\left\|G_{t,q}(v)\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}\leq(e^{\lambda_{0}+\varepsilon}+\varepsilon r_{0})\left\|v\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}\leq e^{\lambda_{0}+2\varepsilon}r_{0}<e^{-\lambda_{0}+2\varepsilon}<1.

Thus relative to
\left\|\bullet\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}
-norms, the image of ball
B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0})
is contained in the ball
B_{k,\varepsilon}(g_{t}q;1)
; combined with (
A.2.8
),
Proposition 2.2.4
(iv)
holds.
∎

A.2.9.
Lyapunov charts and distance

Let
y\in Q
be such that
Proposition 2.2.4
holds.
Let
