\mathcal{L}[y]=\exp_{y}(B_{k,\varepsilon}(y;r_{0}))
be the image of the ball of radius
r_{0}
in the metric
\left\|\bullet\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}
.
For
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{L}[y]
introduce the Lyapunov distance based at
y
via:

d^{\mathcal{L}}_{y}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\left\|\exp_{y}^{-1}(x_{1})-\exp_{y}^{-1}(x_{2})\right\|_{k,\varepsilon}

Note also that for any
\delta>0
, there exists a compact set
K
of measure at least
1-\delta
and
\kappa=\kappa(\delta)
such that if
y\in K
and then
d^{Q}
and
d^{\mathcal{L}}_{y}
are
\kappa
-biLipschitz in the Lyapunov chart at
y
.

A.2.10.
Unstable distance

We can apply
Proposition A.1.8
to
E:=TQ=TQ^{u}\oplus TQ^{cs}
and obtain a set
Q_{0}
of full measure, as well as its saturation by unstables
Q^{u}_{0}
, together with a norm
\left\|\bullet\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}
on
TQ|_{Q^{u}_{0}}
.

For
q\in Q_{0}^{u}
and
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{u}[q]
, denote by
d^{u}(q,q^{\prime})
the length of the shortest path measured with the metric
\left\|\bullet\right\|_{u,\varepsilon}
.
By construction we have that

(A.2.11)

\displaystyle d^{u}(g_{-t}q,g_{-t}q^{\prime})\leq e^{-(\lambda_{u}-\varepsilon)t}d^{u}(q,q^{\prime})\quad\forall t\geq 0.

where
\lambda_{u}>0
is the smallest strictly positive exponent.
Set
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[q]:=\{q^{\prime}\colon d^{u}(q,q^{\prime})<1\}
and we drop the
\varepsilon
-dependence from the notation.

A.3.
Stable manifolds

A.3.1.
Local and global stable manifolds

For
q\in Q
we define the
global stable set of
q
as

\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]=\{q^{\prime}\colon\exists\varepsilon=\varepsilon(q^{\prime})>0\text{ such that }e^{\varepsilon t}d(g_{t}q,g_{t}q^{\prime})\xrightarrow{t\to+\infty}0\}

Assume now that
(Q,g_{t},\nu)
is measurably good smooth dynamical
system, i.e. satisfying
Definition 2.2.6
.

We write

\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\dots>\lambda_{r}>0\geq\lambda_{r+1}>\dots>\lambda_{\ell}

for the distinct Lyapunov exponents of
g_{t}
with respect to
\nu
, listed without multiplicity.
Let

\lambda_{s}:=\max\{|\lambda_{i}|:\lambda_{i}<0\}.

A.3.2 Proposition

(Local stable manifolds relative to Lyapunov charts)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.
Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{k,\varepsilon}(q)
denote the path connected component of

\exp_{q}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]\right)\cap B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0})

containing
0\in TQ(q)
.
Then there exists
C_{\varepsilon,k}>1
and for
\nu
-a.e.
q
, a
C^{\infty}
function
h_{q}\colon W^{s}(q)\to(W^{c}\oplus W^{u})(q)
such that following hold:

(i)

h_{q}(0)=0
.

(ii)

\|D_{0}h_{q}\|=0
and
\|D_{v}h_{q}\|\leq\frac{1}{3}
for all
v
.

(iii)

\|h_{q}\|_{C^{k}}\leq C_{\varepsilon,k}

(iv)

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{k,\varepsilon}(q)=\mathrm{graph}\left(h_{q}\right)\cap B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0})

Moreover, relative to any measurable frame identifying
W^{s}(q)
and
(W^{0}\oplus W^{u})(q)
with
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\dim(Q)-d}
,

(v)

the map
(Q,\nu)\to C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{\dim(Q)-d})
,

q\mapsto h_{q}

is measurable.

Set
\mathcal{W}^{s}_{k,\varepsilon}[q]:=\exp_{q}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{k,\varepsilon}(q)\right)
.
Then, following notation in
Proposition 2.2.4

(vi)

We have

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{k,\varepsilon}(q)=\left\{v\in B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0}):\limsup_{t\to\infty}\tfrac{1}{t}\log\left\|\exp_{g_{t}q}^{-1}\circ g_{t}\circ\exp_{q}(v)\right\|<0\right\}.

(vii)

For all
v_{1},v_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{k,\varepsilon}(q)
and
t\geq 0
,

\left\|\exp_{g_{t}q}^{-1}\circ g_{t}\circ\exp_{q}(v_{1})-\exp_{g_{t}q}^{-1}\circ g_{t}\circ\exp_{q}(v_{2})\right\|\leq e^{-\lambda_{s}t+2\varepsilon t}

(viii)

For all
x,y\in\mathcal{W}^{s}_{k,\varepsilon}[q]
,

d^{Q}(g_{t}x,g_{t}y)\leq\phi_{k,\varepsilon}(q)e^{-\lambda_{s}t+2\varepsilon t}d^{Q}(x,y).

(ix)

For all
t\geq 0
, we have

g_{-t}\left(\mathcal{W}^{s}_{k,\varepsilon}[q]\right)\supset\mathcal{W}^{s}_{k,\varepsilon}[g_{-t}q].

For
\nu
-a.e.
q
we have

\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]=\bigcup_{t\geq 0}g_{-t}\left(\mathcal{W}^{s}_{k,\varepsilon}[g_{t}q]\right).

In particular, for
\nu
-a.e.
q
, the set
\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
is an injectively immersed
C^{\infty}
submanifold tangent to
W^{s}(q)
.

Proof of
Proposition A.3.2
.

This is standard and can be found, for instance, in
[
BP07
, Ch. 7]
.
∎

A.3.3 Remark
.

We remark that given a.e.
z\in Q
,
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[z]
is a pre-compact subset of
\mathcal{W}^{u}[z]
.
It follows from the construction of global (un)stable manifolds that there is a tempered
T_{0}(z)>0
such that for all
t\geq T_{0}(z)
,

g_{-t}\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[z]\subset\mathcal{L}_{k,\varepsilon}[g_{-t}z]

where
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[z]
is defined in
§A.2.10
.

A.4.
Center-stable manifolds

A.4.1.
Summary of constructions

We recall that the formal jet of the center-stable manifold is well-defined and equivariant under the dynamics, but there does not exist a uniquely-defined “center-stable” manifold (or set) which is also equivariant for the dynamics.
Analogously, for a smooth and natural cocycle
E
, or more generally for a forward-dynamically defined one (see
Definition E.1.9
), the formal jets of the forward Oseledets filtrations are also well-defined and equivariant.
For our applications, we will use two workarounds for this issue:

Realized Center-Stables:

We will fix, once and for all, a
C^{\infty}
manifold realizing the jet of the center-stable manifold denoted
\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{loc}[q]
.
For cocycles we will also fix a
C^{\infty}
realization
E^{\leq\lambda_{i}}_{q}(q^{\prime})
for
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{loc}[q]
which varies smoothly in
q^{\prime}
.

Fake Center-Stables:

For each degree of regularity
k
we will construct a family
\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}[q]
with better equivariance properties and controlled norms.
For cocycles we will also construct a
C^{k}
realization
E^{\leq\lambda_{i}}_{fake,k,q}(q^{\prime})
for
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}[q]
which varies
C^{k}
in
q^{\prime}
.

Coherence:

The manifold
\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}[q]
is tangent to order
k
to
\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{loc}[q]
, and both contain the stable manifold
\mathcal{W}^{s}_{loc}[q]
(in the neighborhood where all are defined).

For the following constructions, recall that
B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r)
denotes the ball of radius
r
in
TQ(q)
in the
(k,\varepsilon)
-Lyapunov norm, see
Proposition 2.2.4
.

A.4.2.
Fake center-stable manifolds

Fix an integer
k\geq 2
and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small depending on
k
and the Lyapunov spectrum.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{k,\varepsilon}[q]\subset Q
denote the Lyapunov chart at
q
.
Using a standard construction with bump functions, define
\widetilde{g}_{t}^{(k)}\colon TQ\to TQ
such that for
t\in[-1,1]
we have:

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{t}^{(k)}\colon T_{q}Q

\displaystyle\to T_{g_{t}q}Q

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{t}^{(k)}(v)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}Dg_{t}(v)&\text{ if }v\notin B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0}/2)\\
\exp_{g_{t}q}^{-1}\circ g_{t}\circ\exp_{q}(v)&\text{ if }v\in B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0}/4)\end{cases}

where
r_{0}
is defined in
Proposition 2.2.4
.
With this fixed globalization, it follows from
[
PS89
, Thm. 6.6]
that there exists a unique family of maps and manifolds

\displaystyle\gamma^{cs}_{fake,k}\colon W^{cs}(q)

\displaystyle\to W^{u}(q)

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}(q)

\displaystyle:=\left\{(v,\gamma^{cs}_{fake,k}(v))\colon v\in W^{cs}(q)\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}[q]

\displaystyle:=\exp_{q}\left(\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}(q)\cap B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0})\right)

such that
w\in\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}
if and only if
e^{-\varepsilon t}\widetilde{g}_{t}^{(k)}w\xrightarrow{t\to+\infty}0
.
In particular, it follows that

(i)

We have

\widetilde{g}_{t}^{(k)}\left(\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}(q)\right)=\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}\left(g_{t}q\right)\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}

(ii)

We have

g_{t}\left(\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}[q]\cap\exp_{q}\left(B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;e^{-\varepsilon t})\right)\right)\subset\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}[g_{t}q]\quad\forall t\geq 0.

A.4.3.
Fake cocycle flags along center-stables

Let
E\to Q
be a smooth and natural cocycle (see
Definition E.1.2
), and fix an integer
k\geq 2
and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small depending on
k
and the Lyapunov spectrum of
TQ
and
E
.
Consider the vector bundle

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}:=TQ\times_{Q}E:=\{(q,v,e)\colon q\in Q,v\in TQ(q),e\in E(q)\}\to TQ

We defined in
§A.4.2
a flow
\widetilde{g}_{t}^{(k)}
on
TQ
, and we now extend this to a cocycle on
\widetilde{E}
.
To avoid confusion, we will write
g_{t}^{E}
for the cocycle on
E
and
\widetilde{g}_{t}^{k,\widetilde{E}}
for the cocycle on
\widetilde{E}
.

First, recall that we have trivializations

E(q)\times B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0})\xrightarrow{\chi^{E}_{k,\varepsilon}(q)}E|_{\mathcal{L}_{k,\varepsilon}[q]}

which cover the map
\exp_{q}\colon B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0})\to\mathcal{L}_{k,\varepsilon}[q]
.
Using a bump function, define for
e\in\widetilde{E}(q,v)
with
(q,v)\in TQ
and
t\in[-1,1]
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{t}^{k,\widetilde{E}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}g_{t}^{E}(q)(e)&\text{ if }v\notin B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0}/2)\\
g_{t}^{E}(\exp_{q}(v))\left(\chi^{E}_{k,\varepsilon}(q)(v,e)\right)&\text{ if }v\in B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0}/4)\end{cases}

Using
[
PS89
, Thm. 6.3]
or
[
Rue79
, Rmk. 5.2(b)]
we obtain a
\widetilde{g}_{t}^{k,\widetilde{E}}
-equivariant filtration by order of growth
\widetilde{E}^{\leq\bullet}_{fake,k}(q,v)
which is
C^{k}
along
\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}
and we set

E^{\leq\bullet}_{fake,k,q}(q^{\prime}):=\chi_{k,\varepsilon}^{E}\left(q,\exp_{q}^{-1}(q^{\prime})\right)\left(\widetilde{E}^{\leq\bullet}_{fake,k}\left(q,\exp_{q}^{-1}(q^{\prime})\right)\right)

for
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{k,\varepsilon}[q]
.

A.4.4 Proposition

(Properties of center-stable jets)
.

There exists a set of full measure
Q_{0}
such that all
q,q_{1},q_{2}\in Q_{0}
we have:

(i)

The
k
-jet of
\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k+1}(q)
agrees with that of
\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}(q)
and is
g_{t}
-equivariant.

(ii)

Suppose
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}_{loc}[q_{1}]=\mathcal{W}^{s}_{loc}[q_{2}]
.
Then the formal
k
-jets of
\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}[q_{1}]
and
\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}[q_{2}]
agree at
q^{\prime}
.

(iii)

The analogous statements hold for
\widetilde{E}^{\leq\bullet}_{fake,\bullet}
.

Proof.

By construction
\widetilde{g}_{t}^{(k^{\prime})}
agrees with
\widetilde{g}_{t}^{(k)}
in a neighborhood of the zero section.
Recall also that for fixed
(k,\varepsilon)
the center-stable manifold
\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}(q)
is unique and can be obtained by iterating the dynamics.
Therefore, one can obtain
\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}(q)
as the limit of
\widetilde{g}_{-t}^{(k)}\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k+1}\left(g_{t}q\right)
as
t\to+\infty
and the first claim follows.
Note that equivariance of the flags follows from their construction and uniqueness.

For the second claim, it suffices to prove it if
q_{2}
is sufficiently close to
q_{1}
, depending on the
(k,\varepsilon)
-Lyapunov radius at
q_{1}
and then use equivariance under the dynamics.
But if
q_{2},q_{1}
are sufficiently close, the maps
\widetilde{g}_{t}^{(k)}(q_{1})
and
\widetilde{g}_{t}^{(k)}(q_{2})
are conjugated in a sufficiently small neighborhood containing the origin (using
\exp_{q_{1}}\circ\exp_{q_{2}}^{-1}
) and again it follows that
\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}(q_{2})
can be obtained by iterating
\exp_{q_{2}}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}[q_{1}]\right)
.

The proof of the last claim is analogous to the preceding two.
∎

A.4.5.
Realized Center-Stable Manifolds

We fix, once and for all, a measurable family
q\mapsto\widetilde{W}^{cs}(q)\subset TQ(q)
of embedded,
C^{\infty}
submanifolds that have the same formal jet at the origin as the family
\widetilde{W}^{cs}_{fake,k}
as
k\to+\infty
.
We realize these manifolds as graphs of smooth functions
\widetilde{\phi}_{q}\colon W^{cs}(q)\to W^{u}(q)
.

Denote by
\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{loc}[q]:=\exp_{q}\left(\widetilde{W}^{cs}(q)\cap TQ(q;r_{k,\varepsilon}(q))\right)
where the
(k,\varepsilon)
are fixed in
§6.3.9
.
Note that for any
k\geq 2
and sufficiently small
\varepsilon,\delta>0
there exist a compact set
K
of measure at least
1-\delta
and a constant
A(\delta,k,\varepsilon)
such that
\left\|\widetilde{\phi}_{q}\right\|_{C^{k}(B_{k,\varepsilon}(q;r_{0}))}\leq A(\delta,k,\varepsilon)
.

A.4.6.
Realized Center-Stable Oseledets filtrations

Let now
E\to Q
be a smooth and natural cocycle.
We fix once and for all a measurable family of
C^{\infty}
sections
q^{\prime}\mapsto E^{\leq\bullet}_{q}(q^{\prime})
from
\mathcal{W}^{cs}_{loc}[q]
into the bundle of all flags in
E

such that given
k\in\mathbb{N}
the map
q^{\prime}\mapsto E^{\leq\bullet}_{q}(q^{\prime})
is
C^{k}
tangent at
q
to
q^{\prime}\mapsto E_{fake,k,q}^{\leq\bullet}(q^{\prime})
as above.
The analogous remark about
C^{k}
bounds on these maps apply.

A.5.
Measurable partitions

In this section we discuss some general constructions concerning measurable partitions.

A.5.1.
Some generalities on partitions

We refer to
[
EL10
, §5-7]
for background on the following constructions.
We mainly consider partitions that are measurable (i.e. generated by countably many Borel sets) and denote the atom of a partition
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
containing a point
x
by
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]
.
A probability measure
\nu
then has conditional measures on each atom, denoted
\nu_{\mathfrak{B}}[x]
.
Recall also that a partition is subordinated to another if every atom of the first is contained in an atom of the second.

We work in the setting of a flow
g_{t}
on a manifold
Q
, preserving an ergodic probability measure
\nu
, with
\mathcal{W}^{u}[q]
denoting the unstable manifold through
q
(when it exists).
For a measurable partition
\mathfrak{B}
we will use the shorthand:

\mathfrak{B}_{t}[q]:=g_{-t}\mathfrak{B}[g_{t}q]

so in particular
\mathfrak{B}_{0}=\mathfrak{B}
.

A.5.2 Definition

(Unstable Markov partition)
.

A measurable partition
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
of
Q
is called a
unstable Markov partition
if it is subordinated to the partition into unstable manifolds,
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]
is relatively open and relatively compact in
\mathcal{W}^{u}[q]
, and furthermore for a set of
q
of full measure we have:

•

We have
\mathfrak{B}_{t}[q]\subset\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]
for all
t\geq 0
.

•

We have that
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[q]
, where
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[q]
is the ball of radius
1
around
q
in the
d^{u}
-distance (see
§A.2.10
).

We note that since
h_{\nu}(g_{1})<\infty
, we have
h(g_{1},\mathfrak{B})=h(g_{-1}\mathfrak{B}|\mathfrak{B})\leq h_{\nu}(g_{1})<\infty
for any measurable partition
\mathfrak{B}
with the Markov property
\mathfrak{B}_{t}=g_{-t}\mathfrak{B}\prec\mathfrak{B}
for
t\geq 0
.
Unstable Markov partitions
\mathfrak{B}
have the further property that they cary all the entropy of the flow
g_{t}
.

A.5.3 Proposition
.

For any unstable Markov partition
\mathfrak{B}
as in
Definition A.5.2
,

h_{\nu}(g_{1})=h(g_{1},\mathfrak{B}):=h(\mathfrak{B}_{1}|\mathfrak{B})=h(g_{-1}\mathfrak{B}|\mathfrak{B}).

The proof of
Proposition A.5.3
follows exactly as in the classical results
[
LY85a
]
after showing the dynamics admits good Lyapunov charts.

A.5.4.
Hausdorff distance properties of the partitions

Let
\mathcal{K}
denote the space of compact subsets of
Q
.
The Hausdorff distance coming from the ambient metric
d^{Q}(\cdot,\cdot)
on
Q
turns
\mathcal{K}
into a metric space.

The map
q\to\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]}
is measurable with respect to the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on the metric space
\mathcal{K}
.
Then, by Lusin’s theorem, for every
\delta>0
there
exists a compact set
K\subset Q
such that the map
q\to\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]}

is continuous on
K
.

A.5.5.
Measurable partitions and subgroups compatible with the measure

Assume that we have a family
U^{+}(q)
as defined in
Definition 3.1.5
, relative to an unstable Markov partition
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
.
We then define for
t\in\mathbb{R}
:

(A.5.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{t}[q]&:=\mathfrak{B}_{t}[q]\cap U^{+}[q]\\
\mathcal{B}_{t}(q)&:=\{u\in U^{+}(q)\colon uq\in\mathcal{B}_{t}[q]\}\end{split}

Thus
\mathcal{B}_{0}[q]
is a subset of
U^{+}[q]
, while
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]
is a subset of the unstable manifold
\mathcal{W}^{u}[q]
.
Note that
U^{+}[q]
admits a natural Haar measure up to a normalizing scalar, and therefore
\mathcal{B}_{0}[q]
admits a natural Haar probability measure.

We also use a Markov partition
\mathfrak{B}_{0}^{-}
of
Q
subordinate to the stables,
and pull it back to
X
as in §
2.5.6
.
For
x\in X
, let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{t}[x]

\displaystyle=g_{-t}(\mathfrak{B}_{0}[g_{t}x])\mathfrak{B}_{t}^{-}[x]

\displaystyle=g_{t}(\mathfrak{B}_{0}^{-}[g_{-t}x])

We note the following lemma for future reference:

A.5.7 Lemma
.

(a)

For
t^{\prime}>t\geq 0
,
\mathfrak{B}_{t^{\prime}}[x]\subset\mathfrak{B}_{t}[x]
.

(b)

Suppose
t\geq 0,t^{\prime}\geq 0
,
x\in X_{0}
and
x^{\prime}\in X_{0}
are such that

\mathfrak{B}_{t}[x]\cap\mathfrak{B}_{t^{\prime}}[x^{\prime}]\neq\emptyset
. Then either
\mathfrak{B}_{t}[x]\supseteq\mathfrak{B}_{t^{\prime}}[x^{\prime}]
or
\mathfrak{B}_{t^{\prime}}[x^{\prime}]\supseteq\mathfrak{B}_{t}[x]
(or
both).

For the next statement, recall (see
§A.2.10
) that
\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[q]
is the ball of radius
1
in the
d^{u}
-distance.

A.5.8 Lemma

(Partition adapted to a measurable function)
.

Suppose that
K
is a measurable set with
\nu(K)>0
and
T_{0}\colon K\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is a measurable function.

Then there exists an unstable Markov partition
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
with
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[q]
for a.e.
q
, and with the following additional properties.
We can pick a measurable subset
K_{0}\subset K
,
with the following properties:

•

For any two
q\neq q^{\prime}\in K_{0}
we have
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]\cap\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q^{\prime}]=\emptyset
.
Write
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[K_{0}]:=\bigsqcup_{q\in K_{0}}\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]
.

•

For a.e.
q\in Q
the set
\{t\colon g_{-t}q\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[K_{0}],t>0\}
is nonempty and has a smallest element
T_{r}(q)
(called the recurrence time of
q
).

•

For a.e
q
we have
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[g_{-t}q]=g_{-t}\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]
for
t\in[0,T_{r}(q))
.

•

For all
q\in K_{0}
the “recurrence time” satisfies
T_{r}(q)>T_{0}(q)
.

A.5.9.
Centers of atoms

Note that the function
T_{r}(q)
is constant on atoms of
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
.
It also follows that there exists
T_{r}^{-}(q)\geq 0
such that
g_{T_{r}^{-}(q)}q\in K_{0}
and
g_{t}q\notin K_{0}
for all
t\in[0,T_{r}^{-}(q))
.
It follows that for a full measure set of atoms
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]
, we can define its “center”
q^{*}\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]
as
q^{*}:=g_{-T_{r}^{-}(q)}q_{0}
where
q_{0}\in K_{0}
and
g_{T_{r}^{-}(q)}q\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q_{0}]
.
Note that for an atom intersecting
K_{0}
, its center is the corresponding point of
K_{0}
.

Proof of
Lemma A.5.8
.

The proof is entirely parallel to
[
EM18
, Prop. 3.7]
, see also
[
MT94
, §9]
.
∎

A.5.10 Remark

(Choice of measurable partition)
.

We will construct
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
such that
Proposition B.4.1
holds, with the function
T_{0}
chosen there.
We record here as well that by
Lemma A.5.8
the atoms satisfy
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}_{loc}[q]
.

A.6.
Conditional measures along (un)stable manifolds

A.6.1 Definition

(The measures
\nu_{\mathfrak{B}_{0}}[x]
)
.

We now define
\nu_{\mathfrak{B}_{0}}[x]

to be the conditional measure
of
\nu
along the measurable partition

\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]
. In other words,
\nu_{\mathfrak{B}_{0}}[x]

is the measure on
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]
defined so that for any measurable

\phi:X\to\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E}(\phi\mid\mathfrak{B}_{0})(x)=\int_{X}\phi\,d\nu_{\mathfrak{B}_{0}}[x].

A.6.2 Definition

(The measures
\nu^{u}_{x}
)
.

By
Lemma 11.1.29
, for almost every
x
and all
t\geq 0
we have

\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]\subset g_{t}\mathfrak{B}_{0}[g_{-t}x].

We define a locally finite Radon measure

\nu^{u}_{x}

on
\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
as follows: we declare
\nu^{u}_{x}(\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x])=1
and for any compact
K\subset\mathcal{W}^{u}[x]
, we take
t>0
so that
g_{-t}K\subset\mathfrak{B}_{0}[g_{-t}x]
and declare

\nu^{u}_{x}(K):=\frac{(g_{t})_{*}\nu_{\mathfrak{B}_{0}[g_{-t}x]}(K)}{(g_{t})_{*}\nu_{\mathfrak{B}_{0}[g_{-t}x]}(\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x])}.

The
g_{t}
-invariance of the measure
\nu
ensures that
\nu^{u}_{x}
is well-defined independent of the choice of

t>0
making
g_{-t}K\subset\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x].

Similarly, we get locally finite radon measures
\nu^{s}_{x}
along
W^{s}[x]
.

The following is used repeatedly:

A.6.3 Lemma

(Points of density)
.

Suppose
\delta>0
and
K\subset X_{0}
is such that

\nu(K)>1-\delta
. Then there exists a subset
K^{*}\subset K
with
\nu(K^{*})>1-\delta^{1/2}
such that for any
x\in K^{*}
,
and any
t>0
,

\nu^{u}_{x}(K\cap\mathfrak{B}_{t}[x])\geq(1-\delta^{1/2})\nu^{u}_{x}(\mathfrak{B}_{t}[x]).

Proof.

Let
E=K^{c}
, so
\nu(E)\leq\delta
. Let
E^{*}

denote the set of
x\in X_{0}
such that there exists some
\tau\geq 0
with

(A.6.4)

\nu^{u}_{x}(E\cap\mathfrak{B}_{\tau}[x])\geq\delta^{1/2}\nu^{u}_{x}(\mathfrak{B}_{\tau}[x]).

It is enough to show that
\nu(E^{*})\leq\delta^{1/2}
.
Let
\tau(x)
be the smallest
\tau>0
so that (
A.6.4
)
holds for
x
. Then the (distinct) sets
\{\mathfrak{B}_{\tau(x)}[x]\}_{x\in E^{*}}

cover
E^{*}
and are pairwise disjoint by

Lemma A.5.7
(b). Let

F=\bigcup_{x\in E^{*}}\mathfrak{B}_{\tau(x)}[x].

Then
E^{*}\subset F
. For every set of the form
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[y]
, let

\Delta(y)
denote the set of distinct sets
\mathfrak{B}_{\tau(x)}[x]
where

x
varies over
\mathfrak{B}_{0}[y]
. Then, by (
A.6.4
)

\nu^{u}_{y}(F\cap\mathfrak{B}_{0}[y])=\sum_{\Delta(y)}\nu^{u}_{y}(\mathfrak{B}_{\tau(x)})\leq\\
\leq\delta^{-1/2}\sum_{\Delta(y)}\nu^{u}_{y}(E\cap\mathfrak{B}_{\tau(x)}[x])\leq\delta^{-1/2}\nu^{u}_{y}(E\cap\mathfrak{B}_{0}[y]).

Integrating over
y
, we get
\nu(F)\leq\delta^{-1/2}\nu(E)
. Hence,

\nu(E^{*})\leq\nu(F)\leq\delta^{-1/2}\nu(E)\leq\delta^{1/2}.

∎

A.6.5.
The measure
|\cdot|
.

Recall that
\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]=\mathfrak{B}_{0}[x]\cap U^{+}[x]
.
Since
U^{+}(x)\subset\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(x)
is unipotent, it is
unimodular, and the same holds for
U^{+}_{x}(x)
which is the stabilizer
of
x
in
U^{+}(x)
). Therefore, we can define
“Lebesgue measure”
|\cdot|
on
\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]
as
the pushforward of the Haar measure on
U^{+}(q)/U^{+}_{q}(q)
to
\mathcal{B}_{0}[q]

under the action map
u\to uq
. (The measure
|\cdot|
is only
defined up to normalization).

The same argument as
Lemma A.6.3

also proves the following:

A.6.6 Lemma
.

Suppose
\delta>0
,
\theta^{\prime}>0
and
K\subset X
, with

\nu(K)>1-\delta
.
Then there exists a subset
K^{*}\subset K
with
\nu(K^{*})>1-\delta/\theta^{\prime}

such that for any
x\in K^{*}
, and any
t>0
,

|K\cap\mathcal{B}_{t}[x]|\geq(1-\theta^{\prime})|\mathcal{B}_{t}[x]|,

and thus

|\{u\in\mathcal{B}_{t}(x)\;\>:\;\>ux\in K\}|\geq(1-\theta^{\prime})|\mathcal{B}_{t}(x)|.

Appendix B
Generalities on cocycles

We will work with a number of flavors of cocycles.
We enumerate them here and outline the basic logical structure in their definitions, although some of the flavors below are not introduced until later sections.
We will consider:

Smooth and Natural:

Start with the tangent, and more generally higher-jet cocycles on
Q
, and their natural morphisms.
Then standard linear-algebraic operations (quotients, duals, tensors) lead to the class of smooth and natural cocycles.
These admit smooth trivializations and are defined everywhere on
Q
.
See
Definition E.1.2
.

Fdd and Bdd:

Forward dynamically defined (fdd) cocycles are those cocycles that arise once we introduce the subcocycles of the smooth and natural that are given by stable Lyapunov filtrations, and again allow for standard linear-algebraic operations.
These cocycles are defined only along a subset of
Q
saturated by stable manifolds, and vary smoothly along stable manifolds.
See
Definition E.1.9
.
The bdd (backward dynamically defined) cocycles are the analogues along unstables.

Admissible:

These are the weakest notion, with only a measurable isomorphism to a measurable subcocycle of a smooth and natural one.
See
Definition B.2.5
.

B.1.
Algebraic hulls

B.1.1.
Setup

We will make use of the following basic principles in this section.
Given a fixed vector space
V
, an algebraic subgroup
H\subset\GL(V)
can be defined by specifying a list of subspaces (or vectors) that
H
fixes in some tensor constructions applied to
V
.
Conversely, a theorem of Chevalley says that any algebraic subgroup
H\subset\GL(V)
can be defined as the stabilizer of some line in some tensor construction on
V
.

Given two vector spaces
V_{1},V_{2}
, it is not possible to say that a linear isomorphism
V_{1}\to V_{2}
is in a specific group.
It is possible, however, to say that the linear isomorphism is a bijection on the specific list of subspaces or tensors that determine the corresponding groups for
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
.
Using this point of view, it is frequently possible and convenient to avoid measurable trivializations of cocycles and keep more accurate track of the regularity of various maps involved.

B.1.2.
Trivializations and principal bundles

Suppose that
E\to Q
is a vector bundle and
V
is a fixed vector space (of the same dimension as the fibers of
E
).
Instead of globally trivializing the bundle
E
(in some measurable way, for instance) it is useful to consider the principal bundle of frames
P_{E}
with fiber given by
P_{E}(q)=\Isom(V,E(q))
, where
\Isom
denotes linear isomorphisms.
The group
\GL(V)
acts on the right by precomposing the framings and the action is transitive on the fibers.
If
E
had a symplectic structure or symmetric pairing, we could consider isomorphism that preserve this extra structure (and consider a group smaller than
\GL(V)
).
If the bundle
E
is a cocycle for the (left) group action of
A
on
Q
, then
P_{E}
inherits this left group action, which commutes with the right action of
\GL(V)
.

Suppose now that we have a subgroup
H\subset\GL(V)
.
Then a
reduction of the structure group
of the bundle from
\GL(V)
to
H
is a choice of a principal
H
-subbundle
P_{H}\subset P_{E}
, which again can be measurable, continuous, smooth, etc.
Equivalently, a reduction of the structure group to
H
is a section of the bundle
P_{E}/H
.
This is a
reduction of the cocycle
if the principal bundle is
A
-invariant, i.e.
a(P_{H}(q))=P_{H}(aq),\forall a\in A
and appropriate conditions on
q
, such as
\mu
-a.e. or all.
For instance, if
E
carried a symplectic form and
V
was symplectic, then a reduction to
H=\Sp(V)\subset\GL(V)
would be given by those linear isomorphisms
V\to E(q)
which respect the symplectic pairing.

Finally, suppose we have a principal
H
-bundle
P\to Q
, equipped with a left action of
A
lifted from the base
Q
(and commuting with the right action of
H
).
For any space
F
with a left
H
-action we can form

F_{P}=P\times_{H}F:=\left.\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$P\times F$}\middle/\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$(p,f)\sim(ph,h^{-1}f)$}\right.

which is now an
F
-bundle over
Q
, with a natural left
A
-action.
The bundles of flags in the fibers of a linear bundle, spaces of measures, etc. can be obtained this way (though the more direct standard constructions are also straightforward).

Note that the fibers of
F_{P}
are not canonically identified with
F
, the identification depending on the choice of point in the fiber of
P
.
Any two identifications differ by an element of
H
, and after such identifications the action maps
F_{P}(q)\to F_{P}{(aq)}
are in
H
(a statement which is independent of the choice of identifications).
Essential for later use will be the natural map

(B.1.3)

\displaystyle F_{P}\xrightarrow{\pi_{F}}\left.\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$H$}\middle\backslash\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$F$}\right.

which takes a point in the fiber to its equivalence class under the action of
H
.
By construction the map is “constant on
H
-orbits”, i.e. choosing an identification of the fiber
F_{P}(q)\tilde{\longrightarrow}F
(or equivalently a basepoint in the fiber of
P
) gives a (right) action of
H
on the fiber and for any
h\in H,f\in F_{P}(q)
we have
\pi_{F}(hf)=\pi_{F}(f)
.

An important consequence is that the map is constant on the orbits of the dynamics, i.e.

(B.1.4)

\displaystyle\pi_{F}(af)=\pi_{F}(f)\text{ for any }a\in A.

Note that to define the map
\pi_{F}
constructed in
Eqn. (B.1.3)
, we could have avoided the introduction of principal bundles, etc. and simply use local trivializations and check that the map is independent of such trivializations.

B.1.5.
Separability of orbit space

The fundamental fact that will be used frequently is the following (see
[
Zim84
, Thm. 3.1.3]
).
When
H
is the real points of an algebraic group, and
F
is the real points of an algebraic variety with an algebraic action of the group corresponding to
H
, the orbits of
H
on
F
are locally closed in the usual (analytic) topology.
In particular the quotient space
\left.\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$H$}\middle\backslash\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$F$}\right.
satisfies the separability axiom
T_{0}
, i.e. for any two points, there exists an open set containing one but not the other.

B.1.6.
Algebraic hull of cocycle

For more details on this see
[
EFW18
, §2]
;

[
Zim84
, §4.2]
contains another discussion of the algebraic hull and
[
EFW18
, Appendix B]
shows the notion below coincides with Zimmer’s.

Continuing with the cocycle
E\to Q
over the group action of
A
, assume that there is an
A
-invariant and ergodic measure
\mu
on
Q
.
We will define now a subgroup
Hull(E,A)(q)\subset\GL(E(q))
for
\mu
-a.e.
q
, the algebraic hull of
E
for the action of
A
.
The subgroups agree up to conjugation after an identification of the fibers
E(q)\tilde{\longrightarrow}E(q^{\prime})
.
Given the fixed reference vector space
V
, the algebraic hull can be viewed as a subgroup
H\subset\GL(V)
, well-defined only up to inner conjugation in
\GL(V)
.

Concretely, let
\mathbf{T}(E)
denote some tensor construction on
E
, e.g.
E^{\otimes n}
, or its dual.
An
A
-invariant section
s\colon Q\to\mathbf{T}(E)
is a measurable map such that
s(aq)=as(q)
for any
a\in A
and
\mu
-a.e.
q\in Q
(to obtain a set
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
of full
\mu
-measure on which the identity holds for every
a\in A
see
[
Zim84
, Prop B.5]
).
An
A
-invariant subbundle is defined analogously.
The
algebraic hull
is the subgroup of
\GL(E(q))
which fixes every
A
-invariant subbundle in every tensor representation
\mathbf{T}(E)
.
Note that the Noetherian property of algebraic sets implies that only finitely many tensors or subbundles need to be considered, and a theorem of Chevalley implies a single one suffices (again, see
[
EFW18
, §2]
for details and motivation).

It follows from the definitions that if
A_{1}\subset A
is a subgroup then
Hull(E,A_{1})\subset Hull(E,A)
.
We will be interested in algebraic hulls for the action of
g_{t}
, the group
P
, and all of
\SL_{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
.
We can also speak of measurable, continuous, real analytic, etc. algebraic hulls, depending on the regularity of the required tensors or subspaces.

B.1.7 Proposition

(Algebraic hull and reduction of structure)
.

Let
E\to(Q,\mu)
be an
A
-cocycle, with associated principal bundle
P
with fiber
P(q)=\Isom(V,E(q))
.

(i)

Suppose
H\subset\GL(V)
is an algebraic subgroup such that there exists an
A
-equivariant reduction of the structure group of
E
to
H
, i.e. there exists an
A
-equivariant section
\sigma\colon Q\to P/H
.
Then there exists an
A
-equivariant family of subgroups
H(q)\subset\GL(E(q))
, such that for any
H
-invariant subspace in a tensor construction
S\subset\mathbf{T}(V)
there is an associated
A
-equivariant subbundle
S_{E}\subset\mathbf{T}(E)
such that the fibers
S_{E}(q)
are
H(q)
-invariant.

The dependence of
H(q)
and
S_{E}(q)
on
q
are at least as good as the dependence of
\sigma(q)
on
q
.

(ii)

Conversely, suppose
S_{E}\subset\mathbf{T}(E)
is an
A
-invariant subbundle in some tensor construction, with stabilizers
H(q)\subset\GL(E(q))
.
Then there exists a subspace
S\subset\mathbf{T}(V)
with stabilizer
H\subset\GL(V)
, and an
A
-equivariant reduction of the structure group of the cocycle from
\GL(V)
to
H
.

If
S_{E}
is a measurable subbundle, then the reduction can be chosen measurable and
\mu
-a.e.
If
S_{E}
is continuous or better, then the reduction can be chosen on an
A
-invariant open set of full
\mu
-measure with the same regularity as
S_{E}
.

(iii)

There exists a measurable isomorphism
V\times Q\to E
of the trivial vector bundle with
E
, such that the action of the cocycle is
\mu
-a.e. valued in
H\subset\GL(V)
and
H
is the algebraic hull of the trivialized cocycle.

Equivalently, if we pull back under the trivialization any subspace in a tensor construction on
V
which is invariant under
H
, the corresponding subbundle in the tensor construction on
E
is invariant under the
A
-dynamics.

B.1.8 Remark

(On trivializations of bundles)
.

A trivialization as in
Proposition B.1.7
(iii)
will be called a
trivialization adapted to the algebraic hull
.

The separability of the space of orbits of algebraic group actions, as stated in
§B.1.5
, is used only in part (ii) of
Proposition B.1.7
.
It is the mechanism that allows us to go from invariant subbundles to reductions of the structure group of the cocycle.

Proof of
Proposition B.1.7
.

For (i), suppose given the
A
-equivariant section
\sigma\colon Q\to P/H
.
Then the groups
H(q)
are defined as the stabilizers of
\sigma(q)
inside
\GL(E(q))
acting on
P(q)/H
.
Let also
P_{H}\subset P
denote the principal
H
-bundle.
Then given any tensor construction
\mathbf{T}(V)
with
H
-invariant subspace
S\subset\mathbf{T}(V)
, take the associated vector bundles
S_{E}:=P_{H}\times_{H}S\subset P_{H}\times_{H}\mathbf{T}(V)=\mathbf{T}(E)
to obtain an
A
-invariant subbundle.
The fibers
S_{E}(q)\subset\mathbf{T}(E(q))
will be stabilized by
H(q)\subset\GL(E(q))
.

For the converse construction (ii), given an
A
-invariant subbundle
S_{E}\subset\mathbf{T}(E)
, by the construction in
Eqn. (B.1.3)
the map sending the fiber
S_{E}(q)
to its equivalence class in
\left.\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{$\GL(V)$}\middle\backslash\raisebox{1.00006pt}{$\Gr(\mathbf{T}(V))$}\right.
(where
\Gr
denotes the appropriate Grassmannian) takes the same value
\mu
-a.e. or, in the case
S_{E}(q)
depends continuously on
q
, to the same value on an
A
-invariant open set of full measure.
Let in either case the set be denoted
U\subset Q
.

By construction there exists a corresponding subspace
S\subset\mathbf{T}(V)
such that the isomorphisms
V\to E(q)
taking
S_{E}(q)
to
S
are nonempty for every
q\in U
.
The left action of
H(q)
(the stabilizer of
S_{E}(q)
inside
\GL(E(q))
) on these isomorphisms is transitive, as is the right action of
H
(the stabilizer of
S
inside
\GL(V)
).
This family of isomorphisms
P_{H}(q)\subset P(q)
gives the desired reduction of the structure group of the cocycle to
H
.

Continuing to (iii), let
H(q)
be the algebraic hull of the cocycle
E
, and let
L\subset\mathbf{T}(E)
be the
A
-equivariant line bundle that defined
H
as its stabilizer (the existence of
L
is guaranteed by Chevalley’s theorem).
Let
l\subset V
be the line provided by the construction in part (ii).
Then the set of isomorphisms
V\to E(q)
taking
l
to
L(q)
is non-empty
\mu
-a.e., hence it has a measurable section (by standard measurable selection theorems).
This measurable section is the required choice of trivialization.
∎

B.1.9 Corollary

(Oseledets decompositions are conjugate)
.

Suppose
E\to(Q,\mu)
is a cocycle over
g_{t}
and
E=\oplus E^{\lambda_{i}}
is its decomposition into Lyapunov subspaces.
Then the algebraic hull
Hull(E,g_{t})
of
E
respects this decomposition.

On a set of full measure
Q_{0}
, for any
q,q^{\prime}\in Q_{0}
there exists a linear isomorphism
E(q)\to E(q^{\prime})
respecting the decomposition and also respecting any other
g_{t}
-invariant subspace in any other tensor construction on
E
.
Furthermore the isomorphisms can be chosen to vary measurably in both
q
and
q^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The first part follows from the definition of algebraic hull.
The second part follows by choosing a trivialization adapted to the algebraic hull to identify
E(q),E(q^{\prime})
to
V
, respecting all subspaces in all tensor powers that are invariant under the algebraic hull.
∎

B.2.
Holonomies and algebraic hulls

B.2.1.
Setup

The proof of existence of holonomies in the uniformly hyperbolic setting is in
[
ASV13
, §3]
.
We develop some additional properties and estimates in
Proposition E.2.19
.

B.2.2.
Holonomies

Continuing with the earlier notation, suppose in addition that we have stable manifolds
\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]\subset Q
for the action of a
1
-parameter subgroup
g_{t}
.
The essential property is that for
\mu
-a.e.
q
and
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
we have
\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\dist(g_{t}q,g_{t}q^{\prime})<0
.

We will say that the cocycle
E
has
stable holonomies
if there exists a set of full
\mu
-measure
Q_{0}
such that if
q,q^{\prime}\in Q_{0}
and
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
we have operators

\mathcal{P}^{-}(q,q^{\prime})\colon E(q)\to E(q^{\prime})\text{ with }\mathcal{P}^{-}(q,q^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{P}^{-}(q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime})\circ\mathcal{P}^{-}(q,q^{\prime})\text{ whenever defined}

and additionally

\mathcal{P}^{-}(g_{t}q,g_{t}q^{\prime})\circ g_{t}=g_{t}\circ\mathcal{P}^{-}(q,q^{\prime})\quad\text{ as maps }E(q)\to E(q^{\prime}).

We assume that
\mathcal{P}^{-}(q,q)
is the identity.
The stable holonomies
\mathcal{P}^{-}
will be called measurable, smooth, etc. according to the dependence of
\mathcal{P}(q,q^{\prime})
on
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
(provided the bundle
E
has at least the regularity needed to speak of this).

B.2.3.
Standard measurable connection, smooth case

If the cocycle
E
is smooth along the stable foliation
\mathcal{W}^{s}
, and it has a single Lyapunov exponent, then it has a standard stable holonomy which will be denoted
H^{-}
(see
§D.3
).

For a cocycle
E
which is smooth along the stable foliation, but does not necessarily have a single Lyapunov exponent, let
E=\oplus E^{\lambda_{i}}
be its decomposition into Lyapunov subspaces.
Then the bundles
E^{\leq\lambda_{i}}:=\oplus_{\lambda\leq\lambda_{i}}E^{\lambda}
vary smoothly on the stable
[
Rue79
, §5.2(b)]
and so do their quotients
E^{\leq\lambda_{i}}/E^{\leq\lambda_{i+1}}
which have a single Lyapunov exponent
\lambda_{i}
and therefore standard holonomies
H^{-}_{i}
.
These can be assembled into a
standard holonomy

H^{-}
on

\gr_{\bullet}E
, where
\gr_{\bullet}E
is defined in
§E.2.18
.
Using the splitting of
E
given by the Lyapunov subspaces, which gives us an isomorphism

E=\bigoplus_{\lambda_{i}}E^{\lambda_{i}}\tilde{\longrightarrow}\gr_{\bullet}^{-}E=\bigoplus_{\lambda_{i}}E^{\leq\lambda_{i}}/E^{\leq\lambda_{i+1}}

we define the
standard measurable connection of
E
to be
P^{-}:=\oplus P^{-}_{i}
, where each
P^{-}_{i}
are obtained from
H^{-}_{i}
by the above measurable isomorphism.
Note that the map
P^{-}(q,q^{\prime})
is only defined for
q,q^{\prime}
in a set of full measure
Q_{0}
such that moreover
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
.

The unstable measurable connection
P^{+}(q,q^{\prime})
is defined analogously, for a cocycle smooth along unstables and for
q,q^{\prime}
in a set of full measure
Q_{0}
such that
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{u}[q]
.

B.2.4 Theorem

(Ledrappier invariance principle)
.

Let
E\to Q
be a cocycle over
g_{t}
, smooth along stables, with standard measurable connection
P^{-}_{E}
.

(i)

If
S\subset E
is a measurable
g_{t}
-invariant subbundle, then
S
is preserved by
P^{-}_{E}
.

(ii)

If
\mathbf{T}(E)
denotes a tensor construction applied to
E
, then its standard measurable connection
P^{-}_{\mathbf{T}(E)}
agrees with the measurable connection induced from
P^{-}_{E}
by the tensor construction.

(iii)

If we pick a measurable trivialization
V\times Q\to E
adapted to the algebraic hull, then
P^{-}
is valued in the algebraic hull
H\subset\GL(V)
.

Proof.

Part (i) is the original Ledrappier invariance principle, see

[
Led86
,
AV10
,
EM18
]
.

Part (ii) follows because tensor constructions are compatible with the Oseledets decomposition, so in particular the cocycles
\gr_{\bullet}\mathbf{T}(E)
and
\mathbf{T}\left(\gr_{\bullet}E\right)
are isomorphic, via identifications with
\mathbf{T}(E)
.
It then follows that the induced
P^{-}
maps also agree.

Part (iii) follows from parts (i) and (ii) applied to the
g_{t}
-invariant subbundle
L
in a tensor construction
\mathbf{T}(E)
defining the algebraic hull. ∎

We can enlarge to the class of cocycles to which the above constructions and results apply.
For the next definition, we recall that smooth and natural cocycles are defined in
Definition E.1.2
; these cocycles are derived from the tangent and higher jet cocycles by elementary linear algebra operations.

B.2.5 Definition

(Admissible cocycles)
.

A measurable cocycle
E\to Q
is
admissible
if there is a smooth and natural cocycle
Big(E)
, called the
smooth envelope of
E
, two
g_{t}
-equivariant measurable subcocycles

S_{1}\subset S_{2}\subset Big(E)

and a measurable isometry
E\simeq S_{2}/S_{1}
.

B.2.6.
Standard measurable connection

Suppose now that
E\to Q
is admissible and
E\cong S_{2}/S_{1}
with
S_{i}\subset Big(E)
.
By
Theorem B.2.4
, we have that
P^{-}_{Big(E)}
preserves
S_{1},S_{2}
hence induces a measurable connection
P^{-}_{E}
on
E
.

With this construction, we observe that
Theorem B.2.4
extends to an arbitrary admissible cocycle:

B.2.7 Corollary

(Ledrappier Invariance for admissible cocycles)
.

For any admissible cocycle
E
, the conclusions of
Theorem B.2.4
hold.

The result follows from the original one using
Theorem B.2.4
(i)
.

B.2.8.
Admissible metrics on admissible cocycles

Given any cocycle
F
with a metric to which the Oseledets theorem applies, we can define a new metric on
F
which agrees with the old metric on each Lyapunov subspace, and for which the Lyapunov decomposition is orthogonal.
As a consequence of the Oseledets theorem, there exist tempered
(see
Definition A.1.2
) functions such that the two metrics are comparable up to multiplicative scalars provided by the functions (in one direction the constant
\dim F
suffices).

We call metrics
F
obtained on our cocycles by the above procedure
admissible metrics
.
Note that even if
F
is smooth, the admissible metrics are not in general smooth.
For the class of admissible metrics, given an admissible cocycle
F
and its smooth envelope
E
, there exists an isometric map
F\hookrightarrow E
which is not in general
g_{t}
-equivariant but which induces the identity on
\gr_{\lambda_{i}}F
and
S_{2}^{\lambda_{i}}/S_{1}^{\lambda_{i}}
where
\lambda_{i}
are Lyapunov exponents.

B.2.9.
Compatibility of holonomies and standard measurable connection

Suppose that
E
is an admissible cocycle, in particular it admits the standard measurable connection
P^{-}_{E}
constructed in
§B.2.6
above.
Suppose also that
E
carries holonomies
\mathcal{P}^{-}_{E}
on the stable manifolds, or some equivariant subsets of the stable manifolds.
Suppose that
\mathcal{P}^{-}_{E}
is Hölder-continuous on sets of almost full measure, i.e. there exists an exponent
\alpha>0
such that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a compact set
K_{\varepsilon}
of measure at least
1-\varepsilon
, and a constant
C_{\varepsilon}<+\infty
, such that
E
admits Hölder trivializations on charts covering
K
, and such that
\mathcal{P}^{-}_{E}(q,q^{\prime})
is Hölder-continuous on
K_{\varepsilon}
, with constant
C_{\varepsilon}
and exponent
\alpha
.

With these assumptions, it is a standard fact that
\mathcal{P}^{-}_{E}
preserves the Lyapunov flags on
E
along
\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
.
The measurable connection
P^{-}_{E}
does so as well, and the induced maps on the associated graded of
E
agree.
This last assertion is equivalent to the uniqueness of holonomies for cocycles with one Lyapunov exponent, and a Hölder structure on sets of almost full measure, which is a standard fact.

B.2.10.
Summary on holonomies, notation

Holonomy on graded:

If
E
is smooth along stables, then we have the holonomy maps
H^{-}(q,q^{\prime})
on the associated graded
\gr_{\bullet}E
.
These depend smoothly on
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
.

Measurable connection:

If
E
is any admissible cocycle, the measurable connections
P^{-}
are provided by Ledrappier’s
Corollary B.2.7
, and analogously for
P^{+}
.

Gauss–Manin:

If
E
is smooth along stables, then we have the flat connections
P^{-}_{GM}
on the linearization
L^{-}E
, provided by normal forms as in
Corollary D.3.6
.
Analogous statements hold for unstables and
P^{+}_{GM}
.

B.3.
Jordan Normal Forms for Cocycles

B.3.1.
Zimmer’s amenable reduction

The following general facts about linear cocycles over an action of
\mathbb{R}
are often called “Zimmer’s amenable reduction”.
We state a version adapted to our setup.

Suppose
E\to(Q,\nu)
is a measurable linear cocycle over the
g_{t}
-action.

B.3.2 Definition

(Jordan Normal Form of Cocycle)
.

The measurable linear cocycle
E\to(Q,\nu)

can be put in Jordan normal form
if there exists a filtration by subcocycles:

(B.3.3)

\{0\}=E_{0}(q)\subset E_{1}(q)\subset\dots\subset E_{p}(q)=E(q)

and a measurable family of metrics on the quotient cocycles
E_{i}/E_{i-1}
such that the induced action is by conformal transformations.

We call a filtration
\mathfrak{F}=E_{\bullet}
as in (
B.3.3
), with its metric, a
Jordan flag
and say the Jordan flag (
B.3.3
)
puts the cocycle in Jordan normal form
.
The
length
of a Jordan flag (
B.3.3
) is the number of subspaces
p
appearing the filtration in (
B.3.3
).

B.3.4 Proposition

(Jordan Normal Form and Standard Operations)
.

Suppose that
E\to(Q,\nu)
and
F\to(Q,\nu)
are measurable linear cocycles.

(i)

If
F
is a subcocycle of
E
and if
E
can be put in Jordan normal form, then
F
and
E/F
can be put in Jordan normal form.

(ii)

If
F
is a subcocycle of
E
and if both the quotient
E/F
and
F
can be put in Jordan normal form, then
E
can be put in Jordan normal form.

(iii)

If
E
and
F
can be put in Jordan normal form then the cocycle
E\otimes F\to(Q,\nu)
can be put in Jordan normal form.

Proof.

For (i), suppose
E
can be put in Jordan normal form, with subcocycles
E_{i}
.
Then certainly the subcocycles
E_{i}\cap F
put
F
in Jordan normal form, and their images in the quotient
E/F
will put
E/F
in Jordan normal form.

For (ii), suppose
F
and
E/F
can be put in Jordan normal form by
F_{i}
and
(E/F)_{j}
respectively.
Then we can use the sequence
F_{i}
, followed by the preimages of
(E/F)_{j}
, to put
E
in Jordan normal form.

Finally, for (iii), suppose that
E_{i},F_{j}
put
E,F
in Jordan Normal Form.
Choose any total ordering of the indexes
(i,j)
compatible with the partial order
(i,j)\prec(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
if
i\leq i^{\prime}
and
j\leq j^{\prime}
.
Define then
(E\otimes F)_{k}
to be the span of all the
E_{i}\otimes F_{j}
for the first
k
pairs of
(i,j)
in the selected total order (the compatibility with the partial order ensures that
(E\otimes F)_{k-1}\subset(E\otimes F)_{k}
).
Then the successive quotients of this filtration are of the form
(E_{i}/E_{i-1})\otimes(F_{j}/F_{j-1})
and so the filtration puts the cocycle in Jordan normal form.
∎

Given a cocycle
E\to(Q,\nu)
be a cocycle over a measurable flow and an equivariant finite measurable cover
(\hat{Q},\hat{\nu})\to(Q,\nu)
, we can always consider the pulled-back cocycle
E\to(\hat{Q},\hat{\nu})
on
\hat{Q}
.

B.3.5 Theorem

(Jordan Normal Form)
.

Given any cocycle
E\to(Q,\nu)
, there exists an equivariant finite measurable cover
\sigma\colon(\hat{Q},\hat{\nu})\to(Q,\nu)
and a filtration
\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}
of
E
that puts the cocycle in Jordan Normal Form, with the following additional properties:

(i)

The associated graded cocycle
\gr^{\mathfrak{F}}E
, and the metric that puts it in Jordan normal form, is pulled back from
(Q,\nu)
.

(ii)

The norm of the linear cocycle for the new metric is bounded above by the norm for the original metric.

(iii)

Suppose that
E
is an admissible cocycle.
Then for
\nu
-a.e.
q
and every biregular
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
, the (finite) set of Jordan flags
\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E(q)
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E(q^{\prime})
is intertwined by
P^{-}(q,q^{\prime})
.

For
\nu
-a.e.
q
and a Jordan flag
\mathfrak{F}
on
E(q)
we write
x=(q,\mathfrak{F})\in\hat{Q}
and
define the measurable set

\mathcal{W}^{s}[x]=\mathcal{W}^{s}[q,\mathfrak{F}]:=\{(q^{\prime},\mathfrak{F}^{\prime})\colon q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]\text{ and }\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}=P^{-}(q,q^{\prime})\mathfrak{F}\}\subset\hat{Q}.

Then the projection map identifies
\mathcal{W}^{s}[q,\mathfrak{F}]\tilde{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
.

(iv)

Suppose
E
is smooth along stable manifolds. Then for
\nu
-a.e.
q
and every
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
, there exists a finite collection of flags
\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E(q^{\prime})
with the same combinatorics as
\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E(q)
and the collection
q^{\prime}\mapsto\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E(q^{\prime})
varies smoothly along
\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
. Moreover, there exists a smooth family of linear maps
q^{\prime}\mapsto\widetilde{P}(q,q^{\prime})
such that
\widetilde{P}(q,q^{\prime})\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E(q)=\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E(q^{\prime})
and
\widetilde{P}(q,q^{\prime})
restricts to
P^{-}(q,q^{\prime})\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E(q)=\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E(q^{\prime})
if
q
and
q^{\prime}
are biregular.

Furthermore, for
\nu
-a.e.
q\in Q
and
\mathfrak{F}
a Jordan flag on
E(q)
, we define
x=(q,\mathfrak{F})\in\hat{Q}
and

\mathcal{W}^{s}[x]=\mathcal{W}^{s}[q,\mathfrak{F}]:=\{(q^{\prime},\mathfrak{F}^{\prime})\colon q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]\text{ and }\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}=\widetilde{P}(q,q^{\prime})\mathfrak{F}\}\subset\hat{Q}.

Then the projection map identifies
\mathcal{W}^{s}[q,\mathfrak{F}]\tilde{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
and if two elements belong to the same
\mathcal{W}^{s}[q,\mathfrak{F}]
, then they are exponentially asymptotic in forward time. Moreover, the restriction of
\mathcal{W}^{s}[q,\mathfrak{F}]
to fibers over biregular points
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{s}[q]
coincides with the measurable set in part
(iii)
.

(v)

Any other finite cover
(Q^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\to(Q,\nu)
on which
E
can be put in Jordan Normal Form by a filtration
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
factors via
(Q^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\to(\hat{Q},\hat{\nu})
, and the associated graded
\gr^{\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}}_{\bullet}E
refines the pullback of
\gr^{\mathfrak{F}}_{\bullet}E
.

This last property is universal and characterizes
(\hat{Q},\hat{\nu})
and the filtration
\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}
.

B.3.6.
Preliminary constructions for the proof of
Theorem B.3.5

Let
\mathbf{G}_{E}
denote the algebraic hull of the cocycle
E
for the
g_{t}
-action on
(Q,\nu)
.
Recall that this is an a.e. defined family of algebraic subgroups of
\GL(E(q))
, for
q
in a set of full
\nu
-measure.
Since the acting group, i.e.
\mathbb{R}
, is amenable, the algebraic hull is also amenable by Zimmer’s amenable reduction theorem
[
Zim84
, 9.2.3]
.
In particular, it has a unipotent radical
\mathbf{G}_{E}^{u}\subset\mathbf{G}_{E}
such that the quotient is a product of compact groups and split tori.
The last statement follows from
[
Zim84
, 4.1.9]
which characterizes amenable Lie groups as compact extensions of solvable Lie groups.

B.3.7.
Engel filtration

Any unipotent subgroup
\mathbf{G}_{E}^{u}(q)\subset\GL(E(q))
induces a canonical filtration of
E(q)
: the lowest term consists of vectors fixed by the full group (nonempty by Engel’s theorem), and subsequent terms of the filtration are defined inductively by taking the quotient by the preceding term.
Let us call this the
Engel filtration
which we denote by

\{0\}=V_{0}(q)\subsetneq V_{1}(q)\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq V_{\ell}(q)=E(q)

Let
\mathbf{G}_{E}^{\circ}
denote the connected component of the identity of
\mathbf{G}_{E}
, the algebraic hull of
E
.
We consider the image of
\mathbf{G}_{E}
in
\GL(V_{j+1}(q)/V_{j}(q))
of the Engel filtration and denote it by
\mathbf{G}_{j}
.
This is reductive, since it has no unipotent radical, and we denote by
\mathbf{G}_{j}^{\circ}
its Zariski-connected component of the identity.
The solvable radical
\mathbf{T}_{j}^{sol}\subset\mathbf{G}_{j}^{\circ}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-torus, which has a canonical
2
2

2

Tori over
\mathbb{R}
are equivalent to the data of their cocharacter lattice (obtained by extending scalars to
\mathbb{C}
) and an involution on it coming from complex conjugation; the split part corresponds to the sublattice fixed by the involution.
split subtorus
\mathbf{T}_{j}\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{j}^{sol}
.
Note that
\mathbf{T}_{j}
is a normal subgroup of
\mathbf{G}_{j}
and the quotient is compact.

B.3.8.
Zimmer flags

We now decompose
V_{j+1}/V_{j}
according to the characters
\mathbf{T}^{*}_{j}
of
\mathbf{T}_{j}
:

(B.3.9)

V_{j+1}/V_{j}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{T}^{*}_{j}}V_{j,\alpha}(q)

A
Zimmer flag
at
q
is any flag in
E(q)
comprised of subspace of the form

V_{j-1}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}V_{j,\alpha_{i}}(q)\quad k=0,1,\ldots,k_{j}

where
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k_{j}}
is allowed to be any enumeration of the nonvacuous characters.

B.3.10.
Constructing the conformal metrics

The group
\mathbf{G}_{j}
has finitely many Zariski-connected components denoted
F_{j}
, and a Zariski-connected component of the identity denoted
\mathbf{G}_{j}^{\circ}
.
Recall also that
\mathbf{T}_{j}
is normal and the quotient
\mathbf{G}_{j}/\mathbf{T}_{j}
is compact.

We now return to the decomposition in
Eqn. (B.3.9)
.
Note that the finite group
F_{j}
can permute the
V_{j,\alpha}
’s with different
\alpha
’s.
Let
M_{j}
denote the space of metrics on
V_{j+1}/V_{j}
for which the decomposition
\oplus V_{j,\alpha}
is orthogonal.
This is equipped with a natural metric making it a
\CAT(0)
-space.
Then the quotient
M_{j}/\mathbf{T}_{j}
is still such a space, isomorphic to
\prod\PSL(V_{j,\alpha})/K(V_{j,\alpha})
, where
K(V_{j,\alpha})
is a maximal compact of
\PSL(V_{j,\alpha})
.
Since the quotient group
\mathbf{G}_{j}/\mathbf{T}_{j}
is compact and acts on
M_{j}/\mathbf{T}_{j}
, so its fixed point set is a nonempty geodesically convex subset
M_{j,f}\subset M_{j}/\mathbf{T}_{j}
.

B.3.11.
Equivariance properties

The above constructions were canonical and apply to a set of full measure of the fibers of
E\to(Q,\nu)
.
We now consider what happens over the entire dynamical system, and indicate the dependence on
q\in Q
.

The algebraic hull acts trivially on
M_{j,f}(q)
by construction, so in fact there exists a single “model” space
M_{j,f}^{mod}
and a
g_{t}
-equivariant map
M_{j,f}(q)\to M_{j,f}^{mod}
, as in
Eqn. (B.1.3)
and as explained in the discussion preceding it.
Select one point
m_{j,f}\in M_{j,f}^{mod}
, let
m_{j,f}(q)\in M_{j,f}(q)
be the corresponding equivariant family of spaces, and its preimage
M_{j,c}(q)\subset M(q)
is also a geodesically convex subset on which the algebraic hull acts via
\mathbf{T}_{j}
.
The space
M_{j}(q)
is a geodesically convex subset of
M^{full}(V_{j+1}/V_{j}(q))
of all metrics on the fiber
V_{j+1}/V_{j}(q)
, and so is
M_{j,c}(q)
.
Let
\pi_{0}(q)\colon M^{full}(V_{j+1}/V_{j}(q))\to M_{j,c}(q)
be the nearest point projection.
Note that
\pi_{0}(q)
is distance nonincreasing, which will imply that the norms of the cocycle for the new metrics are not larger than for the original metrics.
All objects and maps defined so far are equivariant for the dynamics.

Now
V_{j+1}/V_{j}(q)
has its original cocycle metric
m_{V}(q)\in M^{full}(V(q))
, and we set
m_{c}(q):=\pi_{0}(q)(m_{V}(q))\in M_{j,c}(q)
to be its projection.
Then the action of the dynamics
g_{t}
takes
m_{c}(g_{-t}q)
to the same
\mathbf{T}^{s}
-orbit as
m_{c}(q)
, and hence the action is conformal on each
V_{\alpha}
.

B.3.12.
The proof of
Theorem B.3.5

The collection of flags
\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E
has been defined in
§B.3.8
.
This is a collection of flags on each
E(q)
with the same associates graded pieces, up to permutation.

We endow the associated graded with the metric constructed in
§B.3.10
.
The property that the dynamics is by conformal transformations, that the metric is induced already on
(Q,\nu)
, and the boundedness properties follow from the discussion in
§B.3.10
and
§B.3.11
.

The universality of the construction follows since by passing to a finite cover, the algebraic hull can only change by finite index, so any filtration of the cocycle on
(Q^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
will have to refine the Engel filtration and character pieces defined in
Eqn. (B.3.9)
.

The
P^{-}
-equivariance of the Zimmer flags follows from the Ledrappier Invariance Principle,
Corollary B.2.7
, applied to an appropriate tensor construction on
E
that has invariant subbundles corresponding to finite collections of Zimmer flags.
The smoothness of the flags
\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E
along stables follows from the fact that they refine the stable flags (which are smooth along stables), the fact that the holonomy on
\gr_{\bullet}E
(which is smooth along stables) agrees with
P^{-}
, and that by Ledrappier again
P^{-}
preserves
\mathfrak{F}_{\bullet}E
.
∎

B.3.13 Remark

(Points in the cover and flags)
.

Let
E=\oplus E^{\lambda_{i}}
be the Lyapunov splitting of the cocycle
E\to Q
.
A point in the finite cover
\hat{Q}\to Q
can be interpreted (equivalently) as the following additional data:

•

A flag in each Lyapunov subspace
E^{\lambda_{i}}
.

•

A flag of
E
, refining the stable flags
E^{\leq\lambda_{i}}
.

•

A flag of
E
, refining the unstable flags
E^{\geq\lambda_{i}}
.

Their associated graded pieces are naturally identified.
Each of these flags has additional properties listed in
Theorem B.3.5
, and the flag on the stable has smoothness properties on the stable, and analogously for the unstable.

B.4.
Good Norms

The next result is an analogue of
[
EM18
, Prop. 4.15]
and its proof is analogous.
Recall that the bundle
\mathbf{H}:=L\mathcal{C}/L\mathcal{C}^{\tau}
is introduced in
Eqn. (5.1.10)
.
We denote by
\mathbf{H}^{\lambda_{i}}_{j}
the flags on
\mathbf{H}^{\lambda_{i}}
obtained from
Theorem B.3.5
applied to
\mathbf{H}
.

B.4.1 Proposition

(Good norms)
.

On a set of full measure
X_{0}\subset X
there exists an inner product
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{q}
on
\mathbf{H}(q)
(or on any bundle for which the conclusions of
[
EM18
, Lemma 4.14]
hold) with the following properties:

(i)

For
q\in X_{0}
, the distinct Lyapunov spaces
\mathbf{H}^{\lambda_{i}}(q)
are orthogonal.

(ii)

Let
\mathbf{H}^{\lambda_{i}}_{j^{\prime}}
denote the orthogonal complement relative to the inner product
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle
of
\mathbf{H}^{\lambda_{i}}_{j-1}
in
\mathbf{H}^{\lambda_{i}}_{j}
.
Then for
q\in X_{0}
, any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and any
v\in\mathbf{H}^{\lambda_{i}}_{j^{\prime}}(q)
we have

g_{t}{\mathbf{v}}=e^{\lambda_{ij}(q;t)}{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}+{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime\prime}

where
\lambda_{ij}(q;t)\in\mathbb{R}
,
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H}^{\lambda_{i}}_{j^{\prime}}(g_{t}q)
,
{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbf{H}^{\lambda_{i}}_{j-1}(g_{t}q)
, and
\left\|{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\right\|=\left\|{\mathbf{v}}\right\|
.
In particular

\left\|g_{t}{\mathbf{v}}\right\|\geq e^{\lambda_{ij}(q;t)}\left\|{\mathbf{v}}\right\|.

(iii)

There exists
\kappa>1
such that for
q\in X_{0}
and any
t>0
:

\frac{1}{\kappa}\cdot t\leq\lambda_{ij}(q;t)\leq\kappa\cdot t.

(iv)

There exists
\kappa>1
such that for
q\in X_{0}
, and
{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbf{H}(q)
, and any
t>0
:

e^{\frac{1}{\kappa}\cdot t}\left\|v\right\|\leq\left\|g_{t}{\mathbf{v}}\right\|\leq e^{\kappa\cdot t}\left\|{\mathbf{v}}\right\|.

(v)

For
q\in X_{0}
and
q^{\prime}\in X_{0}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]
and any
t\leq 0
we have

\lambda_{ij}(q;t)=\lambda_{ij}(q^{\prime};t)

(vi)

For
q\in X_{0}
and
q^{\prime}\in X_{0}\cap\mathfrak{B}_{0}[q]
, and any
v,w\in\mathbf{H}(q)

we have

\left\langle P^{+}(q,q^{\prime}){\mathbf{v}},P^{+}(q,q^{\prime}){\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle_{q^{\prime}}=\left\langle{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle_{q}.

Proof.

The proof of this result is analogous to
[
EM18
, Prop. 4.15, §4.10]
, with the following modifications.

The measurable set
K
and function
T_{0}\colon Q\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
are chosen such that, on
K
we have uniformity in the Oseledets theorem for
\mathbf{H}
with constants
C(K),\varepsilon(K)>0
(see
§A.1.6
), and the measurable connection
P^{+}
satisfies
[
EM18
, Eqn. (4.17)]
.
Then
T_{0}
is chosen such that the conditions from
[
EM18
, Lemma 4.14]
hold.
Then, we apply
Lemma A.5.8
with this
T_{0}
to obtain the measurable partition
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
and follow the same constructions as in
[
EM18
, §4.10]
.
∎

The next result is an elementary consequence of Lusin’s theorem:

B.4.2 Lemma
.

For every
\delta>0
there exists a compact subset
K(\delta)\subset X_{0}
with

\nu(K(\delta))>1-\delta
and a number
C_{1}(\delta)<\infty
such
that for all
x\in K(\delta)
and all
{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbf{H}(x)
,

C_{1}(\delta)^{-1}\leq\frac{\|{\mathbf{v}}\|_{1}}{\|{\mathbf{v}}\|_{2}}\leq C_{1}(\delta),

where
\|\cdot\|_{i}
can be either the Lyapunov norm (see
§A.1.6
), or the natural norm arising on
\mathbf{H}
as an admissible cocycle, or the the dynamical norm defined in

Proposition B.4.1
.

Appendix C
Subresonant algebra and geometry

Outline of section

In
§C.1
we start by recalling some basic facts regarding linear maps between vector spaces equipped with (weighted) filtrations.
We proceed to study polynomial maps between such vector spaces, called
subresonant maps
.
In
§C.2
we consider in more detail decompositions of such maps into
resonant
and
strictly subresonant
maps.

One key construction that we introduce is that of
linearization
, which polynomially embeds one filtered vector space into another, such that the action of polynomial subresonant maps on the source is conjugated to a linear action on the target.
An example that illustrates most of our constructions is in
§C.1.26
.

Ordering of Lyapunov exponents

We recall that our convention for indexing Lyapunov exponents is such that

\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\cdots

with the implicit assumption that
\lambda_{1}>0
.
When discussing negative Lyapunov exponents, we will write them as

-\lambda_{1}<-\lambda_{2}<\cdots

again with the assumption that
\lambda_{1}>0
.

C.1.
Subresonant linear algebra

All our vector spaces are finite dimensional, over
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
.

C.1.1.
Filtered vector spaces

By convention, a filtered vector space
V
will be written as

(C.1.2)

\displaystyle\{0\}\subsetneq V^{\leq-\lambda_{1}}\subsetneq V^{\leq-\lambda_{2}}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq V^{\leq-\lambda_{l}}=V

where the “Lyapunov exponents” satisfy
-\lambda_{1}<-\lambda_{2}<\cdots<-\lambda_{l}
.
This indexing is convenient when assuming that
\lambda_{i}>0
; later we will also label the elements of the filtration by arbitrary
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, but the convention is always that
\lambda<\mu
implies
V^{\leq\lambda}\subseteq V^{\leq\mu}
.
We will also call the
\lambda
that index the filtration the “weights of the filtration”.
The following notation will be useful:

wt(v)\leq\mu\text{ if and only if }v\in V^{\leq\mu}

and more generally

wt(v)=\mu\text{ if and only if }v\in V^{\leq\mu}\setminus V^{<\mu}

where
V^{<\mu}:=\bigcup_{\alpha<\mu}V^{\leq\alpha}
.

C.1.3.
Standard linear algebra operations

Given two filtered vector spaces
(V,V^{\leq\bullet})
and
(W,W^{\leq\bullet})
a linear map

T\colon V\to W

is said to have
weight
\leq\mu
if
T\left(V^{\leq\lambda}\right)\subseteq W^{\leq\lambda+\mu}
.
This induces a filtration on
\Hom(V,W)
.
In particular, giving the scalars the trivial filtration in degree
0
imposes the filtration on the dual vector space

V^{\leq\lambda}=\left\{\xi\in V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}\colon\xi\left(V^{<-\lambda}\right)=0\right\}.

With these conventions, if we have a filtered vector space

\displaystyle 0\subsetneq V^{\leq-\lambda_{1}}\subsetneq

\displaystyle\cdots\subsetneq V^{\leq-\lambda_{l}}=V

then its dual is

\displaystyle 0\subsetneq\left(V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}\right)^{\leq\lambda_{l}}\subsetneq

\displaystyle\cdots\subsetneq\left(V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}\right)^{\leq\lambda_{1}}=V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}

and in particular the dual of
V^{\leq-\lambda_{i}}
is
V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}/\left(V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}\right)^{\leq\lambda_{i+1}}
.

The filtration on
V\otimes W
is induced in the standard way from that on
V
and
W
, therefore the tensor product spaces
V^{\otimes n}
and
\Sym^{n}(V)
acquire natural filtrations.
Note also that for a linear map
T\colon V\to W
and its transpose
T^{t}\colon W^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}\to V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
, the conditions
wt(T)\leq\alpha
and
wt\left(T^{t}\right)\leq\alpha
are equivalent.

Standing assumption:

From now on, vector spaces
V,W
, etc. are assumed to have strictly negative weights, so that their duals
V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
and more generally spaces of polynomial functions have positive weights.

C.1.4.
Filtration on polynomial functions

Suppose that
V
is filtered as in
Eqn. (C.1.2)
.
The ring of polynomial functions on
V
is naturally

\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu})=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\Sym^{i}\left(V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}\right)

and the weights of its filtration are of the form
\sum k_{i}\lambda_{i}
with
k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
-\lambda_{i}
are the weights of
V
.

Given a filtered subspace
S\subset V
, with the filtration on
S
compatible with that on
V
(for instance, but not necessarily,
S=V^{\leq-\lambda_{j}}
for some
j
), we have the restriction of linear functions
V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}\twoheadrightarrow S^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
which is a map of dynamical weight
\leq 0
.
Analogously, the restriction of polynomial functions
\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}})\twoheadrightarrow\Sym^{\bullet}(S^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu})
is also of dynamical weight
\leq 0
.

We also have the useful property of polynomial functions

(C.1.5)

\displaystyle p\in\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}})^{\leq\lambda}\implies p(v+v^{\prime})=p(v)\text{ if }v^{\prime}\in V^{<-\lambda}

which follows from the analogous statement for linear functions (and is in fact a characterization for linear functions).
Indeed
p
is a linear combination of products of linear functions of positive weights adding up to
\lambda
, and the property is true of each such linear function.

C.1.6.
Filtration on polynomial maps

Given two vector spaces
V,W
, a polynomial map
\Poly(V,W)
is an element of
\Sym^{\bullet}\left(V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}\right)\otimes W
.
In particular, the polynomial maps between filtered vector spaces are themselves filtered.
Note that
\Poly(V,W)
includes affine maps (that do not take the origin of
V
to that of
W
), since
\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}})
contains
\Sym^{0}(V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu})
, which is just the scalars.

C.1.7 Proposition

(Characterization of filtration on polynomial maps)
.

The following properties are equivalent for a polynomial map
F\in\Poly(V,W)
:

(i)

We have
F\in\Poly(V,W)^{\leq\alpha}
.

(ii)

For any linear function
\xi\in(W^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}})^{\leq\mu}
we have that its pullback satisfies
F^{*}\xi\in\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu})^{\leq\mu+\alpha}
.

(iii)

For any
d\geq 1
and any homogeneous polynomial function
f\in\Sym^{d}(W^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}})^{\leq\mu}
we have that
F^{*}f\in\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu})^{\leq\mu+d\cdot\alpha}
.

Proof.

It is clear that (iii) implies (ii), and (ii) implies (iii) since any homogeneous polynomial function
f
is a linear combination of products of linear functions whose weights add up to at most the weight of
f
.

Write now a polynomial map as
F=\sum p_{i}\otimes w_{i}
with
p_{i}
polynomial functions on
V
and
w_{i}
a basis of
W
.
We can choose the basis
\{w_{i}\}
compatible with the filtration, i.e.
wt(w_{i})\leq wt(w_{i+1})
.

To check that (i) implies (ii), suppose
wt(F)\leq\alpha
, i.e.
wt(p_{i})+wt(w_{i})\leq\alpha,\forall i
.
The pullback of
\xi\in\left(W^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}\right)^{\leq\mu}
is the polynomial function
\sum p_{i}\cdot\xi(w_{i})
.
Now
\xi(w_{i})\neq 0
implies
wt(w_{i})\geq-\mu
(by definition of the filtration on the dual) and so
wt(p_{i})\leq\alpha+\mu
as desired.

To check that (ii) implies (i), pick a dual to
w_{i}
basis
\xi_{i}\in\mathcal{W}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
, in particular with the property that
wt(\xi_{i})=-wt(w_{i})
.
Applying the assumption in (ii) to each
\xi_{i}
gives that
wt(p_{i})\leq\alpha-wt(w_{i})
which yields the desired conclusion.
∎

C.1.8 Definition

(Subresonant polynomial maps)
.

A polynomial map
F\in\Poly(V,W)
will be called
subresonant
if it is in non-positive weight, i.e.
F\in\Poly(V,W)^{\leq 0}
, and such maps will also be denoted
\Poly^{sr}(V,W)
.

For example, linear maps that preserve the filtrations are subresonant.

C.1.9 Proposition

(Properties of subresonant polynomial maps)
.

Let
U,V,W
be filtered vector spaces and
V\xrightarrow{F}W
a subresonant polynomial map.

(i)

For any subresonant polynomial map
G\in\Poly^{sr}(U,V)
the composition
F\circ G
is also subresonant.

(ii)

For any
x\in V
a reference point, the differential of
F
at
x
, viewed as a linear map
D_{x}F\colon V\to W
, is a linear subresonant map.

(iii)

Suppose
S\subseteq V
is a linear subspace, with the induced filtration.
Then the restricted polynomial map
F|_{S}\colon S\to W
is a subresonant polynomial map.

(iv)

Suppose
S=V^{\leq-\lambda}\subseteq V
is an element of the filtration of
V
, or an affine translate thereof.
Then the image
F(S)
is contained in
\mathcal{W}^{\leq-\lambda}
, or an affine translate thereof.

(v)

Suppose
S=V^{\leq-\lambda}\subseteq V
is an element of the filtration of
V
.
There is an induced subresonant map
[F]
fitting into the diagram

(C.1.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left.\raisebox{1.02228pt}{$V$}\middle/\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{$V^{\leq-\lambda}$}\right.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left.\raisebox{1.02228pt}{$W$}\middle/\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{$\mathcal{W}^{\leq-\lambda}$}\right.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[F]}

Proof.

Part (i) follows from
Proposition C.1.7
and the characterization in terms of pullback of functions.

For part (ii), it suffices to check it for
x=0\in V
since translations on the source or target preserve the set of subresonant maps.
But the pullback of linear functions by
F
does not increase their weight, only adds higher degree functions of lower weight.
So the derivative is a subresonant linear map.

Part (iii) follows because restriction of polynomial functions
\Sym^{\bullet}\left(V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}\right)\to\Sym^{\bullet}\left(S^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}\right)
preserves the filtrations.

To proceed further, write explicitly
F=\sum p_{i}\otimes w_{i}
where
\{w_{i}\}
is a basis of
W
with
wt(w_{i})\leq wt(w_{i+1})
, and
wt(p_{i})+wt(w_{i})\leq 0
.

For part (iv), we can assume again (by translating on the source and target) that
F(0)=0
and
S=V^{\leq-\lambda}
.
But the last condition implies by
Eqn. (C.1.5)
that for for a polynomial function
p
(without constant term) we have
p(S)=0
if
wt(p)<\lambda
.
So the terms
p_{i}\otimes w_{i}
contribute only if
wt(p_{i})\geq\lambda
, and hence
wt(w_{i})\leq-\lambda
.
This implies that
F(S)\subset\mathcal{W}^{\leq-\lambda}
, as required.

For part (v), the map
[F]
is set-theoretically well-defined by part (iv).
It remains to check it is subresonant.
Denote by
[w_{i}]
the image of
w_{i}
in
W/\mathcal{W}^{\leq-\lambda}
.
Then the terms
p_{i}\otimes[w_{i}]
are nonzero only if
wt(w_{i})>-\lambda
, and hence
wt(p_{i})<\lambda
.
Again by
Eqn. (C.1.5)
we know that
p(v+v^{\prime})=p(v)
when
wt(p)<\lambda
and
v^{\prime}\in V^{\leq-\lambda}
.
It follows that each
p_{i}
descends to a polynomial map
[p_{i}]
on
V/V^{\leq-\lambda}
.
Because taking quotients does not affect the weights, it follows that the descended map
[F]=\sum[p_{i}]\otimes[w_{i}]
is also subresonant.
∎

C.1.11.
Veronese embedding for subresonant spaces

Let
P_{V}:=\Sym^{\bullet}\left(V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}\right)^{\leq\lambda_{1}}
be the space of polynomial functions that have weight at most
\lambda_{1}
.
Then entries in any subresonant polynomial map can be only from
P_{V}
(and the span of all such entries equals
P_{V}
).

The action of subresonant maps by pullback preserves
P_{V}
(by
Proposition C.1.7
) and acts on the right, i.e.
(g_{1}g_{2})^{*}p=g_{2}^{*}(g_{1}^{*}p)
(since
(g_{1}g_{2})^{*}p(x)=p(g_{1}g_{2}x)
). We take the action on its dual
P_{V}^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
to then be a left action, as is natural.

We have the tautological embedding given by evaluating at a point.

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447ptev\hskip 8.19447pt}P_{V}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}=:L_{V}

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\quad\mapsto\quad ev(v)(p)=p(v)

If we would have taken all polynomial functions up to some degree, this map is also known as the Veronese embedding.
Note also that we included the constant functions, therefore the image of
V
is contained in a codimension
1
affine subspace.

To ease notation we will use the shorthand
L_{V}:=P_{V}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
.
As we will see below in
Proposition C.1.15
, the purpose of the evaluation map
V\xrightarrow{ev}L_{V}
is to linearize the action of the subresonant maps.

C.1.12.
Subresonant structures on affine spaces

Suppose now that
E
is an affine space, with associated vector space of translations
V
(we will say that
E
is affine over
V
).
Suppose that
V
has a filtration
V^{\leq\bullet}
, and analogously for its dual
V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
.
Then the space of polynomial functions
\Poly(E)
on
E
is itself filtered, with constants in weight
0
.
Note that for an affine space, we now have a
filtration
by degree and not a direct sum decomposition.
The discussion so far generalizes naturally to the case of an affine space
E
and we will denote by
P_{E}:=\Poly(E)^{\leq\lambda_{1}}
and
L_{E}:=P_{E}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
, with evaluation embedding
E\xrightarrow{ev}L_{E}
as before.

C.1.13 Definition

(Subresonant structure on a manifold)
.

A
subresonant structure
on an
n
-dimensional manifold
M
, with weights
-\lambda_{1}<\cdots<-\lambda_{l}
is a collection of charts

\phi_{i}\colon V_{i}\to U_{i}

where
\bigcup U_{i}=M
and
V_{i}
are filtered vector spaces with the same weights, such that the transition maps on overlaps
\phi_{j}^{-1}\circ\phi_{i}
agree with restrictions of subresonant polynomial maps in
\Poly^{sr}(V_{i},V_{j})
.
A
subresonant map
map between manifolds with subresonant structures is a map that is subresonant in respective coordinate charts.

C.1.14.
Linearizing local systems

Given a manifold
M
with a subresonant structure, let
P_{M}
denote the functions on
M
which are pullbacks of the corresponding functions in
P_{V_{i}}
from various charts.
Because subresonant maps respect the weights, the elements of
P_{M}
give well-defined functions on
M
.
On any open set in
M
these form a finite-dimensional vector space, and if the open set is sufficiently small (simply-connected suffices) the dimension of the vector space is one and the same constant.
In general, the elements of
P_{M}
give a locally constant family of vector spaces (see
§C.1.25
and
Example C.1.30
).
Because in all our applications
M
will be simply-connected, in those cases we will speak of a global vector space
P_{M}
of functions on
M
.
Analogously to the earlier discussion, let
L_{M}:=P_{M}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
denote its dual.

To distinguish a vector space of functions such as
P_{U}
for
U\subset M
a simply-connected open chart in
M
, and its dual
L_{U}
, we will denote the corresponding local system by
\mathbb{L}_{M}\to M
, which in local trivializations is identified as
\mathbb{L}_{U}\cong L_{U}\times U
.
Let now
M\xrightarrow{ev_{M}}\mathbb{L}_{M}
denote the tautological evaluation map.
In local charts, or when
M
is simply connected, we identify
\mathbb{L}_{M}
with
L_{M}\times M
, and conflate the evaluation map
ev_{M}
with the induced map
M\to L_{M}
.

C.1.15 Proposition

(Linearization of subresonant maps)
.

Let
V
be a filtered vector space with subresonant structure and
\mathbf{G}^{sr}(V)
be the group of invertible subresonant maps.

(i)

The evaluation map
ev\colon V\to L_{V}
is injective and equivariant for the (left) action of subresonant maps.
It gives a faithful group representation

\rho_{L}\colon\mathbf{G}^{sr}(V)\to\GL\left(L_{V}\right)

The linear span of
ev(V)
inside
L_{V}
equals
L_{V}
.

(ii)

Let
M_{1},M_{2}
be two manifolds with subresonant structures and
M_{1}\xrightarrow{f}M_{2}
a subresonant map.
Then there is an associated map of local systems
\mathbb{L}_{M_{1}}\xrightarrow{L_{f}}\mathbb{L}_{M_{2}}
, covering
f
and linear on the fibers, such that the diagram commutes

(C.1.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{M_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{M_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{M_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{M_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{f}}

Proof.

For part (i), observe that the affine functions are included among all the polynomials in
P_{V}
.
This gives the injectivity of
ev_{V}
, as well as the faithfulness of the representation (since if a polynomial map fixes all affine functions, it must be the identity).
By the definition of subresonant maps, they preserve
P_{V}
and clearly act by linear transformations on it, so the map is a linear representation indeed.

For part (ii), observe that pullback of functions induces a map in local charts
U_{i}
on
M_{i}
as
P_{U_{2}}\xrightarrow{f^{*}}P_{U_{1}}
, so the induced transpose map on dual vector spaces
L_{U_{1}}\xrightarrow{L_{f}}L_{U_{2}}
is well-defined.
Compatibility with the evaluation map is then clear.
∎

C.1.17.
Subresonant vector bundles

Let
V
be a vector space with a subresonant structure and weights
-\lambda_{1}<\cdots<-\lambda_{l}<0
, and let
E_{0}
be another vector space equipped with a filtration

E_{0}=E_{0}^{\leq\mu_{1}}\supsetneq E_{0}^{\leq\mu_{2}}\cdots E_{0}^{\leq\mu_{k}}\supsetneq 0\quad\text{and arbitrary weights }\mu_{1}>\cdots>\mu_{k}.

We will take
E:=E_{0}\times V
as a standard model of vector bundle with subresonant structure (cf.
Definition C.1.18
below).

C.1.18 Definition

(Subresonant maps and structures on bundle)
.

Let
E=E_{0}\times V\to V
and
E^{\prime}=E_{0}^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
be filtered vector bundles as in
§C.1.17
.
A
subresonant map
between them is a polynomial map
F\colon E\to E^{\prime}
, covering a subresonant map
f\colon V\to V^{\prime}
, such that
F
is linear on the fibers, and of total weight required to be nonpositive (see
Remark C.1.19
).

Suppose
M
is a manifold with subresonant structure and
E\to M
is a filtered vector bundle.
A subresonant structure on
E
is a collection of trivializations

c_{i}\colon E_{u_{i}}\times V_{i}\tilde{\longrightarrow}E|_{U_{i}}

where
\cup U_{i}=M
and the maps
V_{i}\tilde{\longrightarrow}U_{i}
give the subresonant structure on
M
, such that the gluing maps
c_{j}^{-1}\circ c_{i}
are subresonant maps of vector bundles.

More generally, given bundles
E_{i}\to M_{i},i=1,2
with subresonant structure, a subresonant map between them is one which is a subresonant map of bundles in each trivializing chart.

C.1.19 Remark

(On weight condition for subresonant maps of vector bundles)
.

To clarify the weight condition in the above
Definition C.1.18
, observe that a polynomial map
F
between trivialized vector bundles splits, using the direct product structure of the bundles, as
F=(F_{E},f)
where
f\in\Poly(V,V^{\prime})^{\leq 0}
and
F_{E}\in\Big[\Poly(V)\otimes\Hom(E_{0},E_{0}^{\prime})\Big]^{\leq 0}
.
See also
§C.1.29
for an example.

C.1.20 Proposition
.

Let
E_{V}=E_{0}\times V\to V
and
E_{V^{\prime}}=E_{0}^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
be trivialized bundles with subresonant structure and weights
\mu_{1}>\cdots>\mu_{k}
on the bundle and
-\lambda_{1}<\cdots<-\lambda_{l}
on the base.
Let
F\colon E_{V}\to E_{V^{\prime}}
be a subresonant bundle map.
Set
D:=\mu_{1}-\mu_{k}
.

(i)

Consider the space
