P_{E_{V}}:=\Poly(V)^{\leq D}\otimes(E^{\prime})^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
of functions on
E_{V}
, which are linear on the fibers and depend polynomially on the base (with
P_{E_{V^{\prime}}}
defined analogously).
Then
F^{*}P_{E_{V^{\prime}}}\subseteq P_{E_{V}}
, i.e. pullback of functions preserves these spaces.

More generally,
F^{*}
preserves the weight filtration on functions on the total spaces of the bundles.

(ii)

The map
F
respects the vector bundle filtrations on
E_{V}
and
E_{V^{\prime}}
and induces a constant map on the associated graded bundles.

Proof.

Let us write the map
F
as
F=(F_{E},f)
using the trivializations, with
f\in\Poly(V,V^{\prime})^{\leq 0}
and
F_{E}\in\Poly(V)\otimes\Hom(E_{0},E_{0}^{\prime})^{\leq 0}
.
Choose now a basis
\xi_{i}
for
E_{0}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
,
e_{j}^{\prime}
for
E_{0}^{\prime}
, and
\xi_{j}^{\prime}
a dual basis for
(E_{0}^{\prime})^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
.
We choose the bases such that
\xi_{l}^{\prime}(e_{j}^{\prime})\neq 0
implies
wt(\xi_{l}^{\prime})+wt(e_{j}^{\prime})\geq 0
(and is equivalent to it).
Let us write explicitly

F_{E}=\sum p_{i,j}\otimes\xi_{i}\otimes e_{j}^{\prime}\text{ with }wt(p_{i,j})+wt(\xi_{i})+wt(e_{j}^{\prime})\leq 0

For a linear functional
\xi_{l}^{\prime}
on
E_{0}^{\prime}
, we can compute

(C.1.21)

\displaystyle F^{*}\xi_{l}^{\prime}=\sum p_{i,j}\otimes\xi_{i}\cdot\xi_{l}^{\prime}(e_{j}^{\prime})

and we see that only terms with
wt(e_{j}^{\prime})\geq-wt(\xi_{l}^{\prime})
contribute, i.e. we must have
wt(p_{i,j})+wt(\xi_{i})\leq wt(\xi_{l}^{\prime})
.
Therefore
wt(F_{E}^{*}\xi_{l}^{\prime})\leq wt(\xi_{l}^{\prime})
, and note also that
wt(p_{i,j})\leq wt(\xi_{l}^{\prime})-wt(\xi_{i})\leq D
.
So at least for linear functions, part (i) is verified.
If we have an element of
P_{E^{\prime}}
of the form
p^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}
with
p^{\prime}
a polynomial function on
V^{\prime}
and
\xi^{\prime}
a linear functional on
E_{0}^{\prime}
, then
F^{*}(p^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime})=f^{*}(p^{\prime})\cdot F_{E}^{*}(\xi^{\prime})
and it is clear again that the weight is respected (since
f^{*}
respects it).

For (ii), observe that the expression for
F^{*}\xi_{l}^{\prime}
from
Eqn. (C.1.21)
implies that if a term
p_{i,j}\otimes\xi_{i}
appears, then
wt(\xi_{i})\leq wt(\xi_{l}^{\prime})
since
wt(p_{i,j})\geq 0
.
Furthermore these last two inequalities are strict unless
p_{i,j}
is a constant.
Therefore the map
F
respects the grading of the bundles pointwise, and induces constant maps on the gradings.
∎

C.1.22.
Linearization of subresonant bundles

We now explain how to produce canonical vector spaces and linearizations associated to vector bundles with subresonant structure, analogously to
Proposition C.1.15
.
Let
M
be a manifold with subresonant structure and
E\to M
a bundle with subresonant structure.
Let
\mathbb{P}_{E}
be the local system of functions on
E
that in local trivializing charts are in
\Poly^{\leq D}\otimes E_{0}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
.
By
Proposition C.1.20
, the space
\mathbb{P}_{E}
is independent of the choice of trivializing charts and in sufficiently small (simply-connected suffices) open sets on
M
, it is a vector space of fixed dimension.
Let then
\mathbb{L}_{E}:=\mathbb{P}_{E}^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
be its dual.

We have as before a tautological evaluation map

(C.1.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}E&\xrightarrow{ev}\mathbb{L}_{E}\\
e&\mapsto ev(e)(p):=p(e)\quad\text{for }p\in\mathbb{P}_{E}.\end{split}

Again for every subresonant map of vector bundles
F\colon E\to E^{\prime}
covering a map
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
there exists a map of local systems
\rho_{L}(F)\colon\mathbb{L}_{E}\to\mathbb{L}_{E^{\prime}}
such that

(C.1.24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{L}(F)}

commutes.

C.1.25.
Local systems and “holonomies”

The linearization constructions associated to a subresonant manifold
M
from
Proposition C.1.15
, and a subresonant vector bundle
E\to M
from
§C.1.22
can be phrased as follows.
The local systems
\mathbb{L}_{M}
and
\mathbb{L}_{E}
on sufficiently small open sets (simply-connected suffices) provide a vector space, and on overlaps the transition maps between the vector spaces are constant.
In most situations of dynamical interest, these considerations are applied to small open sets which are simply connected, so we can speak of a single vector space.

We can alternatively say
\mathbb{L}_{M}
and
\mathbb{L}_{E}
give vector bundles over
M
equipped with a flat “Gauss–Manin” connection.
The parallel transport maps from
x
to
y
along a path in
M
will be denoted
\mathcal{P}_{GM}(x,y)
, with subscripts
M
or
E
when we’ll need to distinguish between
L_{M}
and
L_{E}
.
The maps have the property

\mathcal{P}_{GM}(x,z)=\mathcal{P}_{GM}(y,z)\circ\mathcal{P}_{GM}(x,y)

and when such maps are obtained from dynamical considerations, they are also called “holonomies” in the dynamical literature.

C.1.26.
Example of subresonant maps

Assume that we have
3
Lyapunov exponents
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{3}>0
, with the filtration of
V
given as

0\subsetneq V^{\leq-\lambda_{1}}\subsetneq V^{\leq-\lambda_{2}}\subsetneq V^{\leq-\lambda_{3}}=V

and the filtration of the dual:

0\subsetneq\left(V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}\right)^{\leq\lambda_{3}}\subsetneq\left(V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}\right)^{\leq\lambda_{2}}\subsetneq\left(V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}\right)^{\leq\lambda_{1}}=V^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}.

Let us simplify further and assume that
\dim V=3
and each subquotient has dimension
1
.

Choose a basis
e_{x},e_{y},e_{z}
of
V
and dual basis
x,y,z\in V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
of coordinate functions such that filtration on
V
is
span(e_{x})\subseteq span(e_{x},e_{y})\subseteq span(e_{x},e_{y},e_{z})
and the dual filtration on
V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
is
span(z)\subseteq span(z,y)\subseteq span(z,y,x)
.
Then the general subresonant map from
V
to itself is of the form

(C.1.27)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{bmatrix}\xrightarrow{F}\begin{bmatrix}p\cdot x&+\sum\limits_{i\cdot\lambda_{2}+j\cdot\lambda_{3}\leq\lambda_{1}}a_{ij}y^{i}z^{j}\\
q\cdot y&+\sum\limits_{k\cdot\lambda_{3}\leq\lambda_{2}}b_{k}z^{k}\\
r\cdot z&\end{bmatrix}

Let us make explicit the linearization from
§C.1.11
.
To simplify notation, we omit the constant functions; including them would add an extra coordinate at the bottom.

(C.1.28)

\displaystyle\rho(F)=\begin{bmatrix}p&\cdots a_{i,j}\cdots&a_{1,0}&\cdots&a_{0,k}&\cdots&a_{0,1}\\
&\vdots&&\\
0&q^{i}r^{j}&\cdots&c_{i,j,l,k}&\cdots\\
0&0&\vdots\\
0&0&q&\cdots&b_{k}&\cdots&b_{1}\\
0&0&0&\ddots&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&r^{k}&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&\ddots&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&0&r\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
y\\
z\end{bmatrix}\to\begin{bmatrix}x\\
\vdots\\
y^{i}z^{j}\\
\vdots\\
y\\
\vdots\\
z^{k}\\
\vdots\\
z\end{bmatrix}

where the coefficients
c_{i,j,l}
are computed from
y^{i}z^{j}\mapsto(y+\sum b_{k}z^{k})^{i}z^{j}=\sum c_{i,j,l,k}y^{l}z^{j+k(i-l)}

C.1.29.
Example of subresonant bundle map

For simplicity, we will take the base subresonant manifold to be
2
-dimensional with coordinate functions
(x,y)
and respective weights
-\lambda_{1}<-\lambda_{2}<0
.
Let
E
be a
3
-dimensional vector space with basis
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
and weights
\mu_{1}>\mu_{2}>\mu_{3}
and dual basis
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}
.
A general bundle map (with no translation and identity derivative at the origin on the base) is of the form

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{1}\vphantom{\left(\sum_{i\lambda_{1}+j\lambda_{2}\leq\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}b_{i,j}^{(1,2)}x^{i}y^{j}\right)}\\
\xi_{2}\vphantom{\left(\sum_{i\lambda_{1}+j\lambda_{2}\leq\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}b_{i,j}^{(1,2)}x^{i}y^{j}\right)}\\
\xi_{3}\\
x\vphantom{\sum_{i\lambda_{2}\leq\lambda_{1}}a_{i}y^{i}}\\
y\end{bmatrix}\mapsto\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{1}&+\xi_{2}\cdot\left(\sum_{i\lambda_{1}+j\lambda_{2}\leq\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}b_{i,j}^{(1,2)}x^{i}y^{j}\right)&+\xi_{3}\cdot\left(\sum_{i\lambda_{1}+j\lambda_{2}\leq\mu_{1}-\mu_{3}}b_{i,j}^{(1,3)}x^{i}y^{j}\right)\\
\xi_{2}&+\xi_{3}\cdot\left(\sum_{i\lambda_{1}+j\lambda_{2}\leq\mu_{2}-\mu_{3}}b_{i,j}^{(2,3)}x^{i}y^{j}\right)\\
\xi_{3}\\
x&+\sum_{i\lambda_{2}\leq\lambda_{1}}a_{i}y^{i}\\
y&\end{bmatrix}

The embedding corresponding to
Eqn. (C.1.24)
that linearizes the vector bundle maps takes the point with coordinates
[\xi_{1}\,\,\xi_{2}\,\,\xi_{3}\,\,x\,\,y]^{t}
to

[\xi_{1}\,\,\cdots(x^{i}y^{j}\xi_{2})\cdots\xi_{2}\cdots(x^{k}y^{l}\xi_{3})\cdots\xi_{3}\,\,x\,\,y]^{t}

and makes the corresponding bundle maps upper-triangular and constant.
If desired, we could also linearize the dynamics on the base.

C.1.30 Example

(Affine structure as subresonant structure)
.

We end with the simplest example of a subresonant structure, which should also clarify the “local system” aspect of the discussion in
§C.1.25
.
Let
A
be an affine space over a vector space
V
, i.e. we have a free and transitive action of
V
on
A
.
First, observe that
A
can be canonically realized as an (affine) hyperplane, such that affine maps of
A
act canonically as linear maps.
Indeed, let
P_{A}
denote the vector space of affine functions on
A
, i.e.
\xi\in P_{A}
if for any
a\in A
the function
v\mapsto\xi(a+v)-\xi(a)
(for
v\in V
) is a linear function on
V
.
Then
P_{A}
has dimension
\dim V+1
(since it includes the constants) and if we denote its dual by
L_{A}
, we have a canonical embedding
ev\colon A\hookrightarrow L_{A}
by evaluation of functions.
The image of
A
is the affine hyperplane of functionals that take the constant function
1\in P_{A}
to
1
.

Finally, let
\Lambda\subset V
be a lattice (i.e. discrete cocompact group).
Then we can form the quotient
T:=A/\Lambda
.
Locally on
T
we have the notion of affine function, but globally there are no affine functions.
Therefore
\mathbb{P}_{T}
can be viewed as a vector bundle with flat connection, or a locally constant family of vector spaces.

The next result will turn out useful in constructing interpolation maps:

C.1.31 Lemma

(Polynomial maps from linear maps)
.

Suppose
V
is a vector space, equipped with a filtration with exponents, and associated subresonant structure.
Let
ev\colon V\to L_{V}
be the linearization map from
§C.1.11
.
Suppose that
g\in\GL(L_{V})
is a linear map, preserving
ev(V)
set-theoretically, and respecting the weight filtration on
L_{V}
.

Then there exists a subresonant polynomial map
\mathcal{G}\colon V\to V
such that it’s linearization agrees with
g
.

C.1.32 Corollary

(Polynomial maps between different subresonant manifolds)
.

Suppose that
V_{1},V_{2}
are vector spaces with filtrations, equipped with subresonant structures, and with linearizations
ev_{i}\colon V_{i}\to L_{V_{i}}
.
Suppose also that there exists some subresonant isomorphism
\phi\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
.

Suppose now that
g\colon L_{V_{1}}\to L_{V_{2}}
is a linear map preserving the weight filtrations and taking
ev\left(V_{1}\right)
to
ev\left(v_{2}\right)
.
Then there exists a subresonant map
\mathcal{G}\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
whose linearization is
g
.

Proof.

Let
L\phi\colon L_{V_{1}}\to L_{V_{2}}
be the linearization of the isomorphism
\phi
.
Apply
Lemma C.1.31
to
h:=L\phi^{-1}\circ g\colon L_{V_{1}}\to L_{V_{1}}
to obtain a subresonant map
\mathcal{H}\colon V_{1}\to V_{1}
.
Then set
\mathcal{G}:=\phi\circ\mathcal{H}\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma C.1.31
.

Let
g^{t}\in\GL(L_{V}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}})
be the transpose of the given linear map.
Recall from
§C.1.11
that
L_{V}^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
is a space of polynomial functions on
V
.
Fix a set of (say, linear) coordinate functions
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
on
V
, which are in particular elements of
L_{V}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
.
Then
g^{t}(x_{1}),\ldots,g^{t}(x_{n})
can be taken as the coordinate functions of a polynomial map
\mathcal{G}\colon V\to V
.

Because
g
, and hence
g^{t}
, respects the weight filtration, it follows that the weights of
g^{t}(x_{i})
are at most those of
x_{i}
, hence that
\mathcal{G}
is a subresonant polynomial map, with linearization
L\mathcal{G}\colon L_{V}\to L_{V}
.
We now consider the linear map
g^{-1}\circ L\mathcal{G}
of
L_{V}
, which still preserves
ev(V)
.

Let
x_{I}^{\alpha_{I}}
be the list of monomials that form a basis of
L_{V}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
, with
I,\alpha_{I}
appropriate multi-indexes.
Note that for any subresonant map
\mathcal{G}
, the action of its linearization on
L_{V}
in this basis is given by the pullback
\mathcal{G}^{*}x_{I}^{\alpha_{I}}
for the various monomials.
In particular, the action is compatible whether we view
x_{I}^{\alpha_{I}}
as a basis element of
L_{V}^{{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}}
or as a function on
V
, and the compatibility is obtained by
ev\colon V\hookrightarrow L_{V}
.

It follows that
g^{-1}\circ L\mathcal{G}
acts on
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
as the identity map.
It then follows that it acts as the identity on all monomials
x_{I}^{\alpha_{I}}
and hence that it is the identity map.
∎

C.2.
Resonant maps and groups of (sub)resonant transformations

C.2.1.
Setup

We continue our study of subresonant maps and introduce the notion of resonant maps.
We then study the associated groups of resonant, subresonant, and strictly subresonant maps.

C.2.2.
Splittings and resonant maps

Given a filtered vector space
V
with weights
\{-\lambda_{i}\}
, let
-\Lambda\colon V\to V
be a linear map that preserves the filtration and acts by
-\lambda_{i}
on
V^{\leq-\lambda_{i}}/V^{\leq-\lambda_{i-1}}
.
Then this operator induces a
splitting of the filtration
, i.e. a decomposition
V=\oplus V^{-\lambda_{i}}
given by the eigenspaces of
-\Lambda
.
Conversely, any such splitting determines a unique operator
-\Lambda
.

Note that an initial splitting of the filtered vector space
V
determines a splitting of all corresponding linear algebra constructions
V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
,
\Sym^{d}V
etc., and corresponding operators
\Lambda^{t}
on
V^{\vee\mkern-3.0mu}
,
\Sym^{d}(-\Lambda)
, etc.
We maintain our standing assumption that on vector spaces
V,W
, etc. the weights are strictly negative.

C.2.3 Definition

(Resonant maps)
.

Let
V,W
be filtered vector spaces, with splittings determined by
-\Lambda_{V},-\Lambda_{W}
.
A polynomial map
F\in\Poly(V,W)
is called
resonant
(relative to the fixed splittings) if it is in weight
0
, i.e. if
F\in\Poly^{0}(V,W)
.

Let us observe first that nontrivial translations
cannot
be among the resonant maps, as they must contain terms with strictly negative weights.
The properties of subresonant maps from
Proposition C.1.9
translate immediately to analogous ones for resonant maps.

C.2.4 Proposition

(Resonant and subresonant map characterizations)
.

Let
V,W
be filtered vector spaces with fixed splittings given by
-\Lambda_{V},-\Lambda_{W}
.

(i)

A polynomial map
F\in\Poly(V,W)
is resonant if and only if it commutes with the (exponentiated) splitting operators, i.e.

F\circ e^{\Lambda_{V}}=e^{\Lambda_{W}}\circ F\text{ and similarly for }-\Lambda_{V},-\Lambda_{W}

(ii)

A polynomial map
F\in\Poly(V,W)
is subresonant if and only if the following limit exists and:

\lim_{n\to+\infty}e^{-n\Lambda_{W}}\circ F\circ e^{n\Lambda_{V}}=F^{(0)}

where
F^{(0)}
is the resonant map which is the projection to the
0
th weight of
F
in
\Poly(V,W)
.

The characterizations provided by this proposition show that our notions of resonant and subresonant maps agree with those used in
[
KS17
, Def. 2.2]
, see also
[
Fer04
, §3]
.

Proof.

Using the splittings, we can write any polynomial map
F
as
\sum_{i,j}p_{i,j}^{(\alpha_{i,j})}\otimes w_{i}
where
p_{i,j}^{(\alpha_{i,j})}
are polynomial functions on
V
of weight
\alpha_{i,j}\geq 0
and
w_{i}
are a basis of
W
with weights
{\phi(i)}
where
\phi\colon\{1,\ldots,\dim W\}\to\{-\lambda_{1},\ldots,-\lambda_{l}\}
is an order-preserving map.
Then we have

\displaystyle F\circ e^{\Lambda_{V}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}e^{\alpha_{i,j}}p_{i,j}^{(\alpha_{i,j})}\otimes w_{i}

\displaystyle e^{\Lambda_{W}}\circ F

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}e^{-\phi(i)}p_{i,j}^{(\alpha_{i,j})}\otimes w_{i}

so part (i) follows, i.e. we must have
\alpha_{i,j}+\phi(i)=0,\forall i,j
.

For part (ii) an analogous calculation gives

e^{-n\Lambda_{W}}F\circ e^{n\Lambda_{V}}=\sum_{i,j}e^{n(\alpha_{i,j}+\phi(i))}p_{i,j}^{(\alpha_{i,j})}\otimes w_{i}

so in order to have a limit at all, we must have
\alpha_{i,j}+\phi(i)\leq 0,\forall i,j
, i.e. the map is subresonant, and in the limit as
n\to+\infty
only the terms of weight
0
survive. ∎

C.2.5.
Further decompositions of polynomial maps

The splitting
V=\oplus V^{-\lambda_{i}}
induces corresponding splittings on polynomial maps, and the ones relevant for our discussion are those of nonpositive weight:

\Poly(V,V)^{\leq 0}=\bigoplus_{-\lambda_{i}+\sum a_{j}\lambda_{j}\leq 0}\Poly(V,V)^{\sum a_{j}\lambda_{j},-\lambda_{i}}

We also have the decomposition into weight
0
and strictly negative weight, and a further refinement:

\displaystyle\Poly(V,V)^{\leq 0}

\displaystyle=\Poly(V,V)^{0}\oplus\Poly(V,V)^{<0}

\displaystyle\Poly(V,V)^{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda_{i}}\Poly(V,V)^{\lambda_{i},-\lambda_{i}}\right)\oplus\underbrace{\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda_{i}=\sum_{j>i}a_{j}\lambda_{j}}\Poly(V,V)^{\sum a_{j}\lambda_{j},-\lambda_{i}}\right)}_{\Poly(V,V)^{0}_{nil}}

where the summand
\Poly(V,V)^{\lambda_{i},-\lambda_{i}}
can also be identified with the linear maps
\End\left(V^{-\lambda_{i}}\right)
.
The maps denoted by
\Poly(V,V)^{0}_{nil}
form a nilpotent Lie algebra under composition (and the summation is over tuples of
a_{j}
such that
\lambda_{i}=\sum a_{j}\lambda_{j}
and
j>i
).

Recall that the linearization map
V\xrightarrow{ev}L_{V}
induces an injection compatible with composition of maps:

\Poly(V,V)^{\leq 0}\xrightarrow{L_{\rho}}\End(L_{V})^{\leq 0}

where
\End(L_{V})^{\leq 0}
are the linear maps preserving the weight filtration.

C.2.6 Definition

(Groups of resonant and strictly subresonant maps)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}^{sr}(V)
denote the set of subresonant maps which have invertible derivative at the origin.

Let also
\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(V)
denote the subset of
\mathbf{G}^{sr}(V)
consisting of elements that, under the map to
\End(L_{V})^{\leq 0}
, act as the identity on the associated graded
\gr_{\bullet}L_{V}
.
These will be called
strictly subresonant maps
.

Define also the set of maps
\mathbf{G}^{r}:=\mathbf{G}^{sr}\cap\Poly(V,V)^{0}
consisting of elements in
\mathbf{G}^{sr}
that are also resonant maps.

Note that the sets
\mathbf{G}^{sr}(V)
and
\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(V)
only depend on the weight filtration, and not the decomposition of
V
.
In particular, from
Proposition C.2.7
below it will follow that such groups can be associated to manifolds with subresonant structure, i.e. these objects are independent of subresonant charts that we choose.

C.2.7 Proposition

(Group structure on (sub)resonant maps)
.

(i)

The strictly subresonant maps can be expressed as

\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(V)={\bm{1}}_{V}+\Poly(V,V)^{<0}

and in particular form a unipotent algebraic group.

(ii)

The resonant maps can be expressed as a semidirect product

\mathbf{G}^{r}(V)=\left(\prod\GL\left(V^{-\lambda_{i}}\right)\right)\ltimes\left({\bm{1}}_{V}+\Poly(V,V)^{0}_{nil}\right)

and in particular form an algebraic group.

(iii)

All the subresonant maps can be expressed as a semidirect product

\mathbf{G}^{sr}(V)=\mathbf{G}^{r}(V)\ltimes\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(V)

and in particular form an algebraic group.
The group
\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(V)
is a normal subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{sr}(V)
.

Proof.

For part
(i)
, by the definition of
\mathbf{G}^{ssr}(V)
, these are subresonant maps acting as the identity on the associated graded of
L_{V}
.
So they cannot have a nontrivial component in
\Poly(V,V)^{0}
besides the identity map
{\bm{1}}_{V}
, otherwise they would pull back some linear function to a nontrivial polynomial of the same weight.
But since
\Poly(V,V)^{<0}
form a nilpotent Lie algebra under composition, existence of an inverse and the group structure follow.

For part
(ii)
, it is clear that post-composing with an element of
\prod\GL\left(V^{-\lambda_{i}}\right)
still gives resonant transformations, and in particular such elements are resonant transformations.
Thus, given an arbitrary resonant map
F
, by post-composing with a linear map, we can assume that
F={\bm{1}}_{V}+F_{nil}
where
F_{nil}\in\Poly(V,V)^{0}_{nil}
.
Again we have a nilpotent Lie algebra
\Poly(V,V)^{0}_{nil}
and invertibility follows.
The semidirect product structure also immediately follows by checking that
\prod\GL(V^{-\lambda_{i}})
normalizes
\Poly(V,V)^{0}_{nil}
.

For part
(iii)
, it follows from the definitions that
\mathbf{G}^{ssr}
is the kernel of the map
\mathbf{G}^{sr}\to\prod_{\mu_{j}}\GL(\gr_{\mu_{j}}L_{V})
.
Given any element
F
in
\mathbf{G}^{sr}
(so it has invertible derivative at the origin), we can first compose it with an element of
\prod\GL(V^{-\lambda_{i}})
to make the derivative at the origin strictly subresonant (i.e. unipotent for the filtration
V^{\leq-\lambda_{i}}
).
Then by applying another resonant transformation of the form
{\bm{1}}_{V}+F_{nil}
we can ensure that
F-{\bm{1}}_{V}\in\Poly(V,V)^{<0}
, i.e.
F
is strictly resonant.
It follows that
\mathbf{G}^{r}
surjects onto and is isomorphic to the quotient
\mathbf{G}^{sr}/\mathbf{G}^{ssr}
and the result follows.
∎

Because strictly subresonant maps form a normal subgroup of all subresonant maps, we obtain:

C.2.8 Corollary

(Strictly subresonant maps on subresonant manifolds)
.

Suppose that
M
is a manifold equipped with a subresonant structure in the sense of
Definition C.1.13
, which is furthermore isomorphic as a subresonant manifold to a vector space with a filtration.
We will refer to the set of such isomorphisms as “global charts” on
M
.

Then the set of maps of
M
induced by strictly subresonant ones on global charts are independent of the choice of global charts, and will be called the strictly subresonant maps of
M
.

This will apply, in particular, to the stable and unstable manifolds with the subresonant structure (and global charts) from
Theorem D.1.8
.

C.2.9 Remark

(On resonant maps between manifolds)
.

Given a manifold with subresonant structure, it does not make sense to speak of endowing it with a resonant structure, since the group of resonant transformations of a filtered vector space with splitting does not act transitively on the vector space.
However, given two pointed manifolds
(M_{i},p_{i})
with subresonant structure, as well as charts
U_{i}\to M_{i}
with
0\mapsto p_{i}
and
U_{i}
open sets in a filtered vector space with a splitting, it makes sense to ask if a map
F\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
with
F(p_{1})=p_{2}
is resonant, by considering it in the corresponding charts.

Note that one has to specify not just a splitting of the tangent spaces
T_{p_{i}}M_{i}
, but in addition charts and in particular splittings of the linearization spaces
L_{M_{i}}
.
In the case of interest in dynamics, the resonant normal form coordinates arise by considering the splittings of the linearization spaces of stable/unstable manifolds
L\mathcal{W}^{s/u}
that come from the Oseledets decomposition, since the spaces
L\mathcal{W}^{s/u}
form a cocycle for the dynamics.

Appendix D
Normal forms on manifolds and cocycles

Outline of section

We state the main results in the discrete time setting, the generalization to continuous time being immediate, since one can apply the discrete result to the time-
1
map of a flow and make use of the uniqueness properties of the normal forms coordinates.

The normal forms coordinates on stable manifolds are discussed in
§D.1
, and for cocycles on stable manifolds in
§D.3
.
